Stochastic Extinction An Average Lyapunov Function Approach
Juraj Földes and Declan Stacy

Abstract
. We study the stability of ℳ 0 \mathcal{M}_{0} , an invariant subset of a Markov process ( X t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geq 0} on a metric space ℳ \mathcal{M} . By building the theory of average Lyapunov functions, we formulate general criteria based on the signs of Lyapunov exponents that guarantee extinction ( X t → ℳ 0 X_{t}\to\mathcal{M}_{0} as t → ∞ t\to\infty ). Additionally, we provide applications to a stochastic SIS epidemic model on a network with regime-switching, a stochastic differential equation version of the Lorenz system, a general class of discrete-time ecological models, and stochastic Kolmogorov systems. In many examples we improve existing results by removing unnecessary assumptions or providing sharper criteria for the extinction. Keywords: Stability, general Markov processes, invariant subsets, extinction, Lyapunov function, Lyapunov exponent, Martingale, average Lyapunov function, invariant measures 2020 MSC: 37H20, 37H30, 60H10, 60J05, 60J25, 60J70 60F15, 92D25



Juraj Földes was partly supported under the grant NSF DMS-1816408

1.
Introduction

The stability or instability of an invariant subset of a state space is a central problem in the analysis of stochastic and deterministic dynamical systems.
Specifically, suppose a Markov process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
takes values in a metric space
(\mathcal{M},d)
and there is a closed subset
\mathcal{M}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
, called the extinction set, which is invariant:
X_{t}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
for all
t\geq 0
if
X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
We investigate criteria ensuring that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is stable, meaning (roughly) that
d(X_{0},\mathcal{M}_{0})\ll 1
implies
d(X_{t},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
(extinction).
In addition, we assume that

X_{t}
can only approach
\mathcal{M}_{0}
“asymptotically,” meaning
X_{t}
cannot enter
\mathcal{M}_{0}
in finite time. For the theory of extinction in finite time we refer the reader to
[
MV12
]
and for a detailed discussion on the finite versus asymptotic extinction see
[
Ben23
,
Sch17
]
.
The stability and closely related questions, such as the rate of
convergence of
d(X_{t},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
and the dependence on certain parameters, have been extensively investigated in many contexts.

In this manuscript we consider stochastic processes
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, and therefore the stability and the rate of convergence can in general depend on the realization of the process. Also, we allow
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to have jump discontinuities, and in particular our results also apply to discrete-time Markov chains.
Some processes which can be analyzed using our methods include:

Ecological models
, including stochastic replicator dynamics in evolutionary game theory and more traditional predator-prey type systems (see
[
BS19a
,
HN18
,
HNS22
,
Rud03
,
KS21
,
SBA11
,
Che94
,
CE89
,
CW81
,
Tur77
,
BHS08
,
HS19
,
HNC21
,
BL16
]
), where points in
\mathcal{M}
represent the population densities of some interacting species and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is the subset where certain species are extinct.

Epidemiological models
(see
[
GGH+11
,
HDAD15
,
KABY18
,
ZAE24
,
LLC17
,
BO23
,
SL24
]
), where points in
\mathcal{M}
represent the prevalence of some disease and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(usually a single point) is the subset where the disease is eradicated.

Chemostat models
(see
[
NNY20
,
GJH+20
]
), where points in
\mathcal{M}
represent the concentration levels of bacteria and substrate and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is the subset where the concentration of bacteria is
0
.

Piecewise Deterministic Markov Processes (PDMP)

(see
[
BS19b
,
Str21
,
BH12
]
), where
\mathcal{M}
is the product of some phase space and a finite set of “switches,” which can be thought of as different environments or regimes. For a fixed regime, the process induces a deterministic flow on the phase space with a fixed point
0
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{0}
consists of points which have
0
in the phase space component.

Models for turbulence
of fluid flow (
[
CZH21
,
BKP22a
,
BKP22b
,
FGHRW24
]
), where
\mathcal{M}_{0}
consists of non-turbulent states.

In this paper we introduce a general framework that provides stability criteria, rates of convergence, and dependence on the parameters with wide applicability, in particular, to the settings mentioned above. We leverage the powerful average Lyapunov function technique, substantially extending the ideas from
[
Hof81
,
Hut84
,
Sch00
,
GH03
]
, where average Lyapunov functions were used to analyze deterministic ecological models.

The present work is partly motivated by
[
Ben23
]
, where the question of instability of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, or so called persistence, was investigated. We remark that although our general setting and technical assumptions are quite similar and our results are in some sense dual to those in
[
Ben23
]
, the methods involved in the proofs and applications of our results are quite different, as discussed in detail below.

1.1.
Average Lyapunov Function.

Lyapunov functions are a powerful tool for analyzing the stability of dynamical systems. Lyapunov functions are functions
f
which have certain properties, such as
f\geq 0
is nonnegative or
f(x)\to\infty
as
x\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Also, a bound on the evolution of
f(X_{t})
is assumed, for example
f(X_{t+1})-f(X_{t})\leq 0
, so that
f(X_{t})
is decreasing along positive integer time steps. For continuous-time processes, it is more common to postulate bounds on
\mathcal{L}f
, where
\mathcal{L}f
encodes the average rate of change of
f(X_{t})
. For example, if
X_{t}
solves a deterministic differential equation
\frac{d}{dt}X_{t}=F(X_{t})
, then
\mathcal{L}f=\nabla f\cdot F
since
\frac{d}{dt}f(X_{t})=\nabla f(X_{t})\cdot\frac{d}{dt}X_{t}=(\nabla f\cdot F)(X_{t})
. For random
X_{t}
,
\mathcal{L}f
is something like
\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t})]
. For precise definitions and properties
\mathcal{L}f
see the main body of the manuscript below. After finding a Lyapunov function
f
with suitable properties and bounds on
\mathcal{L}f
, one can derive various conclusions about the long-term behavior of
X_{t}
,
such as convergence to some point or staying inside some set forever.
However, even for deterministic
X_{t}
, Lyapunov functions are often extremely difficult to construct, usually because
\mathcal{L}f
needs to satisfy bounds on the entirety of
\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Unlike a traditional Lyapunov function, an average Lyapunov function
V
does not require bounds on
\mathcal{L}V
everywhere. Instead, one considers the average value of
\mathcal{L}V
with respect to certain measures
\mu
, meaning one only needs to estimate

\mu\mathcal{L}V:=\int\mathcal{L}V(x)d\mu(x)\,,

which we refer to as an (average) Lyapunov exponent. For example, in our paper we consider
\mu\mathcal{L}V
for all invariant measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
Due to their weaker assumptions,
average Lyanpunov functions are a lot easier to construct than traditional Lyapunov functions, but using them to derive properties of
X_{t}
is
significantly harder. Further complications arise in the stochastic setting because
\mathcal{L}V
only gives information about the expectation of the derivative of
V(X_{t})
, and so additional requirements on
V
are needed to show that, with a high probability, the expectation accurately captures the behavior of
X_{t}
. For example, often sufficient additional information is an estimate on the growth of
Var(V(X_{t}))
, quantified by
\Gamma V:=\mathcal{L}V^{2}-2V\mathcal{L}V
. To summarize, traditional Lyapunov functions are difficult to construct and are problem-specific,
whereas average Lyapunov functions are often obvious to construct and instead the challenge lies in computing the Lyapunov exponents (which is problem-specific) and in using their properties to determine the long term behavior of
X_{t}
, which should not be problem-specific. We remark that both approaches are usually more involved in the stochastic setting.

Most of the previous attempts to use an average Lyapunov function
V
to analyze the long-term behavior of
X_{t}
involve constructing a more traditional Lyapunov function which is some sort of combination of
e^{cV}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
and an additional Lyapunov function
W
which prevents
X_{t}\to\infty
in finite time. The combination of
e^{cV}
and
W
is delicate, and moreover it is quite nontrivial to deduce bounds on
\mathcal{L}e^{cV}
or
\mathbb{E}[e^{cV(X_{t+T})}-e^{cV(X_{t})}]
for some
T>0
large enough assuming only some bounds on
\mu\mathcal{L}V
for certain measures
\mu
. As a consequence, these approaches are unsuitable for generalization and require additional unnecessary assumptions.

There are two notable arguments we found in the literature that avoid this sort of construction. The first one in
[
Kha12
]
only applies to linear stochastic differential equations, and therefore it cannot be easily generalized. However, its use of a change of coordinates is crucial, and we often leverage this technique
when applying our theory to examples.
The second is the proof by contradiction method presented in
[
Ben23
]
for solving the problem of persistence (coexistence), which cannot be applied to our problem of extinction. However,
[
Ben23
]
and the present work share the use of several tools such as certain martingales and empirical occupation measures.
Both of these arguments are discussed in greater detail below in the introduction.

1.2.
Our Method

In this paper we use an entirely new approach in order to prove our general results, which, in particular, apply to all of the
examples listed above, see below for more details.
Although the proof of our main result
Theorem

3.2
is somewhat involved, the result itself is a universal tool that can be easily applied.
We show that the long-term behavior of many processes is completely determined by Lyapunov exponents depending only on the behavior on an arbitrarily small neighborhood of the extinction set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. In practice, this allows one to linearize the system about
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and study that instead of the original system. Since our results apply to many different types of processes, each of which has a different notion of linearization, we do not phrase our results in terms of linearization, but
we discuss this connection to linearization later in the introduction.

We formulate sufficient conditions that guarantee
X_{t}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
as
t\to\infty
(extinction) in terms of
an average Lyapunov function
V
defined on some subset of
\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{M}_{0}
and a corresponding Lyapunov exponent
\alpha
which depends only on the behavior of
X_{t}
near
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. The central idea of the proof is to construct a clever sequence of stopping times
\tau_{n}
so that the discrete-time process
V(Y_{n})\coloneqq V(X_{\tau_{n}})
behaves similarly to a
Brownian motion with drift (the mean and variance of
V(Y_{n})
grow linearly in
n
). This allows us to conclude that
X_{t}
stays close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
for all times (stability in probability), and consequently ergodic theory-type arguments imply that
X_{t}
must approach
\mathcal{M}_{0}
as
t\to\infty
(asymptotic stability). If
\mathcal{M}_{0}
satisfies an accessibility criterion,
then a renewal-type argument yields that the extinction occurs with probability
1
, regardless of the initial condition (global asymptotic stability). Additionally, the speed of the convergence is given explicitly as a function of the Lyapunov exponent, for example, if
V(x)=-\log{d(x,\mathcal{M}_{0})}
, then
d(X_{t}^{x},\mathcal{M}_{0})=O(e^{-\alpha t})
.

The main difficulties and novelties of the proof occur in the construction of the stopping times
\tau_{n}
, which can be split into two phases. In the first phase we run the process until
V(X_{t})
or its approximation
\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}V(X_{s})ds
increases enough, and otherwise we stop the process before they decrease too much.
Obtaining the appropriate bounds is quite nontrivial
because our average Lyapunov function
V
is not defined on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, the set on which we have the most information about the long-term behavior of
X_{t}
. However, we assume that
\mathcal{L}V
extends continuously to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, and so for initial conditions
X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
we can use ergodic arguments to control
\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}V(X_{s})ds
, a proxy for
V(X_{t})-V(X_{0})
.
To extend this control to initial conditions
X_{0}
outside of
\mathcal{M}_{0}

we require a strong continuous dependence of
X_{t}
on
X_{0}
, namely continuity of the law of
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
viewed as a random element of the Skorokhod space
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
. It is well-known that such property holds when
X_{t}
is Feller, but,
motivated by applications, we need to extend it to
“
C_{b}
-Feller” processes, which requires new proofs and additional stability assumptions. In the second phase, we let the process (quickly) return to a fixed compact set. The compactness is essential since the success of the first phase relies on having uniform bounds on
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\tau_{1}})-V(X_{0})]
and
\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\tau_{1}})-V(X_{0}))^{2}]
, but for each point in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
we can only obtain bounds which hold
in a small neighborhood. Additionally, we must use clever manipulations of certain martingales to show that the second phase does not diminish the increase of
V(X_{t})
from the first phase.

1.3.
A Brief Summary of Persistence/Coexistence Theory

Before further discussing the details of our manuscript, we summarize
the persistence result proved in
[
Ben23
]
in order to introduce important notation and underlying ideas for our manuscript in a technically easier setting.
Since the assumptions of the persistence result are
almost complementary to ours, together
[
Ben23
]
and our paper completely characterize the long-term behavior of many Markov Processes. However, we again stress that our paper solves a different problem than
[
Ben23
]
does, and thus the methods and proofs are quite different.

In some sense the opposite to extinction,
d(X_{t},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
, is persistence, which heuristically means that
X_{t}
spends an arbitrarily large fraction of time in compact subsets of
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
. To formalize this definition, we introduce
the empirical occupation measures on
\mathcal{M}
as

(1.1)

\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{X_{s}}ds,

which are random measures tracking the time-averaged behavior of
X_{t}
. Then under mild stability assumptions it is known (see
Lemma

4.6
and
Lemma

4.7
) that
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is tight and all limiting measures as
t\to\infty
lie in the set
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
of invariant measures on
\mathcal{M}
, which represent the possible stationary behaviors of
X_{t}
. In other words, the invariant measures completely characterize the possible long-term time-averaged behaviors of
X_{t}
. While extinction forces all limiting measures of
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
as
t\to\infty
to be supported on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, persistence means that they are all supported on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Thus, in the context of empirical occupation measures, the notions of persistence and extinction are in some sense dual.

The crucial assumption that guarantees persistence in
[
Ben23
]
is the existence of an average Lyapunov function

V:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathcal{L}V:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
extends to a continuous function
H
on
\mathcal{M}
and
the “
H
-exponent” or “average Lyapunov exponent”

(1.2)

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H\coloneqq\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\int H(x)d\mu(x)

is strictly negative. In our paper we instead assume that a similar quantity

(1.3)

\alpha\coloneqq\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H

is strictly positive.
Heuristically,
\Lambda<0
(resp.
\alpha>0
) implies an average decrease (resp. increase) of
t\mapsto V(X_{t})
when
X_{t}
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
In the case of
\Lambda<0
, it is further assumed that
V\geq 0
, and so we obtain that
X_{t}
cannot spend much time close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, while for
\alpha>0
we further assume that
V(x)\to\infty
as
x\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
, and so
X_{t}
must approach
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

To provide more justifications, we summarize the argument given in
[
Ben23
]
for the easier case of
\Lambda<0
(persistence). Along with the empirical occupation measures (
1.1
), a key tool in the proof is

(1.4)

M^{V}_{t}\coloneqq V(X_{t})-V(X_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s})ds\,,

a martingale which allows us to relate the behavior of
V(X_{t})
, which is not even defined if
X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, to that of
\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s})ds
, which can be easily analyzed using ergodic theory.

Specifically, for any initial condition
X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
and all large times
t
it holds that

(1.5)

\frac{V(X_{t})}{t}\approx\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s})ds=\mu_{t}H\approx\int H(x)d\mu(x)\,,

where
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is a limit point of
\mu_{t}
.
Hence,
V\geq 0
implies
\int H(x)d\mu(x)\geq 0
. Suppose for contradiction that
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})>0
. Since it is assumed that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
are both invariant sets, we can decompose
\mu
as

\mu=\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\mu_{1}+(1-\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0}))\mu_{2}\,,

where
\mu_{1}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}),\mu_{2}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0})
. If
\mu_{2}
is ergodic and

X_{0}
is distributed as
\mu_{2}
, then

\mu_{2}H=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}H=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t})}{t}=0\,,

where in the last equality we used Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem to conclude that
X_{t}
enters a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
infinitely often (
V
is bounded on such a compact subset). Then by ergodic decomposition
\mu_{2}H=0
for any

\mu_{2}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0})
. However, we assumed
\mu_{1}H\leq\Lambda<0
, and consequently we obtain

0\leq\mu H=\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\mu_{1}H+(1-\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0}))\mu_{2}H\leq\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\Lambda<0\,,

a contradiction.

Rigorously justifying (
1.5
) requires verifying
two technical details. The first is
\frac{V(X_{t})}{t}\approx\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s})ds
, which is
equivalent to the strong law of large numbers for (
1.4
):
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{M^{V}_{t}}{t}=0
, which is assumed to hold in
[
Ben23
]
.
The second is
\mu_{t}H\approx\int H(x)d\mu(x)
, which by the ergodic theorems is valid for
\mu
-a.e. initial condition. However, the proof requires the validity for any initial condition in
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
, which is deduced via the existence of a traditional Lyapunov function
W
with certain properties (
Assumption

3
). In practice,
W
can be almost always constructed. The existence of
W
also yields the correct stability criteria that ensure the tightness and the invariance of limit points of the empirical occupation measures, which are also key to the proof.

The assumptions (besides
\alpha>0
versus
\Lambda<0
) in our work and in
[
Ben23
]
are similar and
let us highlight, mostly technical, differences. For example, instead of assuming that martingales satisfy the strong law of large numbers, in our manuscript we assume the stronger condition given in our
Assumption

5
, which involves another traditional Lyapunov function
U
. Although
Assumption

5
is stronger, in all examples we were able to locate in the literature, the strong law of large numbers is verified via
Assumption

5
, and so for practical purposes there is no difference in the assumptions. In addition, for the special cases of pure jump processes and SDEs (see
Lemma

9.6
and
Lemma

9.3
), we show that
U
can be frequently constructed as a function of
W
, so
Assumption

5
is automatically satisfied. Additionally,
Assumption

5
is satisfied if
\mathcal{M}
is compact (in fact almost all of the technical assumptions are trivial in this case, see
Lemma

3.13
).

Other differences in our assumptions and
[
Ben23
]
are intrinsic to the problem of extinction. For extinction we need to assume that
V(X_{t})\to\infty
implies that
d(X_{t},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
since by our techniques we cannot directly estimate
d(X_{t},\mathcal{M}_{0})
, but we can obtain a lot of information about
V(X_{t})
. On the other hand, compared to
[
Ben23
]
we do not need to assume that
V\geq 0
or that
V
is defined on all of
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}
(we only require
V
to be defined on an open dense subset), which is essential for certain applications.

The similarities of our assumptions and the ones in
[
Ben23
]
have significant practical consequences; for a large class of Markov processes, it suffices to investigate the sign of the
H
-exponents
\Lambda,\alpha
to
determine persistence or extinction.
This is an improvement over the existing literature, where two completely separate arguments were needed, one for persistence and one for extinction.
In the examples below we focus on extinction, but with little additional extra work it is possible to formulate assumptions guaranteeing persistence. Similarly, the examples in
[
Ben23
]
have extinction analogues based on our theorems.

1.4.
Our Results as Linearization

Before applying our techniques to complicated systems, we discuss a more classical example (linear SDEs) which illustrates the connection between the
H
-exponent
\alpha
and the long-term behavior of
X_{t}
. We highlight
[
Kha12
]
’s use of a change of coordinates, a tool which is also applicable to more complicated examples considered in this manuscript. Finally, we remark that in the context of many (nonlinear) SDEs with a fixed point
0
, our
Theorem

3.1
yields that
0
is stable for the nonlinear SDE if
0
is stable for its corresponding linearized system, and a similar principle also applies to SDEs with more complicated extinction sets.

In
[
Kha12
]
the authors discuss the long term behavior of the linear stochastic system

(1.6)

dx_{t}=Ax_{t}dt+\Sigma x_{t}dW_{t}\,,

where
A,\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
are fixed
m\times m
matrices,
x_{t}\in\mathcal{M}:=\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
W_{t}
is a Brownian motion. In this case
\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\{0\}
contains only the origin, and we would like to find conditions that guarantee
x_{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
and the rate of convergence.

Since the system is linear, we expect an exponential convergence (if it happens). Thus we set the average Lyapunov function to be
V(x)=-\log|x|
, where
|x|\coloneqq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
denotes the Euclidean norm, defined for all
x\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Since this function only depends on
|x|
, it is natural to transform the problem to polar coordinates
(v,r)\in S^{m-1}\times[0,\infty)\eqqcolon\mathcal{N}
, where
S^{m-1}\coloneqq\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\mid\|v\|=1\}
is a sphere.

Using Itô formula one can easily show that
\mathcal{L}
is a differential operator and compute
\mathcal{L}V

(see
Section

9
below for details). Then one notices that
\lim_{x\to 0}\mathcal{L}V(x)
depends on the direction that
x
approaches
0
, that is,
\lim_{t\to 0}\mathcal{L}V(tv)
with
v\in S^{m-1}
depends on
v
, and in particular
\mathcal{L}V
cannot be continuously extended to
\mathcal{M}
.
However, in polar coordinates the origin can be interpreted as
\mathcal{N}_{0}\coloneqq S^{m-1}\times\{0\}
, that is,
\mathcal{M}_{0}
becomes a sphere, and then
\mathcal{L}V
indeed extends to a continuous function on
\mathcal{N}
, as detailed in the argument below.
Such approach is common in our examples, where one establishes a “boundary” of the possible trajectories of
X_{t}
by expanding
\mathcal{M}_{0}
to account for all the directions along which
X_{t}
could approach
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. We formalize this change of variables in
Theorem

3.9
below.

To provide more details, we set
x_{t}=r_{t}v_{t}
and then Itô’s formula yields that
(v_{t},r_{t})
satisfies an SDE

\displaystyle dv_{t}

\displaystyle=f(v_{t})dt+\sigma(v_{t})dW_{t}

\displaystyle dr_{t}

\displaystyle=r_{t}g(v_{t})dt+r_{t}\eta(v_{t})dW_{t}

for continuous functions
f,\sigma:S^{m-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
g,\eta:S^{m-1}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
V(v,r)\coloneqq-\log{r}
satisfies

dV_{t}=\Big[-g(v_{t})+\frac{\eta(v_{t})^{2}}{2}\Big]dt-\eta(v_{t})dW_{t}\,.

For

\mathcal{L}V(v,r)\coloneqq-g(v)+\frac{\eta(v)^{2}}{2}
we have that

M^{V}_{t}\coloneqq V(v_{t},r_{t})-V(v_{0},r_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}V(v_{s},r_{s})ds=-\int_{0}^{t}\eta(v_{s})dW_{s}

is a square-integrable martingale whose quadratic variation
\int_{0}^{t}\eta^{2}(v_{s})ds
is bounded by
Ct
for some constant
C>0
. Applying the strong law of large numbers for martingales we have that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{M_{t}^{V}}{t}=0
a.s. Thus, if
P_{inv}(S^{m-1})
denotes the set of invariant measures of
v_{t}
on
S^{m-1}
and

\alpha\coloneqq\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(S^{m-1})}\int\mathcal{L}V(v,0)d\mu(v)

is the Lyapunov exponent,
then Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem implies that for most initial conditions

(1.7)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{-\log r_{t}}{t}\geq\alpha\,.

In particular, if
\alpha>0
then
x_{t}\to 0
exponentially fast with rate
\alpha
. Of course, determining the positivity of
\alpha
may be involved and possible only in special cases. For example in the deterministic case of
\Sigma=0
, we may explicitly solve (
1.6
) to see that
-\alpha
is simply the largest real part of eigenvalues of
A
.

Observe that this argument, in particular the conclusion (
1.7
), crucially depends on the fact that both
\mathcal{L}V
and the right hand side of
dv_{t}
are only functions of
v_{t}
as opposed to
(v_{t},r_{t})
. This does not necessarily hold for the more general system

(1.8)

dx_{t}=\hat{A}(x_{t})dt+\hat{\Sigma}(x_{t})dW_{t}\,,

where
\hat{A},\hat{\Sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are continuously differentiable functions with
\hat{A}(0)=\hat{\Sigma}(0)=0
. A natural question is whether (
1.8
) has the same long term behavior as the
“linearized” system (
1.6
) with

A=\nabla\hat{A}(0)
and
\Sigma=\nabla\hat{\Sigma}(0)
. After the polar change of coordinates as above, we obtain that (
1.6
) and (
1.8
) have the same behavior when the initial distribution is supported on the set
\{r=0\}
, and also that
\mathcal{L}V(v,0)
and
\alpha
are the same for both systems. Since in this paper we show (see, for example,
Theorem

3.1
) that the stability of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is determined by
\alpha
(determined by behavior on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
only), we conclude that if
0
is attractive for the linearized system (
1.6
), meaning
\alpha>0
, then the nonlinear system (
1.8
) is stable as well (compare to
[
Kha12
, Theorem 7.1]
). Additionally, our
Theorem

3.11
immediately implies that
\alpha
depends continuously on
A,\Sigma
, and thus the stability is preserved if the coefficients of the equation are perturbed slightly.

The reasoning presented above is a general principle that extends beyond the case where
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is a singleton. Although it is not formally stated in our manuscript, our main results imply that to determine whether
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is stable (meaning extinction occurs), it suffices to linearize the system about
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and determine whether
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(or a suitable “blown up” version
\mathcal{N}_{0}
after a change of coordinates) is stable for the linearized system.
Indeed, we show that
the stability of
\mathcal{N}_{0}
for the nonlinear and linearized systems occurs when the corresponding
H
-exponent
\alpha
is positive, and since
\alpha
only depends on the behavior of
X_{t}
near the extinction set, both values of
\alpha
are exactly the same for the linearized and nonlinear systems. This principle of linearization is one of the main advantages of our technique; we only need to analyze
X_{t}
and
\mathcal{L}V
near
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, as opposed to on the entirety of
\mathcal{M}
, greatly simplifying our analysis of the important examples discussed in further detail below.

1.5.
Discussion of our Examples

Since we consider general Markov processes including jump processes and SDEs with Markovian switching, our results have a wide range of applications to both discrete-time and continuous-time Markov chains. Due to the multitude of applications and also because our focus is on the development of universal tools, we decided to present
representative examples that motivated the development of our general theory. Notably, we omit a discussion of chemostat models, but we remark that our results can be used to simplify the proof of the general result
[
NNY20
, Theorem 2.2]
in the case of
\lambda<0
.

1.5.1.
SIS Model

The first example is the SIS (susceptible-infected-susceptible) epidemic model on a network with Markovian switching analyzed in
[
BO23
]
.
Each node
i
of the network can be infected at time
t

with probability
x_{i}(t)
based on the interactions given by the network.
The network, the rate of transmission, and the rate of recovery are changing in time according to a Markov process on a finite state space, where each state represents a different environment or regime. In
[
BO23
]
, conditions ensuring that
x(t)\to 0
exponentially fast are established by examining the Lyapunov function
V(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\log{\|x\|^{2}}
. Specifically, using their assumptions and Itô’s formula
[
BO23
]
proves that
\mathcal{L}V
is positive and then the rate of change of the quadratic variation of the martingale

(1.9)

V(x(t))-V(x(0))-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}V(x(s))ds

is bounded and thus (
1.9
) satisfies the strong law of large numbers. Unlike our approach, the authors of
[
BO23
]
needed to bound
\mathcal{L}V
for all possible inputs
x\neq 0
, not just for
x
approaching
0
. Consequently, the assumptions posited in
[
BO23
]
to guarantee extinction include an extra term (which they denote by
K
) that is related to the strength of the noise on the entire state space.

The theory developed in the present paper uses simpler analysis, only requiring us to compute
\mathcal{L}V
as
x
approaches
0
. This allows us to remove the unnecessary
K
term in our improved conditions that guarantee extinction. Using the corresponding persistence theory
[
Ben23
]
, one can show that our extinction conditions are optimal if the network topology remains constant and only the rates of transmission and recovery are switching.

1.5.2.
Lorenz System

In the second example, we consider the Lorenz system, which is
a well-studied simplified (deterministic) model of fluid dynamics and a prototypical model for chaos in three variables
(X,Y,Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The stochastic Lorenz system with additive white noise of strength
\hat{\alpha}
in the
Z
component was analyzed in
[
CZH21
]
. Here we provide a simplified analysis of the stochastic Lorenz system, focusing on the range of parameters for which the

Z
-axis is a global attractor for the deterministic system. In this case, it is shown in
[
CZH21
]
that for small
\hat{\alpha}
, the solutions converge to the
Z
-axis almost surely, while for large
\hat{\alpha}
, persistence occurs.

The proof in
[
CZH21
]
is divided into multiple steps which connect the persistence or extinction to the sign of a Lyapunov-type exponent
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}
(
[
CZH21
, Theorem 4.1]
), and then the sign of
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}
is analyzed (
[
CZH21
, Theorem 5.2]
). To connect the persistence or extinction to the sign of
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}
,
first the known Lyapunov function
V_{1}
for the deterministic system is used to control the process at spatial infinity.
Next, after a cylindrical-like change of coordinates from
(X,Y,Z)
to
(r,\theta,z)
, where
r=-\infty
(a cylinder) corresponds to
X=Y=0
(the
Z
-axis), the authors use the average Lyapunov function
V(r,\theta,z)=-r
to construct a more traditional Lyapunov function
V_{0}:=e^{-\kappa r}(1-\kappa g_{\hat{\alpha}}+V_{2})
, where
V_{2}
is similar to
V_{1}
, the sign of
\kappa
is the same as the sign of
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}
, and
g_{\hat{\alpha}}
(not given explicitly) is the solution to a PDE related to the linearization of the system about the
Z
-axis. Then they show that
V:=V_{0}+V_{1}
satisfies
\mathcal{L}V\leq K-cV
for some constants
K,c>0
and
V
blows-up at infinity. If
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}>0
,
V
also blows up near the
Z
-axis, which provides persistence. If
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}<0
, then
V\approx 0
near the
Z
-axis and satisfies
\mathcal{L}V\lesssim-\Gamma{V}
in a small neighborhood around the
Z
-axis, where
\Gamma{V}
is the average rate of change of the quadratic variation. By a renewal-type argument and a comparison to Brownian motion with drift, the authors obtain extinction, that is, convergence to the
Z
-axis.

On the other hand, our theory immediately implies
[
CZH21
, Theorem 4.1]
using only the standard Lyapunov function
V_{1}
and the average Lyapunov function
V(r,\theta,z)=-r
, bypassing all of the difficult steps outlined in the previous paragraph.
We are also able to greatly simplify the arguments made in
[
CZH21
, Section 5]
, which analyses the behavior of the Lyapunov exponent
\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}
for small and large
\hat{\alpha}
.
Specifically,
Theorem

3.11
below implies that if we perturb the coefficients of an SDE, then the average Lyapunov exponent
\alpha
from (
1.3
) does not change a lot (recall that
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is the set of invariant measures supported on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
). If
\hat{\alpha}=0
, then
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
may have multiple ergodic measures
\mu_{0}
which are each supported on a point or a circle, but they can all be easily characterized and thus
\alpha
can be computed and shown to be positive. However, when
\hat{\alpha}>0
then
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
has exactly one measure
\mu_{\hat{\alpha}}
, which is supported on a cylinder, and both
\mu_{\hat{\alpha}}
and
\mu_{\hat{\alpha}}H=-\lambda_{\hat{\alpha}}

are difficult to analyze or estimate.
Yet, the continuity from
Theorem

3.11
implies that
\mu_{\hat{\alpha}}H>0
for small enough
\hat{\alpha}
, and thus extinction occurs. Since our paper is focused on extinction rather than persistence, we
do not discuss the other half of
[
CZH21
, Theorem 5.2]
(large
\hat{\alpha}
implies persistence), but it would be interesting to see if that analysis can be simplified as well.

1.5.3.
Ecological Models

Historically, the average Lyapunov function technique was primarily developed for deterministic ecological models by
mathematical biologists and ecologists
[
Hof81
,
Hut84
,
Sch00
,
GH03
]
who recognized the importance of the so-called “invasion” rates
r_{i}(\mu)
associated to each species
i
and invariant measure
\mu
. Intuitively,
r_{i}(\mu)
measures how quickly a
small population of the
i
species will grow (invade the environment) when the populations of all the species are distributed according to
\mu
.

Conditions ensuring whether or not a collection of species can coexist in terms of
(r_{i}(\mu))_{i,\mu}
were known mainly for special cases until the recent works of
[
HN18
]
(for the case of SDEs),
[
BS19a
]
(for discrete time systems with a globally attractive compact set),
[
HNC21
]
(for general discrete time systems and also SDEs), and

[
NNY21a
,
NNY21b
]
(for stochastic equations with delay). Since the invasion rates are essentially average Lyapunov exponents, average Lyapunov functions are fundamental to their proofs.

However, instead of working with the average Lyapunov function
V
, the arguments in cited papers typically use tools like the log-laplace transform to instead analyze a more traditional Lyapunov function
\hat{V}
which is constructed using
V
.
The analysis of
\hat{V}
is quite involved since it came from an average Lyapunov function and thus a sufficient time needs to pass before
\hat{V}
starts behaving like a traditional Lyapunov function. In other words, instead of investigating
\mathcal{L}\hat{V}
, one has to analyze
\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}(X(t+T))-\hat{V}(X(t))]
for all
T
in some interval, which is typically harder and often involves rather strong conditions.
The theory developed in this manuscript allows us to easily recover many of these results while also weakening their assumptions.

For example, under our weaker assumptions we show that
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.4]
is a consequence of our
Theorem

3.5
and
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.1]
follows from the corresponding theory developed in
[
Ben23
]
. We are also able to recover
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.5]
.
Since the continuous- and discrete-time ecological models share
the same main ideas, we decided to provide details only for the latter one. This is partly because an application to SDEs is already illustrated via the noisy Lorenz system above, and partly because the calculations involved in the analysis of the discrete-time models cannot be done explicitly and thus require more work. In
Section

10.4
we formulate the proper (weaker) assumptions so that the continuous-time analogues of the results mentioned above (studied in
[
HN18
]
) hold, and briefly discuss how they can be proven using our techniques.

We emphasize that while almost all of the applications discussed above are direct consequences of the theory developed in this manuscript and require very little additional work to analyze, the proof of our improved version of
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.5]
is quite nontrivial and illustrates the power of using our robustness result
Theorem

3.11
in conjunction with the change of variables technique from
Theorem

3.9
. We construct an average Lyapunov function
V
, but
\mathcal{L}V
does not extend continuously to
\mathcal{M}
without some change of variables, the choice of which is not obvious. Since

\mathcal{M}_{0}
has a “corner”, we consider changes of variables
\pi_{p}
for
1>p>0
which approximate
\mathcal{M}_{0}
by a smooth
surface which resembles the unit ball in
L^{p}
. Each
\pi_{p}
maps a simplex-like set
\mathcal{N}_{0}
to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
differently, and thus each
p
induces a different Markov process
Y^{p}
(which can be thought of as
\pi_{p}^{-1}(X)
) on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
, a different set of invariant measures
P_{inv}^{p}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
for
Y^{p}
, and also a different
H_{p}
, which is defined as the continuous extension of
\mathcal{L}V\circ\pi_{p}
to
\mathcal{N}_{0}
. Each of these objects is quite hard to analyze for any individual
p>0
. However, we show that, as
p\to 0
,
H_{p}
converges to a (simpler) function
H_{0}
and also the dynamics governing
Y^{p}
converge in some sense to those of a simple Markov process
Y^{0}
, making
\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mu H_{0}>0
easy to calculate. Using the continuity result
Theorem

3.11
, we conclude that if
p
is small enough, then
\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}^{p}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mu H_{p}>0
, so that
\pi_{p}
gives a change of variables for which
Theorem

3.9
can be applied to, allowing us to conclude our improved version of
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.5]
.

1.6.
Organization of Paper

In
Section

2
we introduce our notation and key assumptions. In
Section

3
we give precise formulations of our main results and techniques.

Section

4
is devoted to proving fundamental estimates on important martingales related to our Lyapunov functions, some ergodic-type facts about empirical occupation measures, and a result concerning the long-term behavior of a class of discrete-time semimartingales. These results are the backbone of the proof of the crucial
Theorem

3.2
. In
Section

5
we generalize some results known for
C_{0}
-Feller processes to the more general class of “
C_{b}
-Feller” processes. Here we also discuss the Skorokhod topology and the continuity of relevant functions on these Skorokhod spaces.
The main theoretical novelties occur in
Section

6
, where we prove
Theorem

3.2
.
Another main
Theorem

3.5
is proved in

Section

7
. In
Section

8
we include technical results relating the extension of the generator
\mathcal{L}
from the domains
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
(
Definition

2.7
,
Definition

8.6
) to the extended domains
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
(
Definition

2.9
,
Definition

2.10
).
In
Section

9
we provide sufficient conditions that verify the bulk of our technical assumptions in special cases: if the Markov process is a switching diffusion or an SDE driven by Brownian motion. Also, we show how our theory can be applied to discrete-time Markov chains.
Finally, the applications of our theory to the SIS model, Lorenz system, and ecological models are given
in
Section

10
.

For the reader primarily interested in applications, we recommend first reading
Section

2
and
Section

3
(skipping the proofs), and then for each example in
Section

10
first reading the corresponding section of
Section

9
(skipping the proofs). For example, one should be familiar with the statements in
Section

9.3
before reading
Section

10.3
.

2.
Notation and Assumptions

Let
(\mathcal{M},d)
be a locally compact Polish (complete and separable) metric space,
\mathcal{M}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
be a closed set, and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
be open and dense in
\mathcal{M}
(where
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
). The set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
can be viewed as an “extinction” set and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
as the set of possible initial conditions. Without loss of generality we assume
d\leq 1
, otherwise we replace
d
by
\min(d,1)
. We endow
\mathcal{M}
with the Borel
\sigma
- algebra. For each
x\in\mathcal{M}
, let
X_{t}^{x}
be a homogeneous Markov process defined on
\mathcal{M}
which has cadlag sample paths. By this we mean that there is a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
(which we assume is complete and right-continuous) and a family of
\mathcal{M}-
valued random variables
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
such that:

•

X_{0}^{x}=x
and
t\mapsto X_{t}^{x}
is cadlag (right continuous with left limits) a.s.

•

X^{x}_{\cdot}
is adapted to
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, meaning
X_{t}^{x}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
measurable for each
t\geq 0
.

•

For all bounded measurable functions
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
, the map

[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{M}\ni(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{t}f(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{x})]

is measurable and for any
s,t\geq 0
we assume (homogeneity)
that

\mathcal{P}_{s}f(X_{t}^{x})=\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t+s}^{x})|\mathcal{F}_{t}]\,.

It is standard to prove that
(\mathcal{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defines
a semigroup: for any
s,t\geq 0
it holds that
\mathcal{P}_{s+t}f=\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{t}f
. Also, the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
makes sense as long as
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable and bounded from below (or above), possibly taking on the value
\infty
(or
-\infty
).

Our setup is summarized in the following definition.

Definition 2.1
.

For a Polish space
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
closed,
\mathcal{M}_{+}\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
open and dense, and
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
a Markov process on
\mathcal{M}
with cadlag sample paths as defined above, we call
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
a Markov quadruple.

For the rest of the section, fix a Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
.

2.1.
Invariant Sets and the Feller Property

Next we list two basic assumptions on our process
X_{t}^{x}
and the sets
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+}
. First, we suppose that if an initial condition is in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}_{+}
) then
X_{t}^{x}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(respectively
\mathcal{M}_{+}
) for all
t\geq 0
. Second, we assume a standard continuity on the law of
X_{t}^{x}
as a function of the initial condition
x
and time
t
. Such a condition is commonly known as the “Feller” property.

Definition 2.2
.

A measurable set
A\subset\mathcal{M}
is invariant if
x\in A
implies that almost surely
X_{t}^{x}\in A
for all
t\geq 0
.

Remark 2.3
.

Some authors use the expression “forward invariant set” instead of our invariant set,
since we do not require that
X_{t}^{x}\in A
for some
t>0
implies
x\in A
. However, there should not be any confusion, since we do not use backward evolution in the present manuscript.

Assumption 1
.

\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
are invariant.

Let
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
denote the space of bounded continuous functions on
\mathcal{M}
endowed with the supremum norm
\|f\|=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}|f(x)|
. The following continuity assumption is vital for our analysis:

Assumption 2
.

Assume that
X_{t}^{x}
is Feller in the sense that for any
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, and also
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
pointwise as
t\downarrow 0
.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that we use “
C_{b}
” Feller continuity as opposed to the usual “
C_{0}
” Feller continuity (just replace
C_{b}
by
C_{0}
in
Assumption

2
, where
C_{0}
is the space of functions vanishing at infinity) because in many examples (particularly ecological ones) the semigroup is not
C_{0}
Feller. See
[
Ben23
, Remark 1 and Example 1]
for more details. We also note that the requirement of right-continuity in time (
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
pointwise as
t\downarrow 0
) is automatically satisfied since our process is assumed to have cadlag paths.

Remark 2.5
.

Under an additional stability assumption which we define below in
Assumption

3
, our definition of Feller is equivalent to the seemingly stronger requirements of
[
Ben23
, Hypothesis 2]
. This is a consequence of the quasi-left continuity showed in
Lemma

8.2
and (a slight modification of the proof of)
Lemma

5.1
, which is similar to the proof of strong continuity for
C_{0}
Feller semigroups.

Definition 2.6
.

If
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
is a Markov quadruple such that
Assumption

1
and
Assumption

2
are satisfied, then
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
is called a Feller quadruple.

2.2.
Two Important Martingales

In this section we introduce important definitions that are used in
Section

2.3
, as well as two martingales that are central to our analysis.

A central object in the study of Feller processes is the generator
\mathcal{L}
of the Markov semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}
defined on its domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\subset C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
as follows:

Definition 2.7
.

Suppose that
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
is a Feller process (see
Assumption

2
) with the Markov semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{s}
(see
•
‣
Section

2
). Then
the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\subset C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
of
the generator
\mathcal{L}
is the set of all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
such that:

(i)

for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f(x)-f(x)}{s}
exists and we set it equal to
\mathcal{L}f(x)
.

(ii)

\mathcal{L}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
.

(iii)

\sup_{s>0}\Big\|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big\|<\infty
.

Heuristically, since
\mathcal{L}f(x)
is the average rate of change of
\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{x})]
at
t=0
, then

(2.1)

M_{t}^{f}(x)\coloneqq f(X_{t}^{x})-f(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})ds

should be a martingale, and this is proven in
Corollary

8.3
below for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
.

The variance of (
2.1
) is related to the Carre du Champ operator
\Gamma
defined on
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})=\{f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\mid f^{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\}
as
\Gamma f=\mathcal{L}f^{2}-2f\mathcal{L}f
. It is a folklore result that
\Gamma
gives the (predictable) quadratic variation of
M_{t}^{f}(x)
as
\langle M^{f}(x)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds
, or equivalently that

(2.2)

(M^{f}_{t}(x))^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds

is a martingale. We make this precise in
Definition

8.4
and
Lemma

8.8
below.

Remark 2.8
.

Informally,
in the context of SDEs,
\mathcal{L}f
(resp.
\Gamma f
) is the
coefficient of the “
dt
” part of “
df
” (resp. “
(df)^{2}
”). Then the formula for
\Gamma
is given by Itô’s formula:
df^{2}=2fdf+(df)^{2}
so that
(df)^{2}=df^{2}-2fdf
.

We wish to apply the operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma
to Lyapunov functions introduced below that are unbounded and possibly defined only on a subset
A
of
\mathcal{M}
. This motivates the definitions of
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
and
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
below, which are general classes of functions for which (
2.1
) and possibly (
2.2
) are (local) martingales for suitable functions
\mathcal{L}f,\Gamma f
. In
Section

8
and
Section

9
we show that
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
and
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
are in some sense the closures of
\{f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\mid f\geq 0\}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, respectively, and we give easily verifiable general conditions for ensuring that functions are in
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
or
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
). We let
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
be the set of all continuous
f:A\to[0,\infty)
such that there exists a continuous function
\mathcal{L}f:A\to\mathbb{R}
such that
M^{f}_{\cdot}(x)

in (
2.1
) is a cadlag local martingale for all
x\in A
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
). We let
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
be set of all continuous
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
such that there exist continuous functions
\mathcal{L}f:A\to\mathbb{R}
and
\Gamma f:A\to[0,\infty)
such that
M^{f}_{\cdot}(x)

in (
2.1
) is a cadlag square integrable martingale and the stochastic process in (
2.2
) is a martingale for all
x\in A
.

Next, we formulate a sufficient condition which ensures that (
2.1
) satisfies the strong law for martingales:

Definition 2.11
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set and
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
. Then we say
f
has linearly bounded quadratic variation if for all
x\in A

it holds that

\sup_{t\geq 1}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}\Gamma f(x)ds\leq C(x)

for some continuous function
C:\overline{A}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\overline{A}
denotes the closure of
A
.

In the following lemma we formulate a law of large numbers for the martingales
(M_{t}^{f}(x))_{t\geq 0}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Assume that
A\subset\mathcal{M}
is an open invariant set and
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
has linearly bounded quadratic variation (see
Definition

2.10
,
Definition

2.11
). Then for all
x\in A
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{M_{t}^{f}(x)}{t}=0
a.s. (recall that
M_{t}^{f}(x)
was defined in (
2.1
)).

Proof.

The proof closely follows the argument in
[
Ben23
, page 76]
and we repeat it here for completeness.

Since
f
has linearly bounded quadratic variation, we have

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}^{f}(x))^{2}]\leq tC(x)
for
t\geq 1
. For any integer
n
and any
\epsilon>0
, Doob’s inequality for right continuous martingales implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{2^{n}\leq t\leq 2^{n+1}}\frac{|M_{t}^{f}(x)|}{t}\geq\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\leq 2^{n+1}}|M_{t}^{f}(x)|\geq 2^{n}\epsilon\Big)\leq\frac{1}{2^{2n}\epsilon^{2}}2^{n+1}C(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2C(x)}{2^{n}\epsilon^{2}}

and the assertion follows from Borel-Cantelli lemma.
∎

2.3.
Lyapunov Functions

In this section we provide the assumptions necessary for our main results. In
Assumption

3
we suppose that there is a Lyapunov function which is large near spatial infinity and we use it to prove tightness of appropriate measures and to estimate return times to compact sets.
Assumption

4
is crucial as it gives the existence of an “average Lyapunov function” that forces
X_{t}^{x}
to (on average) move towards
\mathcal{M}_{0}
if
X_{t}^{x}
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Assumption

5
guarantees that the variances of the mentioned Lyapunov functions evaluated at
X_{t}^{x}
have a controlled growth in time.

Definition 2.13
.

For
f:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
, we call
f
proper if
f
is continuous and has compact sublevel sets, that is for each
m\in[0,\infty)
the set

\{f\leq m\}\coloneqq\{x:f(x)\leq m\}
is compact.

Assumption 3
.

There are proper maps
W,W^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty)
and a constant
K>0
such that:

(i)

W\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Definition

2.10
).

(ii)

\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Since
\mathcal{M}
is a locally compact Polish space, if
A\subset\mathcal{M}
is open, then there are compact sets
K_{n}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that
K_{n}\subset K_{n+1}^{\circ}
(where
K^{\circ}
denotes the interior of
K
) and
\cup_{n}K_{n}=A
. When
A=\mathcal{M}
, the fact that
W
is proper means that we may take
K_{n}=\{W\leq n\}
(the sublevel sets).

Definition 2.15
.

Let
P(\mathcal{M})
denote the set of all Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
.
For
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
,
S\subset\mathcal{M}
measurable and
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
measurable, we use the shorthand

\mu f:=\int_{S}fd\mu\,.

We endow
P(\mathcal{M})
with topology of weak convergence, that is,
\mu_{n}\to\mu
if for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mu_{n}f\to\mu f
.

Definition 2.16
.

For
F\subset\mathcal{M}
a closed invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
), let
P_{inv}(F)\subset P(\mathcal{M})
denote the set of invariant measures on
F
, that is
\mu\in P_{inv}(F)
if
\mu(F)=1
and
\mu\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mu f
for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
.

Remark 2.17
.

By the Tietze extension theorem,
\mu\in P_{inv}(F)
is equivalent to
\mu\in P(F)
and
\mu\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mu f
for all
f\in C_{b}(F)
and
t\geq 0
. Thus, if we view
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in F,t\geq 0}
as a Feller Markov process on the Polish space
F
there is no ambiguity in writing
P_{inv}(F)
.

Definition 2.18
.

We say a function
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
(where
A\subset\mathcal{M}
) vanishes over a function
g:\mathcal{M}\to(0,\infty)
if there is a sequence of compact sets
(V_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that
\cup_{n}V_{n}=\mathcal{M}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in A\setminus V_{n}}\frac{|f(x)|}{g(x)}\leq 0\,.

Note that we have
\leq 0
instead of
=0
only because of the convention
\sup\emptyset=-\infty
. If
A\setminus V_{n}\neq\emptyset
then the limit is equal to
0
.

Assumption 4
.

For
W^{\prime}
as in
Assumption

3
,
there is
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
such that

(i)

For
x_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
V(x_{n})\to\infty
implies
x_{n}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
(meaning
d(x_{n},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
).

(ii)

\mathcal{L}V
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{L}V
extends to a continuous function
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
and there is a constant
\alpha>0
such that
\mu H\geq\alpha
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
.

Remark 2.19
.

In view of
Definition

2.18
, the density of
\mathcal{M}_{+}
implies that
H
, the continuous extension of
\mathcal{L}V
, also vanishes over
W^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.20
.

It is shown below in
Lemma

2.22
that under our assumptions
H
is
\mu
-integrable for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
, so the condition
\mu H\geq\alpha
in
Assumption

4

(iii)
makes sense.

The function
V
from
Assumption

4
could be called an “average Lyapunov function.” Heuristically, under our continuity assumptions, to assess if
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is an “attractor” in some sense, it should be enough to examine the dynamics of
X_{t}^{x}
on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
Assumption

4

(iii)
asserts that on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, the time-averaged value of
H
should be at least
\alpha
, so if
X_{t}^{x}
stays near
\mathcal{M}_{0}
for a long time then
V(X_{t}^{x})
should grow at least as
t\mapsto\alpha t
, and consequently
V(X_{t}^{x})\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
. Then, by
Assumption

4

(i)
,
X_{t}^{x}
approaches
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. To show that
X_{t}^{x}
stays near
\mathcal{M}_{0}
most of the time is not straightforward
and it is one of the major challenges of the present paper.

The next assumption is to ensure that
V
and
W
have linearly bounded quadratic variation (see
Definition

2.11
):

Assumption 5
.

Let
W
and
V
be respectively as in
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

4
. Assume that
there are continuous maps
U,U^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
and a constant
K>0
such that:

(i)

U\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Definition

2.9
).

(ii)

\mathcal{L}U\leq K-U^{\prime}
.

(iii)

\Gamma W\leq KU^{\prime}
.

(iv)

\Gamma V\leq KU^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.21
.

Note that we may without loss of generality assume that the constants
K

in
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5
are the same, since increasing
K
keeps the relevant inequalities valid.

Finally, we list some consequences of our assumptions that will be useful for examples and also needed for the proof of
Theorem

3.11
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Suppose
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
hold true and let

W^{\prime},K
be as in
Assumption

3
. Then for any
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})

we have
\mu W^{\prime}\leq K
, and thus
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is compact.
Consequently, if
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function which
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
, then
H
is
\mu
-integrable for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
. In addition,
\mu\mapsto\mu H
is a continuous
function on
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
and thus
\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H>0
is equivalent to
\mu H>0
for all ergodic

\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
.

Proof.

For the proof of
\mu W^{\prime}\leq K
we refer an interested reader to

[
Ben23
, Theorem 2.2ii]
, and we remark that the argument follows from
Lemma

4.6
,
Corollary

4.8
, Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem, and ergodic decomposition theorem.

The compactness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
is a consequence of the tightness of
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
which follows from Chebyshev inequality since
\mu W^{\prime}\leq K
and
W^{\prime}
is proper (see
Definition

2.13
). By
[
BH22
, Proposition 4.15]
(detailed in
Lemma

4.7
),
\mu\mapsto\mu H
is a continuous function of
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
, and so
\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H>0
is equivalent to
\mu H>0
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
. It suffices to consider only ergodic
\mu
since
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
is convex with extreme points being ergodic measures.
∎

3.
Main Results

In this section we introduce the main theorems that are proved in the remainder of the paper. In particular, we claim that under

Assumption

1
–
5
,
on the Markov process
X_{t}^{x}
, the set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is an “attractor” in the following sense. If an initial condition
y
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, then with high probability
X_{t}^{y}
approaches
\mathcal{M}_{0}
as
t\to\infty
with rate determined by
V
and
\alpha
. For precise statements see
Theorem

3.1
and
Theorem

3.2
. Since
Theorem

3.1
is a direct consequence of
Theorem

3.2
we only prove the latter in
Section

6
after proving preliminary lemmas in
Section

4
and
Section

5
.

The next main result,
Theorem

3.5
, asserts that if
\mathcal{M}_{0}
satisfies some accessbility conditions, then almost surely
X_{t}^{y}
approaches
\mathcal{M}_{0}
as
t\to\infty
for any
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
.
The proof that
Theorem

3.2
is a consequence of
Theorem

3.5
is given in
Section

7
.

In many cases, we need to enlarge
\mathcal{M}_{0}
in order to fully capture the dynamics of
X_{t}^{x}
as
x\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
. For example, if
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is a single point, then it is natural to blow up
\mathcal{M}_{0}
into a sphere
S^{n-1}
and
\mathcal{M}
into
S^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
, essentially transforming the problem to polar coordinates. Such transformation is formalized in
Definition

3.7
. The “blow up” procedure is used if
\mathcal{L}V
from
Assumption

4
cannot be extended continuously to all of
\mathcal{M}
(in particular to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
), but it can be extended continuously to a larger “blown up” space. Then
Theorem

3.9
applies and asserts that the conclusions of
Theorem

3.1
,
Theorem

3.2
, and
Theorem

3.5
still hold.

The most crucial of all of our assumptions is
Assumption

4

(iii)
, which states that
\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H>0
. Then, informally,
Theorem

3.11
claims that
(H,X_{t}^{x})\mapsto\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\mu H
is (lower semi-)continuous.

Finally, in
Lemma

3.13
we state that several assumptions of the main results are immediately satisfied if
\mathcal{M}
is compact.
Let us proceed with the rigorous statements of the main results.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that
Assumption

1
–
5
are valid for the Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
.
If
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
is such that
\lim_{y\to x}V(y)=\infty
, then

\lim_{y\to x,y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)=1.

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose that
Assumption

1
–
5
are valid for the Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
.
Then for every
M,\delta>0
there is
D>0
such that for any
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D\}\cap\{W\leq M\}
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)\geq 1-\delta.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that
Theorem

3.2
implies
Theorem

3.1
by setting
M=W(x)+1
. Also, by
Assumption

4

(i)
,
V(X_{t}^{y})\to\infty
implies
X_{t}^{y}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
as
t\to\infty
, and therefore
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha
could be replaced with
X_{t}^{y}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
in
Theorem

3.1
and
Theorem

3.2
. Alternatively, using results in
Section

4.2
one could replace
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha
by the statement that all limit points of the empirical occupation measures
\mu_{t}^{y}
(defined in
Definition

4.4
) as
t\to\infty
lie in
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
.

For our next result, we need to review the definition of accessibility. The equivalence of the listed conditions in

Definition

3.4

is proved in
Lemma

7.1
.

Definition 3.4
.

For
x\in\mathcal{M}
and open
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
we say
\mathcal{U}
is accessible from
x
if any of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})dt>0
.

(2)

There exists
t\geq 0
such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

(3)

\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq 0\text{ such that }X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose that
Assumption

1
–
5
are valid for the Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
.
Assume additionally that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{0}
and that every point
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
satisfies the following accessibility condition:

(3.1)

\exists M>0\text{ such that }\forall D>0,\{V>D\}\cap\{W<M\}\text{ is accessible from }x\,.

Then

\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)=1

for all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
.

Remark 3.6
.

A sufficient condition for (
3.1
) to hold for
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
is that there is
y\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
such that
\lim_{z\to y,z\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}V(z)=\infty
and
y
is accessible from
x
in the sense that for all open sets
\mathcal{U}
containing
y
,
\mathcal{U}
is accessible from
x
. Indeed, set
M=W(y)+1
and for any
D>0
choose
\epsilon>0
small enough that
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\{d(y,\cdot)<\epsilon\}
, the ball of radius
\epsilon
around
y
, satisfies
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}\subset\{V>D\}\cap\{W<M\}
. We have by assumption that
\mathcal{U}
is accessible from
x
. Since
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is invariant by
Assumption

1
, we have
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})\leq\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\{V>D\}\cap\{W<M\})
for any
t>0
, and thus
\{V>D\}\cap\{W<M\}
is also accessible from
x
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{N},t\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})

be Markov quadruples (see
Definition

2.1
). Then

\pi:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{M}
is called a quadruple map if
\pi
is a continuous surjection such that:

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{0}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
and
\mathcal{N}_{+}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
.

(2)

\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{K})
is compact for all
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
compact.

(3)

For
y_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
,
\pi(y_{n})\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
implies
y_{n}\to\mathcal{N}_{0}
.

(4)

For all
y\in\mathcal{N}
,
\pi(Y_{\cdot}^{y})=X_{\cdot}^{\pi(y)}
almost surely.

Remark 3.8
.

It follows from
Definition

3.7
that if
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{N},t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple (
Definition

2.6
) then so is
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
, but we do not use this fact. In this case, if
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N})
(respectively
P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
) then it standard to prove from
Definition

3.7
that the pushforward measure
\pi^{*}\mu
(given by
\pi^{*}\mu f=\mu f\circ\pi
for
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
) is in
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
(respectively in
P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
).

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{N},t\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
be Feller quadruples (see
Definition

2.6
) and
\pi:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{M}
is a quadruple map.
Then
Theorem

3.2
(and thus its corollaries
Theorem

3.1
and
Theorem

3.5
) remain valid with
Assumption

4

(iii)
replaced with the assumption that:

(3.2)

\mathcal{L}V\circ\pi
extends to a continuous function
H:\mathcal{N}\to\mathbb{R}
and there is a constant
\alpha>0
such that
\mu H\geq\alpha
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
.

Remark 3.10
.

The assumptions of
Theorem

3.9
are somewhat redundant, since by
Definition

2.6
,
Assumption

1
–
2
are automatically satisfied.

Proof.

As an consequence of
Definition

2.9
,
Definition

2.10
, and
Definition

3.7
we immediately obtain that for any
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
it holds that

(1)

If
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
, then
f\circ\pi\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{N})
and we may take
\mathcal{L}(f\circ\pi)=\mathcal{L}f\circ\pi
.

(2)

If
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, then
f\circ\pi\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{N})
and we may take
\mathcal{L}(f\circ\pi)=\mathcal{L}f\circ\pi
,
\Gamma(f\circ\pi)=\Gamma f\circ\pi
.

Also, (1) and (2) hold with
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
replaced with
\mathcal{M}_{+},\mathcal{N}_{+}
.

Suppose
Assumption

1
–
5
are valid for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
, where we replace
Assumption

4

(iii)
with (
3.2
). Then it follows easily from
Definition

2.6
and
Definition

3.7
that

Assumption

1
–
5
are satisfied for
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{N},t\geq 0})
with
W,W^{\prime},U,U^{\prime}
from
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5
being replaced respectively by
W\circ\pi,W^{\prime}\circ\pi,U\circ\pi,U^{\prime}\circ\pi,V\circ\pi
.

Thus, by
Theorem

3.2
for every
M>0,\delta>0
there is a
D>0
such that for any
y\in\mathcal{N}_{+}\cap\{V\circ\pi\geq D\}\cap\{W\circ\pi\leq M\}
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(\pi(Y_{t}^{y}))}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)\geq 1-\delta.

If
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D\}\cap\{W\leq M\}
then by surjectivity of
\pi
and
\mathcal{N}_{+}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
there is some
y\in\mathcal{N}_{+}\cap\{V\circ\pi\geq D\}\cap\{W\circ\pi\leq M\}
such that
\pi(y)=x
and so the claim follows by noting that
\pi(Y_{\cdot}^{y})=X_{\cdot}^{x}
.
∎

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\Theta
be a compact metric space and let

\{\{X_{\theta,t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of Markov processes. For each
\theta\in\Theta
denote
P_{inv}^{\theta}(\mathcal{M})
the set of invariant probability measures (see
Definition

2.16
) for
X_{\theta,t}^{x}
. Define the Markov process
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}
on
\Theta\times\mathcal{M}
by
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}=(\theta,X_{\theta,t}^{x})
.

Suppose that
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}
satisfies
Assumption

2
and that each
\{X_{\theta,t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
satisfies
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
with the same
W,W^{\prime},K,U,U^{\prime}
(independent of
\theta
).
Then if
\theta_{n},\theta_{\infty}\in\Theta
and
\mu_{n}\in P^{\theta_{n}}_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
are such that
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{\infty}
, then there is a subsequence of
\mu_{n}
converging to some
\mu\in P_{inv}^{\theta_{\infty}}(\mathcal{M})
.

Thus, if
H:\Theta\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function which vanishes over
(\theta,x)\mapsto W^{\prime}(x)
(see
Definition

2.18
), then for all
\theta_{\infty}\in\Theta
we have

(3.3)

\liminf_{\theta\to\theta_{\infty}}\inf_{\mu\in P^{\theta}_{inv}(\mathcal{M})}\mu H_{\theta}\geq\inf_{\mu\in P^{\theta_{\infty}}_{inv}(\mathcal{M})}\mu H_{\theta_{\infty}}\,,

where
H_{\theta}(x)\coloneqq H(\theta,x)
. In other words, the function
\theta\mapsto\inf_{\mu\in P^{\theta}_{inv}(\mathcal{M})}\mu H_{\theta}
is lower semicontinuous.

Remark 3.12
.

The condition that
\{Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}\}_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{M}}
satisfies
Assumption

2
is equivalent to the following convergence of the semigroups
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta}
of
\{X_{\theta,t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
: if
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Theta\ni\theta_{n}\to\theta\in\Theta
, then
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta_{n}}f\to\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta_{\infty}}f
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

We use
\tilde{W}
,
\tilde{W^{\prime}}
,
\tilde{U}
,
\tilde{U^{\prime}}
to denote the maps defined by precomposing
W
,
W^{\prime}
,
U
,
U^{\prime}
with
(\theta,x)\mapsto x
so that by the definition
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}=(\theta,X_{\theta,t}^{x})
it follows that
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
are satisfied for
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}
with
\tilde{W}
,
\tilde{W^{\prime}}
,
\tilde{U}
,
\tilde{U^{\prime}}
in place of
W
,
W^{\prime}
,
U
,
U^{\prime}
.
Let
\theta_{n},\theta_{\infty}\in\Theta
and
\mu_{n}\in P^{\theta_{n}}_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
be such that
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{\infty}
. Let
P_{inv}(\Theta\times\mathcal{M})
denote the set of invariant measures for
\{Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}\}_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{M}}
. It readily follows from
Definition

2.16
and
Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}=(\theta,X_{\theta,t}^{x})
that
\nu_{n}\coloneqq\delta_{\theta_{n}}\otimes\mu_{n}\in P_{inv}(\Theta\times\mathcal{M})
, where
\delta_{\theta_{n}}
is the dirac delta measure at
\theta_{n}
and
\otimes
denotes the usual product of measures.
By
Lemma

2.22
we have
that there is a subsequence of
\nu_{n}
converging to some
\nu\in P(\Theta\times\mathcal{M})
. Without loss of generality we assume
\nu=\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}
(otherwise pass to a sub-sequence). By Portmanteau theorem,
\nu=\delta_{\theta_{\infty}}\otimes\mu
for some
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
such that
\mu=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}
. We conclude the proof by showing that
\mu\in P_{inv}^{\theta_{\infty}}(\mathcal{M})
, or equivalently that
\nu\in P_{inv}(\Theta\times\mathcal{M})
. Indeed, if
f\in C_{b}(\Theta\times\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
then

\nu f=\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}f=\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n}\mathcal{P}^{Y}_{t}f=\nu\mathcal{P}^{Y}_{t}f\,,

where
\mathcal{P}^{Y}_{t}
denotes the semigroup of
\{Y_{t}^{(\theta,x)}\}_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{M}}
.

The claim (
3.3
) is a consequence of the first claim and
Lemma

2.22
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

For compact
\mathcal{M}
,
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

5
, and
Assumption

4

(ii)
are satisfied if
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\Gamma V(x)<\infty
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{M}
is compact then constant functions
g(x)=c>0
are proper (as defined in
Definition

2.13
) and all functions vanish over
g
(take
V_{n}=\mathcal{M}
in
Definition

2.18
). Thus, we may simply take
W,U,W^{\prime},U^{\prime}\equiv 1
and
K=1+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\Gamma V(x)
.
∎

4.
General Facts

This section contains statements and proofs of some technical results related to the martingales
M_{t}^{W},M_{t}^{V},M_{t}^{U}
(see (
2.1
)), the empirical occupation measures (see
Definition

4.4
), and discrete semimartingales. Recall that functions

W,V
, and
U
were defined in
Assumption

3
–
5
. In
Section

4.1
we prove estimates on stopping times that are frequently used in
Section

5
and
Section

6
. In
Section

4.2
we show that almost surely the empirical occupation measures are tight and that all of their limit points (with time approaching to infinity) are invariant measures. We also obtain a sufficient conditions that imply
Theorem

3.2
and these conditions are verified below in
Theorem

6.1
.
Finally, in
Section

4.3
we recall and prove some basic facts about discrete-time (semi-)martingales which serve as the motivation for the proof of
Theorem

6.1
. In the entire section we fix a Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
satisfying
Assumption

1
–
5
.

4.1.
Optional Stopping Inequalities

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
W,W^{\prime}
and
U,U^{\prime}
be as in
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5
respectively.
For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
and a stopping time
\tau
such that
\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{\tau}^{x})+\int_{0}^{\tau}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq W(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau].

In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{t}W+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}W^{\prime}ds\leq W+Kt
. The same inequalities hold with
W,W^{\prime}
replaced by
U,U^{\prime}
respectively.

Proof.

Let
M_{t}\coloneqq M_{t}^{W}(x)
be the local martingale defined in (
2.1
). Let
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
being a localizing sequence, meaning
\tau_{n}
is an increasing sequence of bounded stopping times such that
\tau_{n}\uparrow\infty
almost surely and
M_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}
is a martingale for all
n
. Then, by the
optional stopping theorem and the definition (
2.1
), for each
n\geq 1
we have

0=\mathbb{E}[M_{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}^{x})-W(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big].

By
Assumption

3
we have
W\geq 1
a d
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}\leq K
, and therefore

W(X_{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}^{x})-W(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\geq-W(x)-K(\tau_{n}\wedge\tau)\geq-W(x)-K\tau\,.

Since
\mathbb{E}[W(x)+K\tau]<\infty
, Fatou’s lemma and

X^{x}_{\tau_{n}\wedge\tau}\to X^{x}_{\tau}
almost surely as
n\to\infty
give

0\geq\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{\tau}^{x})-W(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\,.

Using
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
again, we have

0\geq\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{\tau}^{x})-W(x)-K\tau+\int_{0}^{\tau}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\,.

Adding
W(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty
to both sides proves the first claim.

Setting
\tau=t
gives

\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{t}^{x})+\int_{0}^{t}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq W(x)+Kt.

By definition of
\mathcal{P}_{t}
we have

\mathbb{E}[W(X_{t}^{x})]=\mathcal{P}_{t}W(x)
, and consequently

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})]ds=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}W^{\prime}(x)ds

by Tonelli’s theorem and the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{s}
, proving the second claim. The proof for
U,U^{\prime}
is analogous with
Assumption

3
replaced by
Assumption

5
.
∎

Corollary 4.2
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
and any stopping time
\tau
with
\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty
we have

(4.1)

\mathbb{E}[M^{W}_{\tau}(x)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau])\,,

where the martingale
M_{t}^{f}(x)
was defined in (
2.1
). Similarly, for any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and any stopping time
\tau
with
\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty
we have

\mathbb{E}[M^{V}_{\tau}(x)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma V(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau]).

Specifically,
V
and
W
have linearly bounded quadratic variation (see
Definition

2.11
).

Proof.

We only prove the claim for
W
as the proof is analogous for
V
.

Since
W\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Assumption

3
),
M_{t}^{W}(x)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds
is a martingale by
Definition

2.10
. By the Optional stopping theorem

\mathbb{E}\Big[(M_{t\wedge\tau}^{W}(x))^{2}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]=0.

By
Assumption

5
,
\Gamma W\leq KU^{\prime}
and so
Lemma

4.1
gives

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(M_{t\wedge\tau}^{W}(x))^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]

\displaystyle\leq K\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}U^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[t\wedge\tau]).

Thus,

\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[(M_{t\wedge\tau}^{W}(x))^{2}\Big]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau])<\infty

and by the Martingale convergence theorem,
M_{t\wedge\tau}^{W}(x)\to M_{\tau}^{W}(x)
in
L^{2}
. Passing
t\to\infty
in (
4.2
) implies that

\mathbb{E}[M^{W}_{\tau}(x)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau])

and (
4.1
) follows.
To show that
W
has linearly bounded quadratic variation (see
Definition

2.11
), we note that for
x\in\mathcal{M}
, by (
4.2
) with
\tau=t
and Tonelli’s theorem

\sup_{t\geq 1}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}\Gamma W(x)ds\leq\sup_{t\geq 1}\frac{K(U(x)+Kt)}{t}\leq KU(x)+K^{2}\,,

as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

If
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\tau
is a stopping time such that
\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty
, then

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}|H(X_{s}^{x})|ds\Big]<\infty\qquad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\tau}^{x})-V(x)]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big].

Proof.

Recall from
Assumption

4
that
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and
\mathcal{L}V
is the restriction of
H
to
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Hence, by
Definition

2.10
and
Corollary

4.2
, for each
t\geq 0
we have that

M_{t}\coloneqq M_{\tau\wedge t}^{V}(x)=V(X_{\tau\wedge t}^{x})-V(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge t}H(X_{s}^{x})ds

is a square-integrable martingale with

\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathbb{E}[M_{t}^{2}]\leq K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\tau]).

By Martingale convergence theorem,
M_{t}\to V(X_{\tau}^{x})-V(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau}H(X_{s}^{x})ds
in
L^{2}
as
t\to\infty
and thus

(4.3)

0=\mathbb{E}\Big[V(X_{\tau}^{x})-V(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\,.

Since
H
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
(see
Remark

2.19
,
Definition

2.18
), there are constants
A>0,b>0
such that
|H|\leq A+bW^{\prime}
. Consequently,
Lemma

4.1
gives

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}|H(X_{s}^{x})|ds\Big]

\displaystyle\leq A\mathbb{E}[\tau]+b\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]

\displaystyle\leq bW(x)+(A+bK)\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty,

which proves the claim after adding
\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{\tau}H(X_{s}^{x})ds]
to both sides of (
4.3
).
∎

4.2.
Empirical Occupation Measures

The empirical occupation measures as defined below in
Definition

4.4
capture the behavior of
X_{t}^{x}
averaged over time, and are central to the proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

Definition 4.4
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
t>0
let
\mu_{t}^{x}
denote the empirical occupation measure

(4.5)

\mu_{t}^{x}(\omega)\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{X_{s}^{x}(\omega)}ds,

where
\delta_{y}
is the Dirac measure concentrated at
y
, that is,
\delta_{y}f=f(y)
. Note that
\omega\in\Omega
, and therefore
\mu_{t}^{x}
is a random measure which depends on
\omega
, but often this dependence is suppressed.

Remark 4.5
.

Since the paths
t\mapsto X_{t}^{x}
are cadlag a.s., almost surely all continuous functions on
\mathcal{M}
(in particular
W^{\prime}
and
H
) are
\mu_{t}^{x}
-integrable for all
t>0
.

For what follows, recall
Definition

2.15
and
Definition

2.16
.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
x\in\mathcal{M}
(respectively
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
), then almost surely the following holds. For every sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
such that
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\omega)
converges to some
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
, then
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
(respectively
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
).

Proof.

The claim is proven for
\mathcal{M}
in
[
Ben23
, Theorem 2.2ii]
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, additionally note that Portmanteau theorem and
Assumption

1
imply that if
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu
, then
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\mathcal{M}_{0})=1
a.s.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

[
BH22
, Proposition 4.15]

If
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is proper and
\mu_{n}\in P(\mathcal{M})
is such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}f<\infty
, then
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight. If furthermore
g:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
