is a continuous function which vanishes over
f
, then
\mu_{n}\to\mu
implies
\mu_{n}g\to\mu g
.

Corollary 4.8
.

For
\mu_{t}^{x}
as in
Definition

4.4
we have

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(W^{\prime})\leq K
a.s. Thus, almost surely
(\mu_{t_{n}}^{x})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight for all
t_{n}\to\infty
.

Proof.

A similar proof can be found in
[
Ben23
, Theorem 2.2ii]
but we include the details here for completeness.

Since
W
has linearly bounded quadratic variation (
Corollary

4.2
),
M_{t}^{W}(x)
satisfies the strong law for martingales (
Lemma

2.12
), and thus
\frac{-W(X_{t}^{x})}{t}+\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{L}W)\to 0
a.s. as
t\to\infty
. Moreover, from
W\geq 0
and
\mathcal{L}W\leq K-W^{\prime}
(see
Assumption

3
) it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{W(X_{t}^{x})}{t}=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(\mathcal{L}W)\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}K-\mu_{t}^{x}W^{\prime}\,,

and therefore

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}W^{\prime}\leq K\,.

Hence, since
W^{\prime}
is proper, by
Lemma

4.7
almost surely
(\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight for all
t_{n}\to\infty
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

For all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mu_{t}^{x}
defined in (
4.5
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s the following are equivalent:

(i)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}H\geq\alpha
.

(ii)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha
or
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

(iii)

If
t_{n}\to\infty
with
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu
for some
\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
, then
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})=1
.

Proof.

To show
(i)
implies
(ii)
we assume
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and show that a.s.

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}H\geq\alpha\quad\textrm{ implies }\quad\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha\,.

By the definition of
M^{V}_{t}
in (
2.1
) and
\mathcal{L}V
being the restriction of
H
to
\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we have

\frac{M^{V}_{t}(x)}{t}=\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}-\frac{V(x)}{t}-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{x})ds=\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}-\frac{V(x)}{t}-\mu_{t}^{x}H\,.

By
Corollary

4.2
,
V
has linearly bounded quadratic variation, so by
Lemma

2.12

\frac{M^{V}_{t}(x)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
, proving the claim.

To show
(ii)
implies
(iii)
, we first note that if
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha
a.s., then by
Assumption

4

(i)
it follows that
d(X_{t}^{x},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to 0
a.s. as
t\to\infty
. Also, if
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, then
d(X_{t}^{x},\mathcal{M}_{0})=0
for each
t\geq 0
by
Assumption

1
(see
Definition

2.2
). In either case, the Portmanteau Theorem implies

\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\mu(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})\leq\epsilon\})\geq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})\leq\epsilon\})=1\,.

To show
(iii)
implies
(i)
, first note that by
Corollary

4.8
, almost surely for all
t_{n}\to\infty
, the sequence
(\mu_{t_{n}}^{x})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight and thus has a further subsequence
\mu_{t_{n_{k}}}^{x}
which converges to some
\mu
. Applying
Lemma

4.6
and
(iii)
,
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
, so
Assumption

4

(iii)
implies
\mu H\geq\alpha
. Since
H
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
(
Remark

2.19
) and
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(W^{\prime})\leq K
(
Corollary

4.8
), then
\mu_{t_{n_{k}}}^{x}H\to\mu H\geq\alpha
by
Lemma

4.7
. Thus,
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}H\geq\alpha
.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

For
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we have a.s.

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}=0\quad\textrm{implies}\quad\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha.

Proof.

We claim that a.s.

(4.6)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}=0

implies

(4.7)

\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\})=0\,.

Indeed, suppose (
4.6
) holds and fix any
\epsilon>0
. By Chebyshev inequality and

Corollary

4.8
, there is a
K_{\epsilon}=\frac{2K}{\epsilon}
such that a.s.

(4.8)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{W^{\prime}>K_{\epsilon}\})<\epsilon\,.

Since
W^{\prime}
is proper (
Assumption

3
), the set
\{W^{\prime}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}
is compact, so by continuity of
W
there is
M>0
such that
\{W\geq M\}\subset\{W^{\prime}>K_{\epsilon}\}
. Combining this with (
4.8
) gives

(4.9)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{W\geq M\})\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{W^{\prime}>K_{\epsilon}\})<\epsilon\,.

Using (
4.6
), by making
M
large enough we may also assume

\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}<\epsilon\,.

In particular, there is a (random)
T>0
such that
d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}<\epsilon
for all
t\geq T
. Thus,

(4.10)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{W<M\}\cap\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\})=0\,.

Since

\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\}\subset\{W\geq M\}\cup(\{W<M\}\cap\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\}),

then (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) show that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{y}(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\})<\epsilon

and since
\epsilon>0
is arbitrary, we have proven the claim that almost surely (
4.6
) implies (
4.7
).

To finish the proof, we assume (
4.7
) holds and show that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha
. By
Lemma

4.9
, it suffices to show that for all
t_{n}\to\infty
, every limit point
\mu
of
(\mu_{t_{n}}^{y})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})=1
. Due to (
4.7
) and the Portmanteau theorem,

\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c})=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\mu(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\})\leq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu^{y}_{t_{n}}(\{d(\cdot,\mathcal{M}_{0})>\epsilon\})=0\,,

as desired.
∎

4.3.
Discrete-Time Semimartingales

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\{M_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a (discrete-time) martingale with respect to some filtration
\{\mathcal{G}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
M_{0}=0
and
\mathbb{E}[(M_{n}-M_{n-1})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\leq B
for some constant
B>0
. Then for every
b>0
,
\delta>0
there is some
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(|M_{n}|\leq bn+C\text{ for all }n\geq 0)>1-\delta,

and
C
depends only on
\delta
,
B
, and
b
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Lemma

2.12
. First note that since
\mathbb{E}[M_{n}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]=M_{n-1}
,

\mathbb{E}[(M_{n}-M_{n-1})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]=\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]-M_{n-1}^{2}.

Thus
\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\leq B+M_{n-1}^{2}
, and by induction
\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{2}]\leq Bn
. By Doob’s inequality, for any integer
m\geq 0

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{2^{m}\leq k\leq 2^{m+1}}\frac{|M_{k}|}{k}\geq\frac{b}{2}\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{k\leq 2^{m+1}}|M_{k}|\geq 2^{m}\frac{b}{2}\Big)\leq\frac{4}{2^{2m}b^{2}}2^{m+1}B=\frac{8B}{2^{m}b^{2}}\,,

and so, for any integer
n\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{2^{n}\leq k}\frac{|M_{k}|}{k}\geq b\Big)\leq\sum_{m=n}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{2^{m}\leq k\leq 2^{m+1}}\frac{|M_{k}|}{k}\geq\frac{b}{2}\Big)\leq\frac{64B}{2^{n}b^{2}}.

Choose
n
large enough so that
\frac{64B}{2^{n}b^{2}}<\frac{\delta}{2}
(note that this
n
only depends on
\delta,B
and
b
). On the other hand, for any
C>0
, applying Doob’s inequality again gives

\mathbb{P}(\max_{0\leq k\leq 2^{n}}|M_{k}|>C)\leq\frac{B2^{n}}{C^{2}}.

For
C
large enough, only depending on
\delta,B,
and
n
, we have
\frac{B2^{n}}{C^{2}}<\frac{\delta}{2}
, which proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables adapted to some filtration
\{\mathcal{G}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

Z_{0}=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}-Z_{n-1})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\leq B,\qquad\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}-Z_{n-1}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\geq b

for some constants
B,b>0
. Then for every
\delta>0
there is
C>0
depending only on
\delta
,
B
, and
b
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(Z_{n}\geq\frac{b}{2}n-C\text{ for all }n\geq 0\Big)>1-\delta\,.

Proof.

It is standard to show that

M_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}-Z_{i-1}-\mathbb{E}[(Z_{i}-Z_{i-1})\mid\mathcal{G}_{i-1}]

is a martingale with respect to

\{\mathcal{G}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}[(M_{n}-M_{n-1})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]=\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}-Z_{n-1}-\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}-Z_{n-1})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}])^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\\
=\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}-Z_{n-1})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]-\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}-Z_{n-1})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]^{2}\leq B,

and therefore the assumptions of
Lemma

4.11
are satisfied. Hence, there is some
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(|M_{n}|\leq\frac{b}{2}n+C\text{ for all }n\geq 0\Big)>1-\delta,

where
C
depends only on
\delta
,
B
, and
b
. Since

Z_{n}=M_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(Z_{i}-Z_{i-1})\mid\mathcal{G}_{i-1}]\geq M_{n}+bn,

then

\mathbb{P}\Big(Z_{n}\geq\frac{b}{2}n-C\text{ for all }n\geq 0\Big)\geq\mathbb{P}\Big(|M_{n}|\leq\frac{b}{2}n+C\text{ for all }n\geq 0\Big)>1-\delta\,,

as desired.
∎

5.
Continuous Dependence on Initial Condition

In this section we extend results known for
C_{0}
-Feller processes to our weaker “
C_{b}
” definition of Feller (
Assumption

2
) combined with
Assumption

3
. Notably, we show that the strong Markov property holds and the entire path of the process (viewed as a random element of the Skorkhod space
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
, defined later) depends continuously on the initial condition. We also recall some facts about the Skorokhod topology and show that certain functions between Skorokhod spaces are continuous. On a first read most of this section (especially the proofs) can be skipped, but the statements of
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.6
should be understood well before reading
Section

6
. We begin with a technical lemma that is not used outside of this section:

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
is Feller (see
Assumption

2
) satisfying
Assumption

3
then for any
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
we have that
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
uniformly on compact sets as
t\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

Let
K_{m}=\{W\leq m\}
and let
A\subset C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
be the set of functions
f
such that
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
uniformly on each
K_{m}
as
t\to 0
.

Let
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
\lambda>0
be arbitrary. We claim that
A
contains the function
R_{\lambda}f
, where
R_{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s}ds
denotes the resolvent operator. Indeed, by Fubini’s theorem
\mathcal{P}_{t}R_{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s+t}ds
and since
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is a contraction, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{t}R_{\lambda}f-R_{\lambda}f\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s+t}fds-\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s}fds\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq(e^{\lambda t}-1)\Big\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s}fds\Big\|+e^{\lambda t}\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda s}\mathcal{P}_{s}fds\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|f\|\frac{e^{\lambda t}-1}{\lambda}\,.

and the claim follows after passing
t\to 0
.

Since
\lambda R_{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-s}\mathcal{P}_{s/\lambda}ds
, by
Assumption

2
and Dominated Convergence Theorem,
\lambda R_{\lambda}f\to f
pointwise as
\lambda\to\infty
. For any integer
m\geq 1
, Riesz-Markov Representation Theorem implies that for any linear functional
F
on
C_{b}(K_{m})
there is a finite Borel measure
\mu
such that
F(f)=\mu f
for any
f\in C_{b}(K_{m})
.
Since
\|\lambda R_{\lambda}f\|\leq\|f\|
,
the Dominated Convergence Theorem implies
\mu(\lambda R_{\lambda}f)\to\mu f
, and therefore

\lambda R_{\lambda}f\rightharpoonup f
in the weak topology on
C_{b}(K_{m})
. By Mazur’s lemma (see
[
Bre11
, Corollary 3.8]
), for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there are convex combinations
f_{n}
of
\lambda R_{\lambda}f
such that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on
K_{m}
. By a diagonal argument, we can assume the convex combinations satisfy
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on each
K_{m}
. Since
A
is closed under finite convex combinations,
f_{n}\in A
, and since

\|\lambda R_{\lambda}f\|\leq\|f\|
we also have
\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|\leq\|f\|<\infty
.

Next we show that our fixed
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
belongs to
A
. Fix
K_{m}
and let
\|\cdot\|_{m}
denote the sup norm over
K_{m}
, that is,
\|f\|_{m}=\sup_{x\in K_{m}}|f(x)|
. For any
t>0
,
M>0
, and
x\in\mathcal{M}
we have

\mathcal{P}_{t}f_{n}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{t}f(x)=\mathbb{E}[f_{n}(X^{x}_{t})-f(X^{x}_{t})]\\
=\mathbb{E}[(f_{n}(X^{x}_{t})-f(X^{x}_{t}))\mathbbm{1}_{X^{x}_{t}\in K_{Mm}}]+\mathbb{E}[(f_{n}(X^{x}_{t})-f(X^{x}_{t}))\mathbbm{1}_{X^{x}_{t}\not\in K_{Mm}}]\,,

and therefore

\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f_{n}-\mathcal{P}_{t}f\|_{m}\leq\|f_{n}-f\|_{Mm}+\|\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\notin K_{Mm})\|_{m}(\|f_{n}\|+\|f\|),

where
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\notin K_{Mm})
is viewed as a function of
x
. By Markov inequality and
Lemma

4.1
,

\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\notin K_{Mm})=\mathbb{P}(W(X_{t}^{x})>Mm)\leq\frac{\mathcal{P}_{t}W(x)}{Mm}\leq\frac{W(x)+Kt}{Mm},

and consequently

\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f_{n}-\mathcal{P}_{t}f\|_{m}\leq\|f_{n}-f\|_{Mm}+\frac{m+Kt}{Mm}(\|f_{n}\|+\|f\|).

Also,

\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f-f\|_{m}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f_{n}-\mathcal{P}_{t}f\|_{m}+\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f_{n}-f_{n}\|_{m}+\|f_{n}-f\|_{m}\,.

The middle term converges to
0
as
t\to 0
because
f_{n}\in A
. The last term and
\|f_{n}-f\|_{Mm}
converge to
0
as
n\to\infty
since
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on each
K_{m}
. By letting
t\to 0
and then
n\to\infty
, we have for any
M>0
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f-f\|_{m}\leq\frac{\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|+\|f\|}{M}.

Since
M
and
m
were arbitrary, therefore
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\to f
uniformly on each
K_{m}
as
t\to 0
. Consequently,
f\in A
. Since
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
was arbitrary, by our definition of
A
the claim is proven.
∎

One important consequence of
Assumption

2
is the strong Markov property.
In fact, the proof is identical to the one for
C_{0}
Feller semigroups.
In order to state the strong Markov property, we introduce the Skorokhod space
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
, which consists of all cadlag functions
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{M}
endowed with the Skorokhod (
J_{1}
-)topology. Specifically,
f_{n}\to f
if and only if there exists increasing bijections
\lambda_{n}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
f_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}\to f
and
\lambda_{n}\to\textnormal{Id}
uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
, where
Id
denotes the identity map on
[0,\infty)
. It is known that
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
is a Polish space and that the Borel
\sigma
- algebra is generated by the projections
\pi_{t}:f\mapsto f(t)
(see
[
Kal21
, Lemma A5.3]
). Thus, there is no difference between processes
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)}
with cadlag sample paths and random elements
X
of
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
. In what follows,
X^{x}
denotes the random variable given by
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto X_{\cdot}^{x}(\omega)\in\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
is Feller (see
Assumption

2
), then it satisfies the strong Markov property, meaning that if
\phi:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to[0,\infty)
is a measurable function and
\tau
is a stopping time, then
\tilde{\phi}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
given by

\tilde{\phi}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(X^{y})]

is measurable
and for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\tau<\infty}\phi(X_{\tau+\cdot}^{x})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]=\mathbbm{1}_{\tau<\infty}\tilde{\phi}(X_{\tau}^{x}).

Proof.

The proof is the same as
[
LG16
, Theorems 6.16 and 6.17]
. We remark that our notation differs from
[
LG16
]
. For example, we use
\mathcal{M}
instead of
E
,
X_{t}^{x}
instead of
Y_{t}
,
\mathcal{P}_{t}
instead of
Q_{t}
, and
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
instead of
C_{0}(E)
. Note that
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
differs from
C_{0}(E)
, but the proof in
[
LG16
]
is unchanged if
C_{0}(E)
is replaced by
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
as it only uses continuity of functions in
C_{0}(E)
, but not the vanishing at infinity.
The book
[
LG16
]
also uses a stronger definition of Feller, but in the proofs of Theorems 6.16 and 6.17, it only uses that
Q_{t}:C_{0}(E)\to C_{0}(E)
, which in our notation translates to
\mathcal{P}_{t}:C_{b}(\mathcal{M})\to C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Assumption

2
).
∎

Remark 5.3
.

For example, if
\tau
is a finite stopping time,
s>0
, and
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, then applying the strong Markov property detailed in
Lemma

5.2
with
\phi(x(\cdot)):=x(s)
we have

(5.1)

\mathcal{P}_{s}f(X_{\tau}^{x})=\mathbb{E}[f(X_{\tau+s}^{x})|\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]\,.

The Feller property (
Assumption

2
) tells us that if
x_{n}\to x
, then
X_{t}^{x_{n}}\to X_{t}^{x}
(in distribution). In other words, the law of the process at time
t
depends continuously on the initial position. Using
Assumption

3
, we can actually show that the entire path of the process (viewed as a random element of
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
) depends continuously on the initial condition.

First, we recall an Arzela-Ascoli-type result for weak convergence on
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
:

Lemma 5.4
.

If
Y^{n},Y
are
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
-valued random variables, then
Y^{n}\to Y
in distribution if both statements hold:

(i)

For any
0\leq t_{1}<\ldots<t_{m}
,
(Y^{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,Y^{n}(t_{m}))\to(Y(t_{1}),\ldots,Y(t_{m}))
in distribution.

(ii)

For any
t>0
, any sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of stopping times with
\tau_{n}\leq t
, and any sequence
(h_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of positive constants such that
h_{n}\to 0
, we have
\mathbb{E}[d(Y_{\tau_{n}}^{n},Y_{\tau_{n}+h_{n}}^{n})]\to 0
.

Remark 5.5
.

By convergence in distribution we mean that the laws of the random variables converge weakly, and in
(i)
we view each
(Y^{n}(t_{1}),\ldots,Y^{n}(t_{m}))
as a
\mathcal{M}^{m}
-valued random variable. In
(ii)
, we view each
Y^{n}
as a collection of
\mathcal{M}
-valued random variables
Y^{n}_{t}\coloneqq Y^{n}(t)
and
\tau_{n}
is a stopping time with respect to the filtration generated by
\{Y^{n}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Can be found in
[
Kal21
, Theorem 23.9i and Theorem 23.11]
.
∎

The proof of the following lemma is inspired by
[
Kal21
, Theorem 17.25]
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
is Feller (see
Assumption

2
) and satisfies
Assumption

3
. If
x_{n}\to x
then
X^{x_{n}}\to X^{x}
in distribution. (Recall we use
X^{x}
to denote the random variable given by
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto X_{\cdot}^{x}(\omega)\in\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
.)

Proof.

It suffices to show conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
of
Lemma

5.4
.

Proof of
(i)
.
The proof is by induction on
m
. If
m=1
, then for any
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
,
Assumption

2
implies

\mathcal{P}_{t_{1}}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, and therefore

\mathbb{E}[f(X^{x_{n}}(t_{1}))]=\mathcal{P}_{t_{1}}f(x_{n})\to\mathcal{P}_{t_{1}}f(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(X^{x}(t_{1}))].

Suppose the claim holds for
m-1
and fix
0\leq t_{1}<\ldots<t_{m}
and let
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M}^{m})
be Lipschitz. Recall that by Portmanteau theorem it suffices to consider only Lipschitz functions
f
to show weak convergence of measures, or equivalently the convergence in distribution of the corresponding random variables.
Let
\phi:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

\phi(x(\cdot))=f(x(0),x(t_{2}-t_{1}),\dots,x(t_{m}-t_{1}))

and
\tilde{\phi}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
be as defined in
Lemma

5.2
:

(5.2)

\tilde{\phi}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(X^{y})]=\mathbb{E}[f(y,X^{y}(t_{2}-t_{1}),\dots,X^{y}(t_{m}-t_{1}))]\,.

Applying the (strong) Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
) with
\tau=t_{1}
, for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
we have

(5.3)

\mathbb{E}[f(X^{x}(t_{1}),X^{x}(t_{2}),\dots,X^{x}(t_{m}))]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\phi}(X^{x}(t_{1}))]=\mathcal{P}_{t_{1}}\tilde{\phi}(x)\,.

Next, we show that the left hand side of (
5.3
) is continuous in
x
, which by
Assumption

2
follows once we prove that
\tilde{\phi}\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
. By our inductive hypothesis,

\mathcal{M}\ni y\mapsto(X^{y}(t_{2}-t_{1}),\dots,X^{y}(t_{m}-t_{1}))\in P(\mathcal{M}^{m-1})

is continuous (again we conflate random variables with their laws).

Since
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M}^{m})
is Lipschitz,

\mathcal{M}\ni y\mapsto f_{y}\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M}^{m-1})\quad\text{where}\quad f_{y}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1})\coloneqq f(y,x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1})

is continuous. Since

P(\mathcal{M}^{m-1})\times C_{b}(\mathcal{M}^{m-1})\ni(\mu,f)\mapsto\mu f\in\mathbb{R}

is continuous, by (
5.2
)
\tilde{\phi}
is continuous, as desired.

Proof of
(ii)
Let
t>0
,
(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, and
(h_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be as in
Lemma

5.4

(ii)
. Define
\phi_{n}:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to[0,1]
as
\phi_{n}(Y)=d(Y(0),Y(h_{n}))
and
\tilde{\phi}_{n}(y)=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{n}(X^{y})]
. By the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
),

(5.4)

\mathbb{E}[d(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x_{n}},X_{\tau_{n}+h_{n}}^{x_{n}})]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\phi}_{n}(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x_{n}})]=\nu_{n}\tilde{\phi}_{n},

where
\nu_{n}
denotes the law of
X_{\tau_{n}}^{x_{n}}
. Since
\tau_{n}\leq t
,
Lemma

4.1
implies

\nu_{n}W=\mathbb{E}[W(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x_{n}})]\leq W(x_{n})+Kt.

Since
x_{n}\to x
,
\sup_{n}W(x_{n})<\infty
, and therefore
\sup_{n}\nu_{n}W<\infty
. This implies

(5.5)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{n}\nu_{n}(K_{m})=0\,.

Note that by the Markov property,

(5.6)

\tilde{\phi}_{n}(x)=\mathbb{E}[d(x,X_{h_{n}}^{x})]=\mathbb{E}[d_{x}(X_{h_{n}}^{x})]=\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)\,,

where
d_{x}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
given by
d_{x}(y)=d(x,y)
is continuous.

Let
K_{m}=\{W\leq m\}
and
\|\cdot\|_{m}
be the supremum norm over
K_{m}
as in the proof of
Lemma

5.1
. Then for any
m
and any
x,y\in K_{m}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle|\tilde{\phi}_{n}(x)-\tilde{\phi}_{n}(y)|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{y}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{y}\|+|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq d(x,y)+|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(y)|

and by the Feller property (see
Assumption

2
) we have that
\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}
, and thus
\tilde{\phi}_{n}
, is continuous.
In addition, by using
d_{x}(x)=0
it holds that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(y)|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(x)-d_{x}(x)-d_{x}(y)+d_{x}(y)-\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq d(x,y)+2\|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}-d_{x}\|_{m}\,,

and consequently

(5.7)

\displaystyle|\tilde{\phi}_{n}(x)-\tilde{\phi}_{n}(y)|\leq 2d(x,y)+2\|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}-d_{x}\|_{m}\,.

Lemma

5.1
implies
\|\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}-d_{x}\|_{m}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and thus by (
5.7
) and continuity of each
\tilde{\phi}_{n}
we have that
\{\tilde{\phi}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is equicontinuous on
K_{m}
.
By
Assumption

2
and
h_{n}\to 0
, for each
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{P}_{h_{n}}d_{x}\to d_{x}
pointwise, which by (
5.6
) means that
\tilde{\phi}_{n}\to 0
pointwise since
d_{x}(x)=0
. Then Arzela-Ascoli theorem yields that
\tilde{\phi}_{n}\to 0
uniformly on each
K_{m}
. Since
\sup_{n}\|\tilde{\phi}_{n}\|\leq 1
, then by (
5.4
) and (
5.5
),
\mathbb{E}[d(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x_{n}},X_{\tau_{n}+h_{n}}^{x_{n}})]=\nu_{n}\tilde{\phi}_{n}\to 0
, which proves
(ii)
.
∎

The proof of the next lemma is standard for the more familiar topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, but since we work with the Skorokhod topology we provide more details.

Lemma 5.7
.

The following functions are continuous:

(i)

x(\cdot)\mapsto g(x(\cdot))
from
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})
to
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
, where
g:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous.

(ii)

x(\cdot)\mapsto\int_{0}^{\cdot}x(s)ds
from
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
.

(iii)

x(\cdot)\mapsto\inf_{0\leq s\leq\cdot}x(s)
from
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
.

(iv)

x(\cdot)\mapsto x(T)
from
\{x\in\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})\mid x\text{ continuous}\}\eqqcolon C_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, where
T
is a constant.

Proof.

The claim (iv) holds true, since the subspace topology of
C_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
coincides with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets (see for example
[
Kal21
, Theorem 23.9iii]
).

Next, we prove (i) – (iii).
In all three cases the corresponding map denoted
F:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(A)\to\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
(where
A=\mathcal{M}
or
\mathbb{R}
) is continuous if we endow
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(A)
and
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.
Thus, by the definition of the Skorokhod topology it suffices to show that if
f_{n}\in\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(A)
and there exists an increasing bijection
\lambda_{n}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
f_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}\to f
and
\lambda_{n}\to\textnormal{Id}
uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
, then

(5.8)

F(f_{n})\circ\lambda_{n}-F(f_{n}\circ\lambda_{n})\to 0

uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
(we already noted that
F(f_{n}\circ\lambda_{n})\to F(f)
).

For (i) and (iii), (
5.8
) is immediate since respectively
(g\circ f_{n})\circ\lambda_{n}=g\circ(f_{n}\circ\lambda_{n})
, and
\inf_{0\leq s\leq\lambda_{n}(t)}f_{n}(s)=\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))
for each
t
.

For (ii) we need to show that for all
T>0

(5.9)

\int_{0}^{\lambda_{n}(t)}f_{n}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))ds\to 0

uniformly for
t\in[0,T]
. Since
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges in
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
,
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is relatively compact in
\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
and thus by
[
Kal21
, Theorem A5.4]
we have

(5.10)

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sup_{n}\tilde{w}_{T+1}(f_{n},h)=0\,,

where for any
g:[0,T+1)\to\mathcal{M}
and
h>0

\tilde{w}_{T+1}(g,h)=\inf_{(I_{k})}\max_{k}\sup_{r,s\in I_{k}}|g(r)-g(s)|

and the infimum is taken over all partitions of the interval

[0,T+1)
into sub-intervals
I_{k}=[u_{k},v_{k})
with
v_{k}-u_{k}\geq h
when

v_{k}<T+1
.

To prove (
5.9
) we
fix any
T>0
and denote

C:=1+\sup_{t\in[0,T+1]}|f(t)|

For any fixed
\epsilon>0
, by (
5.10
) there is a finite partition of
[0,T+1)
into
M=M(\epsilon)
intervals
I_{k}=[u_{k},v_{k})
such that
\sup_{s,t\in I_{k}}|f_{n}(s)-f_{n}(t)|<\epsilon
for any
k\leq M
,
n\geq 1
.
Fix
N\geq 1
such that for any
n\geq N
we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\lambda_{n}(t)-t|<\frac{\epsilon}{CM}\wedge 1\quad\text{ and }\quad C\geq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(t))|\,.

Then for any
n\geq N
and
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle J_{1}(t):=\Big|\int_{0}^{\lambda_{n}(t)}f_{n}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}f_{n}(s)ds\Big|\leq C\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\lambda_{n}(t)-t|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{M}

and

\displaystyle Q_{k}

\displaystyle:=\int_{u_{k}}^{v_{k}}|f_{n}(s)-f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))|ds\leq\int_{u_{k}+\frac{\epsilon}{CM}}^{v_{k}-\frac{\epsilon}{CM}}|f_{n}(s)-f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))|ds

\displaystyle+\int_{u_{k}}^{u_{k}+\frac{\epsilon}{CM}}|f_{n}(s)-f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))|ds+\int_{v_{k}-\frac{\epsilon}{CM}}^{v_{k}}|f_{n}(s)-f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))|ds

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon(v_{k}-u_{k})+2C\frac{2\epsilon}{CM}=\epsilon(v_{k}-u_{k})+\frac{4\epsilon}{M}\,,

where in the last inequality we used that
s,\lambda_{n}(s)\in[u_{k},v_{k})
if
s\in[u_{k}+\frac{\epsilon}{CM},v_{k}-\frac{\epsilon}{CM})
. The desired result (
5.9
) follows once we observe that

\Big|\int_{0}^{\lambda_{n}(t)}f_{n}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}f_{n}(\lambda_{n}(s))ds\Big|\leq J_{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}Q_{k}\leq\epsilon(T+1)+4\epsilon+\frac{\epsilon}{M}\,.

∎

6.
Proof of Main Theorem

In this entire section, we fix a Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
(see
Definition

2.1
) satisfying
Assumption

1
–
5
.
We aim to provide a proof of
Theorem

3.2
which is split into two parts. In the first step we show that for any compact set
\mathcal{K}
, if
V(x)
is big enough for some
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, then, with high probability,

X_{t}^{x}
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
whenever
t
is such that
X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{K}
.
Furthermore, we establish that there is a sequence of times
t_{n}\to\infty
such that
V(X_{t_{n}}^{x})\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
X_{t_{n}}^{x}\in\mathcal{K}

for all
n
. Specifically, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

There is an
N>0
such that for all
\delta>0,M>N
there is
D>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V>D\}\cap\{W\leq M\}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}\leq\frac{1}{M}\text{ and }\limsup_{t\to\infty,W(X_{t}^{y})\leq M}V(X_{t}^{y})=\infty\Big)\geq 1-\delta\,,

where
\limsup_{t\to\infty,W(X_{t}^{y})\leq M}V(X_{t}^{y})=\infty
means that there is some sequence of times
t_{n}\to\infty
such that
W(X_{t_{n}}^{y})\leq M
and
V(X_{t_{n}}^{y})\to\infty
.

In the second step we show that
Theorem

6.1
implies
Theorem

3.2
, which amounts to showing that if
V(x)
is big enough, then almost surely all limit points of the sequence of measures
\mu_{t}^{x}
(see
Definition

4.4
) are supported on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

To prove
Theorem

6.1
we sample
X_{t}^{x}
at special stopping times that are
introduced and analyzed
in
Section

6.1
,
Section

6.2
, and
Section

6.3
. Then, we obtain a discrete-time Markov chain which, after composition with
V
, induces
a discrete-time semimartingale satisfying the assumptions of
Corollary

4.12
. We make this argument precise in
Section

6.4
to prove
Theorem

6.1
and then finally prove
Theorem

3.2
in
Section

6.5
.

6.1.
Good Stopping Time

For this section, recall
Assumption

4
. We start by showing that as long as
y
is close enough to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, there is some bounded stopping time
\tau_{y,T}
(see
Definition

6.4
below) for which
V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)
is (on average) large. The idea is to
estimate
V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)
by bounding

\int_{0}^{t}H(X^{y}_{s})ds
. Since we have information about the behavior of
\int_{0}^{t}H(X^{y}_{s})ds
when
y\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, we can use the continuity with respect to initial conditions shown in
Section

5
to infer similar behavior when
y
is close to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
\delta>0
, and
S>0
there is
T\geq S
and
\epsilon>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}
with
d(y,x)<\epsilon
,

P_{y,T}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}H(X_{s}^{y})ds>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\text{ and }\inf_{0\leq t^{\prime}\leq T}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds>-\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big)>1-\delta\,.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Then
Lemma

4.9
(ii)
is satisfied, and therefore

Lemma

4.9
(i)
holds true, that is,
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mu_{t}^{x}(H)\geq\alpha
a.s. for
\mu_{t}^{x}
as in
Definition

4.4
.
Hence, almost surely there is a finite (random)
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mu_{t}^{x}H=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{x})ds>\frac{\alpha}{2}
for all
t\geq\mathcal{T}
. By making
\mathcal{T}
larger if necessary, we may suppose
\inf_{0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds>-\frac{\alpha}{2}t
for all
t\geq\mathcal{T}
. It follows that
\liminf_{T\to\infty}P_{x,T}=1
, so there is a (deterministic)
T>S
such that
P_{x,T}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}
.

Define the map
F_{T}:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
which is the composition of the following maps

\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\ni x(\cdot)\mapsto H(x(\cdot))\mapsto\int_{0}^{\cdot}H(x(s))ds\mapsto\inf_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{0}^{t}H(x(s))ds\,.

Then,
F
is continuous by
Lemma

5.7
, and similarly

G_{T}:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
given by

G_{T}(x(\cdot)):=\int_{0}^{T}H(x(s))ds

is continuous. Thus,
Q:=\big\{Y\in\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M}):G_{T}(Y)>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\text{ and }F_{T}(Y)>-\frac{\alpha}{2}T\big\}
is open and

P_{y,T}=\mathbb{P}(X^{y}_{\cdot}\in Q)\,.

Then
Lemma

5.6
and the Portmanteau theorem give us

\liminf_{y\to x}P_{y,T}\geq P_{x,T}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}\,.

Thus, by choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough,
d(y,x)<\epsilon

implies
P_{y,T}>1-\delta
.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

For every
\delta>0
and compact
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
there is
S>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
T\geq S
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big|V(X_{t}^{x})-V(x)-\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big|>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big)\leq\delta\,.

Proof.

Let
M_{t}^{V}(x)
be as in (
2.1
).
By
Corollary

4.2

V
has linearly bounded quadratic variation (see
Definition

2.11
), so there is a continuous function
C:\overline{\mathcal{M}_{+}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for
T\geq 1
and
x\in\overline{\mathcal{M}_{+}}
it holds that

(6.1)

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{P}_{s}\Gamma V(x)ds\leq C(x)T\,.

By Tonelli’s theorem and
Corollary

4.2

\mathbb{E}[(M_{T}^{V}(x))^{2}]=\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{P}_{s}\Gamma V(x)ds\leq C(x)T\,.

Then by Doob’s inequality and (
6.1
),

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|M_{t}^{V}(x)|>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big)\leq\frac{4}{T^{2}\alpha^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(M_{T}^{V}(x))^{2}]\leq\frac{4}{T\alpha^{2}}C(x).

Since
C
is continuous and thus bounded on the compact set
\mathcal{K}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}_{+}}
,

\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|M_{t}^{V}(x)|>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big)=0,

which proves the claim.
∎

Definition 6.4
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
T>0
define the bounded stopping time

\tau_{x,T}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0:|V(X_{t}^{x})-V(x)|>\alpha T\}\wedge\inf\Big\{t\geq 0:\Big|\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big|>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big\}\wedge T.

Lemma 6.5
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
S>0
there is
\epsilon>0
and
T\geq S
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
d(y,x)<\epsilon
,
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)]\geq\frac{\alpha}{4}T
.

Proof.

For any
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
T>0
we define the events

Q\coloneqq\Big\{\int_{0}^{T}H(X_{s}^{y})ds>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big\}\cap\Big\{\inf_{0\leq t^{\prime}\leq T}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds>-\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big\}\\
\cap\Big\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big|V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)-\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big\}=:Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}\cap Q_{3}\,,

and

\Big\{\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big\}=:R\,.

Fix some compact neighborhood
\mathcal{K}
of
x
(recall that
\mathcal{M}
is locally compact).
By
Lemma

6.3
, there is some
S>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
T\geq S
,

(6.2)

\mathbb{P}(Q_{3}^{c})=\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big|V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)-\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|>\frac{\alpha}{2}T\Big)\leq\frac{1}{8}.

By
Lemma

6.2
, there is a
T\geq S
and
\epsilon>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}
with
d(y,x)<\epsilon
, it holds that

(6.3)

\mathbb{P}(Q_{1}\cap Q_{2})=P_{y,T}>\frac{7}{8}\,.

We may decrease
\epsilon>0
if necessary such that
d(y,x)<\epsilon
implies
y\in\mathcal{K}
. We claim that
Q\subset R
. Indeed, for fixed
\omega\in Q
, if
\tau_{y,T}(\omega)=T
, then
\omega\in Q_{1}
implies
\omega\in R
. If
\tau_{y,T}(\omega)<T
, then either
\Big|\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}T
, in which case
\omega\in Q_{2}
implies
\omega\in R
, or
|V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)|\geq\alpha T
, in which case
\omega\in Q_{3}
yields that

\displaystyle\alpha T

\displaystyle\leq|V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)-\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|+\Big|\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}T+\Big|\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|\,,

and again
\omega\in Q_{2}
implies
\omega\in R
, as desired.

Hence, by (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) we have for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
d(y,x)<\epsilon
that

(6.4)

\mathbb{P}(R)\geq\mathbb{P}(Q)=\mathbb{P}((Q_{1}\cap Q_{2})\setminus Q_{3}^{c})\geq\mathbb{P}(Q_{1}\cap Q_{2})-\mathbb{P}(Q_{3}^{c})\geq\frac{7}{8}-\frac{1}{8}=\frac{3}{4}\,.

By the continuity of
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{y})ds
and the definition of
\tau_{y,T}
(
Definition

6.4
) we have
\Big|\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}T
, and thus by (
6.4
) we conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big]\geq\mathbb{P}(R)\frac{\alpha}{2}T+(1-\mathbb{P}(R))\frac{-\alpha}{2}T\geq\frac{\alpha}{4}T\,.

Since
\tau_{y,T}
is a bounded stopping time, by
Corollary

4.3

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\tau_{y,T}}^{y})-V(y)]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau_{y,T}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big],

which proves the claim.
∎

6.2.
Return Time

As mentioned at the beginning of this section, we wish to sample the paths of
X_{t}^{y}
at a sequence of stopping times
\tau_{n}
so that
V(X_{\tau_{n}}^{y})
satisfies
Corollary

4.12
. This means that we not only need a uniform lower bound on
\mathbb{E}[V(X^{y}_{\tau_{1}})-V(y)]
, but also a uniform upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[(V(X^{y}_{\tau_{1}})-V(y))^{2}]
. Given any
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
S>0
,
Lemma

6.5
gives us the required uniform lower bound (namely,
\frac{\alpha}{4}S
) using
\tau_{1}=\tau_{y,T_{x}}
for some
T_{x}\geq S
. However, this is only a local result holding for
y
close enough to
x
. In particular, if
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is not compact there is no reason to believe that
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}T_{x}<\infty
. Since it is unclear how to obtain an upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[(V(X^{y}_{\tau_{y,T}})-V(y))^{2}]
which is independent of
T
, we need to modify
\tau_{y,T}
to ensure that
X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}
stays in some fixed compact set. In particular, we let the process run until
X^{y}_{t}
reenters a fixed compact set at time
\sigma_{x,T,m}
(see
Definition

6.6
below). The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving an upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[|V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})|]
which ensures that
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(x)]
is big.

Definition 6.6
.

For
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
T>0
, and
m>0
we define the stopping time

\sigma_{x,T,m}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq\tau_{x,T}\mid W(X_{t}^{x})<m\}\,,

where
\tau_{x,T}
is as in
Definition

6.4
.

Lemma 6.7
.

For
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
T>0
, and
m>0
,
X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
a.s.

Proof.

By
Assumption

1
and
Definition

2.2
,
X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
a.s. By the right-continuity of the sample paths of
X_{t}^{x}
and the definition of
\sigma_{x,T,m}
(
Definition

6.6
),
X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x}\in\{W\leq m\}
a.s.
∎

For the rest of the section, recall that
K>0
is as in
Assumption

3
.

Lemma 6.8
.

There is
N>0
such that for all
m\geq N
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
the stopping time
\eta_{m}(x)\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\mid W(X_{t}^{x})<m\}
satisfies

\tilde{\eta}_{m}(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\eta_{m}(x)]\leq W(x).

Proof.

Since
W^{\prime}
is proper, there is a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that
W^{\prime}(x)\geq K+1
for
x\notin\mathcal{K}
. Let
N
be the maximum value of
W+1
on
\mathcal{K}
. Fix any
m\geq N
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
and we simplify the notation by setting
\eta\coloneqq\eta_{m}(x)
. Then by the definition of
\eta
we have

\inf_{0\leq t<\eta}W(X_{t}^{x})\geq m\geq N\,.

Since
X_{t}^{x}\notin\mathcal{K}
implies
W^{\prime}(X_{t}^{x})\geq K+1
for all
0\leq t<\eta
, then
W\geq 0
and
Lemma

4.1
applied to
\eta\wedge t
yield for any
t\geq 0

(K+1)\mathbb{E}[\eta\wedge t]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[W(X_{\eta\wedge t}^{x})+\int_{0}^{\eta\wedge t}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]\leq W(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\eta\wedge t].

Thus,
\mathbb{E}[\eta\wedge t]\leq W(x)
and the claim follows from the Monotone convergence theorem after passing
t\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

There is
N>0
such that for
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
T>0
, and
m\geq N
,

\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}-\tau_{x,T}]\leq W(x)+KT.

In particular,

\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}]\leq W(x)+(K+1)T.

Proof.

Let
N>0
be as in
Lemma

6.8
, fix any
m\geq N
and let
\eta_{m}(x)
be as in
Lemma

6.8
.
By
Lemma

6.8
,
\eta_{m}(x)
is almost surely finite,
and then from the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
) it follows that

\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}-\tau_{x,T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{x,T}}]=\tilde{\eta}_{m}(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})\leq W(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x}).

Since
\tau_{x,T}\leq T
, by
Lemma

4.1

\mathbb{E}[W(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]\leq W(x)+KT,

which proves the first claim. The second one follows since
\tau_{x,T}\leq T
.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

For every
\beta>0
there is
N>0
such that for all
T>0
,
m\geq N
, and
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
,

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]\geq-\beta(K+1)(m+KT).

Proof.

Since
H
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
(
Remark

2.19
) and
W
is proper (
Assumption

3
), there is
N>0
such that
W(y)\geq N-1
implies

|H(y)|\leq\beta W^{\prime}(y)
. Increase
N
if necessary such that the assumptions of
Lemma

6.8
are satisfied and fix
m\geq N
.

For any
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\geq m\}
,
let
\eta\coloneqq\eta_{m}(y)
be as in
Lemma

6.8
and recall that
\mathbb{E}[\eta]\leq W(y)
. Since
W(X^{y}_{t})\geq m\geq N
for
t\in(0,\eta)
, we have
|H(X^{y}_{t})|\leq\beta W^{\prime}(X^{y}_{t})
for
t\in(0,\eta)
, and so it follows from
Corollary

4.3
that

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\eta}^{y})-V(y)]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\eta}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big]\geq\beta\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\eta}-W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big].

By
Lemma

4.1
and
\mathbb{E}[\eta]\leq W(y)
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\eta}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big]\leq W(y)+K\mathbb{E}[\eta]\leq(K+1)W(y)\,,

and therefore

(6.5)

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\eta}^{y})-V(y)]\geq-\beta(K+1)W(y).

Fix
T>0
and
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
and note that
x
belongs to a different set than
y
above.

By the definition of
\sigma_{x,T,m}
, the
strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
), and (
6.5
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x}))\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})\geq m}]

\displaystyle\geq-\beta(K+1)\mathbb{E}[W(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})].

Noting that
\tau_{x,T}\leq T
(see
Definition

6.4
), applying
Lemma

4.1
, and recalling that
W(x)\leq m
proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 6.11
.

There is
N>0
such that for all
m\geq N
there is
S>0
such that for all
T\geq S

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]\geq-\frac{\alpha T}{8}\,,

where
\alpha>0
is as in
Assumption

4
.

Proof.

Let
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{16K(K+1)}
, let
N>0
be as in
Lemma

6.10
, and fix
m\geq N
. For any
T>0
, by
Lemma

6.10

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]\geq-\beta(K+1)(m+KT).

Let
S=\frac{m}{K}
so that
T\geq S
implies
m+KT\leq 2KT
, which gives

\beta(K+1)(m+KT)\leq\frac{\alpha T}{8},

proving the claim.
∎

Definition 6.12
.

For the rest of the paper, we fix
N>0
large enough to satisfy
Lemma

6.8
,
Corollary

6.9
, and
Corollary

6.11
. In particular, recalling the proof of
Lemma

6.8
we may assume that
W(x)\geq N
implies
W^{\prime}(x)\geq K+1
and thus
\mathcal{L}W(x)\leq-1
by
Assumption

3
.

For every
\epsilon>0
and any closed set
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
we define

(6.6)

N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{S}):=\{y\in\mathcal{M}\mid d(y,\mathcal{S})=\inf_{x\in\mathcal{S}}d(y,x)<\epsilon\}\,.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
N
be as in
Definition

6.12
.
For every
m\geq N
there is
\epsilon>0
,
n\geq 1
, and
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
there is some
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{y,T_{i},m}}^{y})-V(y)]\geq\frac{\alpha T_{i}}{8}
.

Proof.

Fix any
m\geq N
. By
Corollary

6.11
, there is
S>0
such that for all
T\geq S

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(X_{\tau_{x,T}}^{x})]\geq-\frac{\alpha T}{8}.

By
Lemma

6.5
, for every
x\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
there is
\epsilon_{x}>0
and
T_{x}\geq S
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
d(y,x)<\epsilon_{x}
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\tau_{y,T_{x}}}^{y})-V(y)]\geq\frac{\alpha T_{x}}{4}
. Thus, if
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
and
d(y,x)<\epsilon_{x}
,

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{y,T_{x},m}}^{y})-V(y)]\geq\frac{\alpha T_{x}}{8}.

Hence, by the compactness of
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\}
,
there is
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})
there is some
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{y,T_{i},m}}^{y})-V(y)]\geq\frac{\alpha T_{i}}{8}
.
To finish the claim, we show that there is
\epsilon>0
such
that

\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})\subset\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})\,.

Since
\{W\leq m\}
is compact, then
\{W\leq m\}\setminus N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})
is compact and is covered by the collection of open sets
(\{W\leq m\}\setminus\overline{N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})})_{\epsilon>0}
, because

\bigcup_{\epsilon>0}\{W\leq m\}\backslash\overline{N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}=\{W\leq m\}\backslash\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{W\leq m\}\backslash(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})

which clearly contains
\{W\leq m\}\backslash N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})
. Thus there is
\epsilon>0
such that

\{W\leq m\}\backslash N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})\subset\{W\leq m\}\backslash\overline{N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})},

which means that

\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\{W\leq m\})\supset\{W\leq m\}\cap\overline{N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})}\supset\{W\leq m\}\cap N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})

and the proof is finished.
∎

6.3.
Bounding the Variance

In this section we upper bound
\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(x))^{2}]
, where
\sigma_{x,T,m}
was introduced in
Definition

6.6
. We start by bounding
\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]
.

Lemma 6.14
.

For every
T>0
and
m>N
(see
Definition

6.12
) we have

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]<\infty\,,

where
\sigma_{x,T,m}
was defined in
Definition

6.6
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
, denote

\sigma=\sigma_{x,T,m}
, and note that
\sigma
is a.s. finite by
Corollary

6.9
. By
Assumption

3

(i)
(see
Definition

2.10
) and
Assumption

5

(i)
(see
Definition

2.9
),

M_{t}\coloneqq(M_{t}^{W}(x))^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma W(X_{s}^{x})ds+KM_{t}^{U}(x)

is a local martingale since it is a sum of a martingale and a local martingale. By
Assumption

5

(iii)
and
(ii)
,
\Gamma W\leq KU^{\prime}\leq K(K-\mathcal{L}U)
, and therefore by the definition of
M_{t}^{U}(x)
(see (
2.1
)) we have for each
t\geq 0
that

(6.7)

M_{t}\geq(M_{t}^{W}(x))^{2}-K^{2}t+K(U(X_{t}^{x})-U(x))\geq-K^{2}t-KU(x).

In particular,

\inf_{t\geq 0}M_{t\wedge\sigma}\geq-K^{2}\sigma-KU(x)

and by
Corollary

6.9
,

(6.8)

\mathbb{E}[K^{2}\sigma]\leq K^{2}(W(x)+(K+1)T)\,.

Thus the martingale
M_{t\wedge\sigma}
is uniformly bounded from below by an integrable random variable, so by Fatou’s lemma, optional stopping and (
6.7
), we obtain

(6.9)

0\geq\mathbb{E}[M_{\sigma}]\geq\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma}^{W}(x))^{2}-K^{2}\sigma-KU(x)].

By applying the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-b^{2}
twice,

\displaystyle(M_{\sigma}^{W}(x))^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{s}^{x})>N}ds\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{s}^{x})\leq N}ds\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-(W(X_{\sigma}^{x})-W(x))^{2}.

Observe
W(X_{s}^{x})\geq m>N
for all
s\in(\tau_{x,T},\sigma)
, and let
A\coloneqq\sup_{W(x)\leq N}|\mathcal{L}W(x)|
, which is finite since
\mathcal{L}W
is continuous (by
Definition

2.9
) and
W
is proper (by
Assumption

3
). Since
\tau_{x,T}\leq T
,

\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})1_{W(X_{s}^{x})\leq N}ds\Big)^{2}=\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{x,T}}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})1_{W(X_{s}^{x})\leq N}ds\Big)^{2}\leq A^{2}T^{2}.

Similarly, since
W(x)>N
implies
\mathcal{L}W(x)\leq-1
(recall
Definition

6.12
), we obtain

\frac{1}{4}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})1_{W(X_{s}^{x})>N}ds\Big)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}(\sigma-\tau_{x,T})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{8}\sigma^{2}-\frac{1}{4}T^{2}.

Finally, since we assumed
W(x)\leq m
, then it follows from
Lemma

6.7
that

(W(X_{\sigma}^{x})-W(x))^{2}\leq 4m^{2}\,.

Overall,

(M_{\sigma}^{W}(x))^{2}\geq\frac{1}{8}\sigma^{2}-A^{\prime}

where
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}T^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A^{2}T^{2}+4m^{2}
is a positive constant. A substitution into
(
6.9
) gives us

0\geq\mathbb{E}[M_{\sigma}]\geq\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{8}\sigma^{2}-A^{\prime}-K^{2}\sigma-KU(x)\Big].

Rearranging and using (
6.8
) gives

\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}]\leq 8A^{\prime}+8K^{2}(W(x)+(K+1)T)+8KU(x),

which proves the claim since the right hand side is uniformly bounded for
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

For every
T>0
,
m>N
(see
Definition

6.12
)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(x))^{2}]<\infty\,.

Proof.

Fix
T>0
,
m>N
, and
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
. First note that for
M^{V}_{t}
as in (
2.1
) and
H
as in
Assumption

4

(iii)
(so
H
agrees with
\mathcal{L}V
on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
) we have

\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(x))^{2}]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{V}(x))^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]\,.

Since
H
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
(see
Remark

2.19
,
Definition

2.18
), there is
A>0
such that
|H|\leq A+W^{\prime}
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}H(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq 2A^{2}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big].

Since
0\leq W^{\prime}\leq K-\mathcal{L}W
(see
Assumption

3
),

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}W^{\prime}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq 2K^{2}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big].

Furthermore, by (
2.1
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{x,T,m}}\mathcal{L}W(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{W}(x))^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[(W(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-W(x))^{2}]\,.

By
Lemma

6.7
and our assumption that
W(x)\leq m
,

\mathbb{E}[(W(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-W(x))^{2}]\leq 4m^{2}\,.

Combining all of these inequalities,

\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{x})-V(x))^{2}]\\
\leq 2\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{V}(x))^{2}]+(4A^{2}+8K^{2})\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]+16\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{W}(x))^{2}]+64m^{2}.

We finish the proof by noting that by

Lemma

6.14
it holds that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}^{2}]<\infty

and by
Corollary

4.2

\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{V}(x))^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[(M_{\sigma_{x,T,m}}^{W}(x))^{2}]\leq 2K(U(x)+K\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{x,T,m}])\,,

where the right hand side is uniformly bounded for
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq m\}
due to
Lemma

6.14
and
the continuity of
U
on the compact set
\{W\leq m\}
.
∎

6.4.
Proof of
Theorem

6.1

In this section we prove
Theorem

6.1
and we start with an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 6.16
.

For
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
T>0
, and
m>0
,

\inf_{0\leq t<\sigma_{x,T,m}}(V(X_{t}^{x})-V(x))\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{x})<m}\geq-\alpha T\text{ a.s.}

Proof.

By
Definition

6.4

\inf_{0\leq t<\tau_{x,T}}(V(X_{t}^{x})-V(x))\geq-\alpha T\text{ a.s.}

The claim follows since
W(X_{t}^{x})\geq m
for
\tau_{x,T}\leq t<\sigma_{x,T,m}
(see
Definition

6.6
).
∎

Next, we prove
Theorem

6.1
:

Proof.

Let
N
be as in
Definition

6.12
and fix
\delta>0,M>N
.
By
Lemma

6.13
there is
\epsilon\in\big(0,\frac{1}{M}\big)
,

n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}>0
such that for all

(6.10)

z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}:=\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq M\}\cap N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})

(see (
6.6
)) there is
1\leq i(z)\leq n
such that

(6.11)

\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{z,T_{i(z)},M}}^{z})-V(z)]\geq\frac{\alpha T_{i(z)}}{8}\,.

To simplify the notation, we set
\sigma_{z}:=\sigma_{z,T_{i(z)},M}
for

z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
and
\sigma_{z}=1
for
z\notin\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
.

For
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we define an increasing sequence of stopping times
\tau_{n}
as
\tau_{0}=0
,
\tau_{n+1}=\tau_{n}+\sigma_{X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}}
. (To interpret
\tau_{n+1}=\tau_{n}+\sigma_{X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}}
rigorously, we note that there is a measurable function
\psi:\mathbb{D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathcal{M})\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sigma_{z}=\psi(X^{z}_{\cdot})
for all
z\in\mathcal{M}
, and we set
\tau_{n+1}=\tau_{n}+\psi(X^{y}_{\tau_{n}+\cdot})
.) It follows from the strong Markov property for
X_{t}^{y}
(
Lemma

5.2
) that the discrete-time process
Y_{n}^{y}\coloneqq X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}
is a Markov chain on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
with respect to the filtration
\mathcal{G}_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}}
.

Next, we claim that
\tau_{n}\to\infty
a.s. as
n\to\infty
. Indeed, let
T^{\ast}
be the random variable such that
\tau_{n}\uparrow T^{\ast}
. Suppose
T^{\ast}<\infty
with positive probability, and we restrict to such event. To simplify the notation, we set
\tau_{z}\coloneqq\tau_{z,T_{i(z)}}
for

z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
and
\tau_{z}=1
for
z\notin\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
. Then
\sigma_{Y^{y}_{n}}=\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n}\to 0
, and since
\sigma_{z}\geq\tau_{z}
(see
Definition

6.6
), we obtain
\tau_{Y^{y}_{n}}\to 0
. Note that a.s.
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{y})ds
is continuous (recall that
t\mapsto X_{t}^{y}
is cadlag a.s.) and
t\mapsto M^{V}_{t}(y)
is cadlag (see
Definition

2.10
), so
t\mapsto V(X_{t}^{y})
is also cadlag. In particular, we have that the limit as
t\uparrow T^{\ast}
of the aforementioned functions exist, so
\Big|\int_{\tau_{n}}^{\tau_{n}+\tau_{Y^{y}_{n}}}H(X_{s}^{y})ds\Big|
and
|V(X_{\tau_{n}+\tau_{Y^{y}_{n}}}^{y})-V(X_{\tau_{n}}^{y})|
both
\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. By
Definition

6.4
, this implies that
\tau_{Y^{y}_{n}}
is eventually equal to
T_{i(Y^{y}_{n})}
, contradicting
\tau_{Y^{y}_{n}}\to 0
, so
T^{\ast}=\infty
a.s.

We also define the discrete-time process

Z_{n}^{y}\coloneqq V(Y_{\tau^{\prime}\wedge n}^{y})+b(n-\tau^{\prime})_{+}-V(y)\,,

where
b\coloneqq\frac{\alpha}{8}\min_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}
,

a_{+}:=a\vee 0

for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\tau^{\prime}:=\tau^{\prime}_{y}\coloneqq\inf\{n\geq 0\mid Y_{n}^{y}\notin\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}\}\,,

which is a
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-stopping time.
Next, we show that
Z_{n}^{y}
satisfies the assumptions of
Corollary

4.12
. By (
6.11
),

(6.12)

\inf_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}}\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{z}}^{z})-V(z)]\geq b>0

and by
Lemma

6.15
, there is some
B>0
such that

(6.13)

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}}\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{z}}^{z})-V(z))^{2}]\leq B.

Furthermore, by the definition of
Z_{n}
,

(6.14)

Z_{n}^{y}-Z_{n-1}^{y}=(V(Y_{n}^{y})-V(Y_{n-1}^{y}))\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}+b\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}\leq n-1}\,.

Let
f(y)=\mathbb{E}[(V(Y_{1}^{y})-V(y))^{2}]
. Using (
6.14
), recalling that
\tau^{\prime}
is a stopping time with respect to
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, and using the Markov property of
Y_{n}^{y}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}^{y}-Z_{n-1}^{y})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]=\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}\leq n-1}b^{2}+\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}f(Y_{n-1}^{y})\,.

On the event
\tau^{\prime}>n-1
,
Y_{n-1}^{y}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
and (
6.13
) implies

\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}f(Y_{n-1}^{y})\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}\sup_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}}\mathbb{E}[(V(X_{\sigma_{z}}^{z})-V(z))^{2}]\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}B\,.

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}[(Z_{n}^{y}-Z_{n-1}^{y})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]\leq b^{2}+B.

Similarly, by (
6.12
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}^{y}-Z_{n-1}^{y}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}\leq n-1}b+\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}\mathbb{E}[V(Y_{n}^{y})-V(Y_{n-1}^{y})\mid\mathcal{G}_{n-1}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}\leq n-1}b+\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{\prime}>n-1}\inf_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}}\mathbb{E}[V(X_{\sigma_{z}}^{z})-V(z)]

\displaystyle\geq b\,.

Then by
Corollary

4.12
, there is some
C>0
such that
for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}

(6.15)

\mathbb{P}\Big(Z_{n}^{y}\geq\frac{b}{2}n-C\text{ for all }n\geq 0\Big)>1-\delta\,,

where we stress that
C
is independent of
y
.
Also, by
Assumption

4

(i)
, there is
A>0
such that

(6.16)

V(x)\geq A\qquad\textrm{implies }\quad x\in N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})\,.

We set
D\coloneqq A+C+\alpha\max_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}
and fix
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V>D\}\cap\{W\leq M\}
. Denote
\Omega^{*}
the event that
Z_{n}^{y}\geq\frac{b}{2}n-C
for all
n\geq 0
. Then (
6.15
) implies that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{*})>1-\delta
, so we conclude the proof by verifying the following claims:

(6.17)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty,W(X_{t}^{y})\leq M}V(X_{t}^{y})=\infty\textrm{ a.s. on }\Omega^{*}

(6.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}\leq\frac{1}{M}\textrm{ a.s. on }\Omega^{*}

First, we note that by
Lemma

6.7
and the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}
in (
6.10
) we have that

\tau^{\prime}=\inf\{n\geq 0\mid Y_{n}^{y}\notin N_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{0})\}\,,

so by (
6.16
) we obtain
V(Y_{\tau^{\prime}}^{y})<A
a.s. on the event
\{\tau^{\prime}<\infty\}
. In particular, on the event
\{\tau^{\prime}<\infty\}
we have a.s. that

Z_{\tau^{\prime}}^{y}=V(Y_{\tau^{\prime}}^{y})-V(y)\leq A-D=-C-\alpha\max_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}<-C\,.

Since
Z_{n}^{y}\geq\frac{b}{2}n-C\geq-C
for all
n\geq 0
on
\Omega^{*}
,

(6.19)

\tau^{\prime}=\infty\quad{and}\quad Z_{n}^{y}=V(Y_{n}^{y})-V(y)\textrm{ for all }n\geq 0\text{ a.s. on the event }\Omega^{*}\,.

Consequently, on
\Omega^{*}

(6.20)

V(Y_{n}^{y})=Z_{n}^{y}+V(y)>\frac{b}{2}n-C+D=\frac{b}{2}n+A+\alpha\max_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}

so that with
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{W\leq M\}\cap\{V>A+\alpha\max_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}\}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,M}

we have

(6.21)

Y_{n}^{y}\in\mathcal{U}\textrm{ for all }n\geq 0\text{ a.s. on the event }\Omega^{*}\,.

By passing
n\to\infty
in (
6.20
), noting that
W(Y_{n}^{y})\leq M
by (
6.21
), and recalling that
\tau_{n}\to\infty
a.s., we obtain (
6.17
).

By
Lemma

6.16
, if
z\in\mathcal{U}
, then
almost surely

\inf_{0\leq t<\sigma_{z}}(V(X_{t}^{z})-A)\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{z})<M}\geq\inf_{0\leq t<\sigma_{z}}(V(X_{t}^{z})-V(z)+\alpha\max_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{z})<M}\geq 0

so that by (
6.16
) we have

\sup_{0\leq t<\sigma_{z}}d(X_{t}^{z},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{z})<M}\leq\epsilon<\frac{1}{M}\text{ a.s.}

In other words, defining
\tilde{\phi}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]
by

\tilde{\phi}(z)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{0\leq t<\sigma_{z}}d(X_{t}^{z},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{z})<M}<\frac{1}{M}\Big)\,,

we have
\tilde{\phi}(z)=1
for all
z\in\mathcal{U}
. Recall that
Y_{n}^{y}=X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}
, so applying the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{\tau_{n}\leq t\leq\tau_{n+1}}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}<\frac{1}{M}\text{ and }Y_{n}^{y}\in\mathcal{U}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\phi}(Y_{n}^{y})\mathbbm{1}_{Y_{n}^{y}\in\mathcal{U}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}^{y}\in\mathcal{U})\,,

or in other words

(6.22)

Y_{n}^{y}\in\mathcal{U}\qquad\textrm{implies }\quad\sup_{\tau_{n}\leq t\leq\tau_{n+1}}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}<\frac{1}{M}\text{ a.s.}

Thus, (
6.18
) follows from (
6.21
) and (
6.22
) by recalling that
\tau_{n}\to\infty
a.s. and writing

\sup_{t\geq 0}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}=\sup_{n\geq 0}\sup_{\tau_{n}\leq t\leq\tau_{n+1}}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}\,.

∎

6.5.
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

In this section we provide the proof of
Theorem

3.2
:

Proof.

Since by enlarging
M
we make the statement stronger, we can without loss of generality assume
M>N
, where
N
is as in
Theorem

6.1
.
By
Theorem

6.1
, for all
n\geq 1
there is a
D_{n}>0
such that for all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D_{n}\}\cap\{W\leq M+n\}
,

(6.23)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M+n}\leq\frac{1}{M+n}\\
\text{ and }\limsup_{t\to\infty,W(X_{t}^{y})\leq M+n}V(X_{t}^{y})=\infty\Big)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{n},

and we may without loss of generality assume that
D_{n}\uparrow\infty
. Fix
y\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D_{1}\}\cap\{W\leq M\}
and define

\tau_{n}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\mid V(X_{t}^{y})>D_{n+1},W(X_{t}^{y})<M+2\},

and we claim that a.s.
\tau_{n}\uparrow\infty
. Indeed,
by
Definition

2.10

t\mapsto M^{V}_{t}(y)
is cadlag and so by the continuity of
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}H(X_{s}^{y})ds
it follows that
t\mapsto V(X_{t}^{y})
is cadlag. However, on the event
\tau_{n}\to T^{*}<\infty
, have
V(X_{\tau_{n}}^{y})\geq D_{n+1}\to\infty
, and therefore such event has zero probability.

Next, let

A_{n}=\{\tau_{n}<\infty\}\cap\Big\{\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M+n+1}\leq\frac{1}{M+n+1}\Big\},

a decreasing sequence of events. Define
\tilde{\phi}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to[0,\infty)
by

\tilde{\phi}(z)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{z},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{z})<M+n+1}\leq\frac{1}{M+n+1}\Big)

and by (
6.23
),
\tilde{\phi}(z)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{n+1}
for
z\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D_{n+1}\}\cap\{W\leq M+n+1\}
. In particular, by the definition of
\tau_{n}
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\tau_{n}<\infty}\tilde{\phi}(X_{\tau_{n}}^{y})\geq\mathbbm{1}_{\tau_{n}<\infty}\Big(1-\frac{\delta}{n+1}\Big)\,,

where we used that
X_{\tau_{n}}^{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V\geq D_{n+1}\}\cap\{W\leq M+2\}
a.s. by the right continuity of
t\mapsto X_{t}^{y}
and the invariance of
\mathcal{M}_{+}
(see
Assumption

1
). By the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
),

\mathbb{P}(A_{n})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\tau_{n}<\infty}\tilde{\phi}(X_{\tau_{n}}^{y})]\geq\Big(1-\frac{\delta}{n+1}\Big)\mathbb{P}(\tau_{n}<\infty).

Since
\tau_{n}<\infty
on the event that
\limsup_{t\to\infty,W(X_{t}^{y})\leq M+1}V(X_{t}^{y})=\infty
, then for each
n\geq 1
we have by (
6.23
) that

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{n}<\infty)\geq 1-\delta\,.

Thus,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}A_{n}\Big)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\geq 1-\delta.

Since

\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}A_{n}\subset\big\{\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{t\to\infty}d(X_{t}^{y},\mathcal{M}_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{W(X_{t}^{y})<M}=0\big\},

by
Corollary

4.10

\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)\geq 1-\delta\,,

proving the claim.
∎

7.
Global Extinction

In this section we prove technical facts related to accessibility (see
Definition

3.4
) and give a proof of
Theorem

3.5
. In the entire section we fix a Markov quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
satisfying
Assumption

1
–
5
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
open the following are equivalent:

(1)

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})dt>0
.

(2)

There exists
t\geq 0
such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

(3)

\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq 0\text{ such that }X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

Proof.

The statements (1) implies (2) and (2) implies (3) are straightforward. To prove that (3) implies (1), denote
f(t)=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{U}}(X_{t}^{x})
so that (3) is equivalent to
\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq 0\text{ such that }f(t)>0)>0
. By the right continuity of
t\mapsto X_{t}^{x}
and openness of
\mathcal{U}
, this is equivalent to the existence of random
t_{2}>t_{1}\geq 0
such that
f(t)>0
for
t\in[t_{1},t_{2})
. In particular, the condition (3) implies
\mathbb{P}(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}f(t)dt>0)>0
, which by
f(t)\geq 0
, Tonelli’s theorem, and
\mathbb{E}[f(t)]=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})
is equivalent to (1).
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose every point
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
satisfies (
3.1
).
Then there is some
N>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+},D>0
it holds that
\{V>D\}\cap\{W<N\}
is accessible from
x
.

Proof.

Let
N
be as in
Lemma

6.8
and fix
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Let
M
satisfy (
3.1
) and let
D>0
be arbitrary. Set
T=3M
and choose any
y\in\{W\leq M\}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Using the notation of
Lemma

6.8
we have

\mathbb{P}(\eta_{N}(y)>T)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\eta_{N}(y)]}{T}\leq\frac{W(y)}{T}\leq\frac{1}{3}\,.

With
M^{V}_{t}(y)
as defined in (
2.1
) and
H
as in
Assumption

4
so that
H
agrees with
\mathcal{L}V
on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we have by Doob’s inequality,
Corollary

4.2
, and (
4.4
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\leq T}|V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)|]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\leq T}|M^{V}_{t}(y)|\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}|H(X_{s}^{y})|ds\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 1+4\mathbb{E}\Big[|M^{V}_{T}(y)|^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}|H(X_{s}^{y})|ds\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 1+4K(U(y)+KT)+bW(y)+(A+bK)T

\displaystyle\leq C\,,

where
A,b
are as in the proof of
Corollary

4.3
and
C>0
is a constant depending only on
M
. Thus, for any
y\in\{W\leq M\}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
we have by Chebyshev inequality that

\mathbb{P}(\exists t\leq T\text{ such that }|V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)|\leq 3C\text{ and }W(X_{t}^{y})\leq N)\\
\geq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t\leq T}|V(X_{t}^{y})-V(y)|\leq 3C\text{ and }\eta_{N}(y)\leq T)\geq\frac{1}{3}\,.

Consequently, the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
), applied to the stopping time
\tau=\inf\{t:V(X_{t}^{x})>D+3C\text{ and }W(X_{t}^{x})<M\}
implies

\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq 0\text{ such that }V(X_{t}^{x})>D\text{ and }W(X_{t}^{x})<N+1)\\
\geq\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{P}(\exists t\geq 0\text{ such that }V(X_{t}^{x})>D+3C\text{ and }W(X_{t}^{x})<M)\,,

which is strictly positive by (
3.1
) (using condition 3 of
Definition

3.4
).
Since
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+},D>0
were arbitrary, the claim is proven.
∎

Now we prove
Theorem

3.5
:

Proof.

Let
N
be as in
Lemma

7.2
and fix
x\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. By
Theorem

3.2
, there is
D>0
such that for all
y\in\Psi_{D,N}:=\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\{V>D\}\cap\{W<N\}
we have

(7.1)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{y})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big)\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

Let
\tau_{0}^{\prime}=0
,
\tau_{n}^{\prime}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{n-1}^{\prime}+1\mid X^{x}_{t}\in\Psi_{D,N}\}
, and
\Omega^{*}\coloneqq\Big\{\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big\}
. Then the strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
) and (
7.1
) imply that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}^{\prime}})\geq 1/2
on the event
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}<\infty\}
, where
n\geq 1
. By Levy’s 0-1 law,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}^{\prime}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega^{*}}
a.s. Thus, (up to a set of measure zero) if
\omega\in\Omega
is such that
\tau_{n}^{\prime}(\omega)<\infty
for all
n\geq 1
, then
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}^{\prime}})(\omega)\geq 1/2
for all
n\geq 1
, which implies that
\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega^{*}}(\omega)=1
. To summarize, almost surely we have that

(7.2)

\{X_{t}^{x}\in\Psi_{D,N}\text{ i.o.}\}\subset\Big\{\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{t}^{x})}{t}\geq\alpha\Big\}=\Omega^{*}\,,

where by “i.o.” we mean there are times
t_{n}\uparrow\infty
such that
X^{x}_{t_{n}}\in\Psi_{D,N}
.
Thus, it suffices to show that almost surely

(7.3)

(\Omega^{*})^{c}\subset\{X_{t}^{x}\in\Psi_{D,N}\text{ i.o.}\}\,,

which combined with (
7.2
) implies
\mathbb{P}((\Omega^{*})^{c})=\mathbb{P}((\Omega^{*})^{c}\cap\Omega^{*})=0
.

In the rest of the proof we show (
7.3
). Let
K_{n}
be as in
Remark

2.14
applied to
A=\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Let
\tau_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}
, where
\sigma_{i}
are iid exponentially distributed (with parameter 1) random variables independent of
X_{t}^{x}
, and
Y_{n}\coloneqq X_{\tau_{n}}^{x}
. We claim that almost surely

(7.4)

(\Omega^{*})^{c}\subset\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\{Y_{n}\in K_{m}\text{ i.o.}\}\,.

By
Lemma

4.9
and
Corollary

4.8
, almost surely on
(\Omega^{*})^{c}
we may assume there are
t_{n}\to\infty,\mu\in P(\mathcal{M})
such that
\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}\to\mu
and
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{0})<1
(where
\mu_{t}^{x}
is as in
Definition

4.4
). Then, by Prohorov’s Theorem and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{0}
, it follows that there is some
m
such that
\inf_{n}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(K_{m})>0
. With the notation
B=\{t\in[0,\infty)\mid X_{t}^{x}\in K_{m}\}
, the previous statement implies that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(t)\,dt=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{t_{n}}^{x}(K_{m})>0\,.

In particular,
m(B)=\infty
, where
m(\cdot)
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\infty)
. By the definition of Poisson process we have for any
t>0
that
|\{n:\tau_{n}\in B\cap[0,t]\}|
is a Poisson random variable with parameter
m(B\cap[0,t])
, where
|S|
denotes the cardinality of the set
S
. Thus, for any
N>0
we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[|\{n:\tau_{n}\in B\cap[0,t]\}|\leq N]=0
. It follows that
\mathbb{P}(|\{n:\tau_{n}\in B\}|=\infty)=1
, and since
\tau_{n}\in B
is equivalent to
Y_{n}\in K_{m}
, this shows (
7.4
) (
Y_{n}\in K_{m}
i.o.)

Finally, we prove that (
7.4
) implies (
7.3
).
Fix
m
and note that by Fatou’s Lemma,
Assumption

2
, and Portmanteau’s Theorem, the function
F:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

F(y)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathbb{P}(V(X_{t}^{y})>D\text{ and }W(X_{t}^{y})<N)\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{y}\in\Psi_{D,N})\,dt

is lower semicontinuous, where the equality follows from the invariance of
\mathcal{M}_{+}
(see
Assumption

1
). By
Lemma

7.2
(using condition (1) of
Definition

3.4
), we have
F(y)>0

for all
y\in K_{m}
and thus
\inf_{y\in K_{m}}F(y)>0
. By strong Markov property (
Lemma

5.2
) and exponential distribution of
\sigma_{n+1}
(see definition of
\tau_{n}
), it follows that for each
n

\mathbb{P}(Y_{n+1}\in\Psi_{D,N}|Y_{n})=F(Y_{n})

and so, similarly to the above, we have by Levy’s 0-1 law that almost surely

\{Y_{n}\in K_{m}\text{ i.o.}\}\subset\{Y_{n}\in\Psi_{D,N}\text{ i.o.}\}\,,

which by (
7.4
) shows (
7.3
), completing the proof.
∎

8.
Generator Details

In this section, we first expand upon the exposition given in
Section

2.2
regarding the generator
\mathcal{L}
, the Carre Du Champ
\Gamma
, and the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
. Then we show that if an unbounded function can be approximated in a certain sense by functions in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
), then it lies in the extended domain
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
). Finally, under general assumptions on
\mathcal{M}
and the action of the generator
\mathcal{L}
on the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
, we show that any smooth enough function that does not grow too quickly belongs to the extended domain. The proofs are mostly routine and can be skipped on the first reading.

8.1.
Generator for Bounded Functions

In this subsection we fix a Feller process
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
(see
Assumption

2
) on a locally compact Polish space
\mathcal{M}
with the Markov semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{s}
(see
•
‣
Section

2
). Recall
Definition

2.7
and note that since
f\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{t}f
is linear, it is standard to check that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
is a subspace of
C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\to C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
is a linear map.

Lemma 8.1
.

For
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
and
t\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{t}f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathcal{L}f
.

Proof.

By
Definition

2.7

(i)
, the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{t}
(see
•
‣
Section

2
),
Definition

2.7

(iii)
and the Dominated convergence theorem, we have for any
t\geq 0
that

\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{t}f-\mathcal{P}_{t}f}{s}=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\mathcal{P}_{t}\Big(\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big)=\mathcal{P}_{t}\Big(\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big)=\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathcal{L}f,

where all limits are pointwise. Thus,
Definition

2.7

(i)
holds with
f
replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
.
Next, since
\mathcal{L}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
by
Definition

2.7

(ii)
, then
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathcal{L}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
by
Assumption

2
and so
Definition

2.7

(ii)
holds with
f
replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
. Finally, since
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is a contraction (
\|\mathcal{P}_{t}f\|\leq\|f\|
), then
Definition

2.7

(iii)
implies

\sup_{s>0}\Big\|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{t}f-\mathcal{P}_{t}f}{s}\Big\|=\sup_{s>0}\Big\|\mathcal{P}_{t}\Big(\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big)\Big\|\leq\sup_{s>0}\Big\|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big\|<\infty

and
Definition

2.7

(iii)
with
f
replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{t}f
follows.
∎

Before proceeding we need a preliminary lemma, called quasi-left continuity, which allows us to deduce that
t\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
is continuous in
t
(we only assumed it was right-continuous in
Assumption

2
):

Lemma 8.2
.

If
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
\tau
is a predictable stopping time, meaning there are stopping times
\tau_{n}
such that
\tau_{n}\uparrow\tau
and
\tau_{n}<\tau
almost surely on
\{\tau>0\}
for all
n
, then almost surely on
\{\tau<\infty\}
we have
X_{\tau}^{x}=X_{\tau-}^{x}\coloneqq\lim_{t\uparrow\tau}X_{t}^{x}
. In particular, if
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
then
t\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
is continuous in
t
.

Proof.

We follow
[
Kal21
, Proposition 17.29]
. By
[
Kal21
, Lemma 10.1v]
,
\tau\wedge n
is predictable for all
n>0
, and since

\{\tau<\infty\}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\{\tau\leq n\}
we may without loss of generality assume
\tau
is finite. Since
\mathcal{M}
is Polish, there is a countable collection of bounded continuous functions which separate the points, so it is enough to show that for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mathbb{E}[(f(X_{\tau-}^{x})-f(X_{\tau}^{x}))^{2}]=0
.
Since
f
is continuous and
X_{t}^{x}
is cadlag, then

(f(X_{\tau-}^{x})-f(X_{\tau}^{x}))^{2}=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}(f(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})-f(X_{\tau_{n}+h}^{x}))^{2}\text{ a.s.}

Thus, (
5.1
) and the Dominated Convergence Theorem combined with
Assumption

2
imply

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(f(X_{\tau-}^{x})

\displaystyle-f(X_{\tau}^{x}))^{2}]=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[(f(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})-f(X_{\tau_{n}+h}^{x}))^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}}]\big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})+P_{h}f^{2}(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})-2f(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})P_{h}f(X_{\tau_{n}}^{x})]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(X_{\tau-}^{x})+P_{h}f^{2}(X_{\tau-}^{x})-2f(X_{\tau-}^{x})P_{h}f(X_{\tau-}^{x})]

\displaystyle=0\,,

as desired. The remaining claim follows by taking
\tau=t
and applying Dominated Convergence Theorem to deduce left-continuity of
t\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
, and then noting that we assumed right-continuity in
Assumption

2
.
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

For all
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
,
x\in\mathcal{M}
the process
M_{t}^{f}(x)
given by (
2.1
) is a martingale.

Proof.

For any
t\geq 0
,
M_{t}^{f}(x)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
adapted, and
in addition
M_{t}^{f}(x)
is integrable (
\mathbb{E}[|M_{t}^{f}(x)|]<\infty
) since
f,\mathcal{L}f\in C_{b}(M)
. By Fubini’s theorem for conditional expectation and the Markov property, for
t,s\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[M_{t+s}^{f}(x)-M_{s}^{f}(x)\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X^{x}_{t+s})-f(X_{s}^{x})-\int_{s}^{t+s}\mathcal{L}f(X_{a}^{x})da\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big].

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{t}f(X_{s}^{x})-f(X_{s}^{x})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{a}\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})da.

Thus, to show that
M_{t}^{f}(x)
is a martingale it suffices to show that
\mathcal{P}_{t}f=f+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{L}fds
for all
t>0
. By
Lemma

8.1
, for fixed
x\in M
the function
g(t)=\mathcal{P}_{t}f(x)
has right derivative equal to
t\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathcal{L}f(x)
, and both
g
and its right derivative are continuous by
Lemma

8.2
. Thus, by
[
Bru78
, Theorem 1.3]

g
is differentiable and
g^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathcal{L}f(x)
. By fundamental theorem of calculus,
g(t)=g(0)+\int_{0}^{t}g^{\prime}(s)ds
, which proves the claim.
∎

Next, we focus on the quadratic variation of
M_{t}^{f}(x)
defined as follows:

Definition 8.4
.

Given a square integrable martingale
M_{t}
, we say that
\langle M\rangle_{t}
is the (predictable) quadratic variation of
M_{t}
if
\langle M\rangle_{t}
is the unique integrable, predictable, increasing process such that
M_{t}^{2}-\langle M\rangle_{t}
is a martingale. The existence and uniqueness of
\langle M\rangle_{t}
is guaranteed by Doob-Meyer decomposition theorem (see
[
Kal21
, Theorem 10.5]
).

Remark 8.5
.

Note that for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Definition

2.7
) the martingale
M_{t}^{f}(x)
defined in (
2.1
) is bounded and thus square integrable, so
Definition

8.4
applies.

We also use the following definition.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
be the set of all
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
such that
f^{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
. For
f\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, let
\Gamma f\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(f^{2})-2f\mathcal{L}f
.

Remark 8.7
.

Note that
\Gamma f\geq 0
since
\Gamma f=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f^{2}-(\mathcal{P}_{s}f)^{2}}{s}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f^{2})-2f\mathcal{L}f

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f^{2}-f^{2}}{s}-2f\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f^{2}-2f\mathcal{P}_{s}f+f^{2}}{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f^{2}-(\mathcal{P}_{s}f)^{2}}{s}+\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{(\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f)^{2}}{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f^{2}-(\mathcal{P}_{s}f)^{2}}{s}+0\times(\mathcal{L}f)^{2}\,.

Lemma 8.8
.

For
f\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
,
\langle M^{f}(x)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds
.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
Ben23
, Lemma 9.1]
.
∎

8.2.
Extending the Domain of the Generator

In this subsection we fix a Feller process
\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
(see
Assumption

2
) on a locally compact Polish space
\mathcal{M}
with the Markov semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{s}
(see
•
‣
Section

2
).

In lieu of
Corollary

8.3
and
Lemma

8.8
, we have shown that
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
as defined in
Definition

2.10
and that the two notions of
\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma
agree. Similarly,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\cap\{f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})\mid f\geq 0\}\subset\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
as defined in
Definition

2.9
. Next we show that
\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
are in some sense the closures of
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})\cap\{f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})\mid f\geq 0\}
respectively.

In order to verify
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
, we construct a localizing sequence of stopping times for
M_{t}^{f}(x)
based on
Remark

2.14
. We expand upon a similar proof given in
[
Ben23
, Lemma 9.3]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose
A\subset\mathcal{M}
is an open invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
) with
K_{n}
as in
Remark

2.14
. Then for
any
x\in A
, the sequence

\tau_{n}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\mid X_{t}^{x}\notin K_{n}\}\wedge n

is an increasing sequence of stopping times such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}=\infty
a.s.

Proof.

Since
K_{n}^{c}
is open,
X_{t}^{x}
is cadlag, and the filtration is right-continuous, then

(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a non-decreasing sequence of stopping times (see
[
Kal21
, Lemma 9.6iii]
) and the limit
\tau=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}
is almost surely well defined.

We claim that
\tau_{n}<\tau
almost surely. Indeed, for any
m\geq n\geq 1
denote

\Omega_{n,m}:=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\tau_{n}=\tau<\infty,X_{\tau}\in K_{m}\}\,.

Then on
\Omega_{n,m}
, by right the continuity there is a random
h>0
such that
X_{t}^{x}\in K_{m+1}^{\circ}
for all
\tau\leq t\leq\tau+h
, and since
\tau_{n}=\tau
we have that
X_{t}^{x}\in K_{n}\subset K_{m+1}^{\circ}
for all
t<\tau
. Thus,
X_{t}^{x}\in K_{m+1}
for all
t\leq\tau+h
and so
\infty>\tau\geq\tau_{m+1}\geq\tau+h>\tau
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\infty>\tau>\tau)=0
,
\Omega_{n,m}
has zero probability.

Hence, by the invariance of
A=\cup_{m\geq n}K_{m}
for each
n\geq 1
we have

\{\tau_{n}=\tau<\infty\}\subset\bigcup_{m\geq n}\Omega_{n,m}

and the claim follows.
In particular, we showed that
\tau
is a predictable stopping time.

We finish the proof by showing that
\mathbb{P}(\tau<\infty)=0
. For a contradiction assume that there is
N>1
such that
\mathbb{P}(\tau<N)>0
. On
\{\tau<N\}
, for any
n>N
it holds that
\tau_{n}<n
and thus
X_{\tau_{n}}^{x}\in\overline{K_{n}^{c}}
. Since
K_{n-1}\subset K_{n}^{\circ}
, then
