\overline{K_{n}^{c}}\subset\overline{(K_{n}^{\circ})^{c}}=(K_{n}^{\circ})^{c}\subset K_{n-1}^{c}\,,

and therefore
X_{\tau_{n}}^{x}\in K_{n-1}^{c}
for any
n>N
. By
Lemma

8.2
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{\tau_{n}}^{x}=X_{\tau}^{x}
almost surely on the event
\{\tau<N\}
, and thus
X_{\tau}^{x}\in\cap_{n}K_{n}^{c}=A^{c}
which by
Definition

2.2
has probability
0
, a contradiction to
\mathbb{P}(\tau<N)>0
.
∎

Remark 8.10
.

Note that if
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function and
t\mapsto X_{t}^{x}
is a cadlag process, then
t\mapsto f(X_{t}^{x})
is almost surely cadlag, so all of the martingales defined in this section are cadlag (without any modification).

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
) and assume
f:A\to[0,\infty)
is such that:

(1)

There exists
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
such that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
and
f\geq f_{n}\geq 0
.

(2)

\mathcal{L}f_{n}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
A
to a continuous function from
A
to
\mathbb{R}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{L}f
.

Then for all
x\in A
,
M_{t}\coloneqq M_{t}^{f}(x)
defined in (
2.1
) is a cadlag local martingale with the localizing sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as defined in
Lemma

8.9
. In particular,
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
(see
Definition

2.9
).

Proof.

By
Corollary

8.3
, for any
n\geq 1

(8.1)

M_{t}^{n}\coloneqq f_{n}(X_{t}^{x})-f_{n}(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds

is a martingale. Since for any
m\geq 1

(\mathcal{L}f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly on the compact set
K_{m}
and
X^{x}_{t}\in K_{m}
for any
t<\tau_{m}\leq m
, then a.s.

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\to\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})ds
. Consequently, for any
m\geq 1
we have that
M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}^{n}\to M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}
almost surely as
n\to\infty
.
Thus, to show that
M
is a local martingale it suffices to show that
M_{\tau_{m}}^{n}\to M_{\tau_{m}}
in
L^{1}
, or equivalently that
\{M_{\tau_{m}}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable. By the uniform convergence
\mathcal{L}f_{n}\to\mathcal{L}f
on
K_{m}
again, we obtain that
\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds
is uniformly bounded. Since
f_{n}\leq f
, by (
8.1
) we just need to show that
f(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})
is integrable, which follows from Fatou’s lemma and
M_{t}^{n}
being a martingale:

(8.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f_{n}(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})]

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[f_{n}(x)+\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]<\infty.

∎

We can prove a similar claim for
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
which can be viewed as a generalization of
Lemma

8.8
:

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{M}
be an open invariant set (see
Definition

2.2
) and
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
be such that:

(i)

There exists
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
such that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
and
|f|\geq|f_{n}|
.

(ii)

\mathcal{L}f_{n}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
A
to a continuous function from
A
to
\mathbb{R}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{L}f
.

(iii)

\Gamma f_{n}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
A
to a continuous function from
A
to
[0,\infty)
, which we denote by
\Gamma f
.

(iv)

\Gamma f\leq KU^{\prime}
for some
U,U^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
and
K>0
satisfying
Assumption

5

(i)
,
(ii)
.

Then for all
x\in A
,
M_{t}\coloneqq M_{t}^{f}(x)
from (
2.1
) is a square-integrable cadlag martingale with
\langle M\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds
(see
Definition

8.4
). In particular,
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
(see
Definition

2.10
).

Proof.

Let
(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be as defined in
Lemma

8.9
.
Recall from
Corollary

8.3
and
Lemma

8.8
that
M_{t}^{n}
defined in (
8.1
) is a square-integrable martingale with
\langle M^{n}\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds
, and from the proof of
Lemma

8.11
that
M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}^{n}\to M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}
a.s. as
n\to\infty
. Consequently the Monotone Convergence Theorem,
Lemma

8.9
, Doob’s Inequality, and Fatou’s Lemma yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}M_{s}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\wedge\tau_{m}}M_{s}^{2}]\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}4\mathbb{E}[M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}4\mathbb{E}[(M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}^{n})^{2}]\,,

and then
Lemma

8.8
, Domintated convergence theorem, Monotone convergence theorem,
Lemma

8.9
, and Tonelli’s theorem imply

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}M_{s}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}4\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]

\displaystyle=\liminf_{m\to\infty}4\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]=4\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{s}\Gamma f(x)ds<\infty\,,

where in the last inequality we used our assumption
(iv)
and
Lemma

4.1
.
Thus,
M
is a square-integrable martingale, so to finish the proof we show that

M_{t}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds
is a martingale (see
Definition

8.4
). Since
\Gamma f\geq 0
and (
8.3
) imply

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\int_{0}^{s}\Gamma f(X_{u}^{x})du\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]<\infty\,,

it is enough to establish that
M_{t}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds
is a local martingale. Similarly to the proof of
Lemma

8.11
, we obtain
that that almost surely for any
m\geq 1

(M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}}^{n})^{2}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\to(M_{t\wedge\tau_{m}})^{2}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{m}}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\qquad\textrm{as}\quad n\to\infty\,,

and consequently it suffices to show that
\{(M_{\tau_{m}}^{n})^{2}-\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable. Since

X_{s}^{x}\in K_{m}
for any
s<\tau_{m}
and
\Gamma f_{n}\to\Gamma f
uniformly on

K_{m}
, then
\sup_{n}\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds<\infty
. We finish the proof by showing that
\{(M_{\tau_{m}}^{n})^{2}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable. By QM-AM inequality we have

\displaystyle(M_{\tau_{m}}^{n})^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(f_{n}(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})-f_{n}(x)-\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3\Big[f_{n}(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}+f_{n}(x)^{2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big],

and as above, since
f_{n}\to f
and
\mathcal{L}f_{n}\to\mathcal{L}f
uniformly on
K_{m}
, the last two terms on the right hand side are bounded uniformly in
n
. Finally, since
f_{n}(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}\leq f(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}
it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[f(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}]<\infty
, and this follows similarly to the proof of
Lemma

8.11
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f_{n}(X_{\tau_{m}}^{x})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}3\mathbb{E}\Big[(M^{n}_{\tau_{m}})^{2}+f_{n}(x)^{2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}3\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\Gamma f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]+3f_{n}(x)^{2}+3\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f_{n}(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=3\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\Gamma f(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big]+3f(x)^{2}+3\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{m}}\mathcal{L}f(X_{s}^{x})ds\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle<\infty\,,

as desired.
∎

8.3.
Special Generator

In this subsection we assume that the generator
\mathcal{L}
can be written as
L_{1}+L_{2}
, where
L_{1}
is a second order differential operator representing the “smooth” part of the process and
L_{2}
is the generator for the “jump” part of the process. Below in applications
L_{1}
arises when Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) driven by Brownian motion are involved, whereas
L_{2}
appears in the context of random switching and other jump processes.
Our goal is to show that the assumptions of
Lemma

8.11
and
Lemma

8.12
are satisfied if
L_{1}f,L_{2}f
are well defined and continuous. We being with some preliminary definitions and a technical lemma.

Definition 8.13
.

For a Polish space
\mathcal{M}
, let
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M})
denote the set of all finite positive Borel measures on
\mathcal{M}
. We endow
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M})
with topology of weak convergence, that is,
\mu_{n}\to\mu
if for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mu_{n}f\to\mu f
.

Remark 8.14
.

Note that the Portmanteau theorem is valid for
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M})
as well. Indeed, if
\mu_{n}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mu_{n}\to\mu
weakly, then for
f=1
we have
\mu_{n}(\mathcal{M})\to\mu(\mathcal{M})
. Thus, if
\mu(\mathcal{M})\neq 0
, then
\mu_{n}\to\mu
if and only if
\mu_{n}/\mu_{n}(\mathcal{M})\to\mu/\mu(\mathcal{M})
. Since
\mu_{n}/\mu_{n}(\mathcal{M})
is a probability measure, Portmanteau theorem applies to
\mu_{n}\to\mu
. If
\mu(\mathcal{M})=0
, then the Portmanteau theorem theorem is straightforward to prove since all relevant limits vanish.

Lemma 8.15
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Polish space, let
\mu:\mathcal{M}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M})
and
f:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous maps, and fix
\epsilon>0
. Then the function
x\mapsto\int f(x,y)d\mu_{x}(y)
is continuous if
x\mapsto\int|f(x,y)|^{1+\epsilon}d\mu_{x}(y)
is bounded above by a continuous function.

Proof.

First we show that the function from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M})
given by
x\mapsto\delta_{x}\otimes\mu_{x}
is continuous, where
\delta_{x}
is dirac delta measure and
\otimes
denotes the product of measures. Indeed, for any bounded Lipschitz function
f:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
with the Lipschitz constant
C
and any
x_{n}\to x
we have

\displaystyle|\delta_{x_{n}}\otimes\mu_{x_{n}}(f)-\delta_{x}\otimes\mu_{x}(f)|

\displaystyle=|\mu_{x_{n}}f(x_{n},\cdot)-\mu_{x}f(x,\cdot)|

\displaystyle\leq C\mu_{x_{n}}(\mathcal{M})d(x_{n},x)+|\mu_{x_{n}}f(x,\cdot)-\mu_{x}f(x,\cdot)|\to 0\,,

where in the limit
n\to\infty
we used
Remark

8.14
, continuity of
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
, and
x_{n}\to x
.

Consequently, the function from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R})
given by
x\mapsto f^{*}(\delta_{x}\otimes\mu_{x})
is continuous, where
f^{*}
denotes the pushforward by
f
. Since
a\mapsto a
is a continuous function from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
which vanishes over the proper function
a\mapsto|a|^{1+\epsilon}
, the result follows by the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

4.7
.
∎

Definition 8.16
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a locally compact Polish space,
n\geq 0
, and
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
. We define
\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
to be the set of all continuous functions
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
such that there is an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
with
A\subset\mathcal{U}
such that
f
extends to a continuous function
\tilde{f}
on
\mathcal{U}
which is twice continuously differentiable with respect to
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
(b,x)\in\mathcal{U}
, we denote
\partial_{i}f(b,x)
the partial derivative of
\tilde{f}
with respect to the
i
th coordinate of
b
. We define
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(A)
to be the space of compactly supported functions in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
. If
n=0
, that is
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\{0\}
, we abbreviate
\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
as
\mathcal{C}(A)
, the set of all continuous functions
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
.

For the rest of the section, we fix a Feller quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
(see
Definition

2.6
) such that
\mathcal{M}=B\times\mathcal{X}
where
B
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
and
\mathcal{X}
is a locally compact Polish space. We also fix a continuous function
\mu:B\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathscr{P}(B\times\mathcal{X})
, and we use
\mu_{(b,x)}
to denote
\mu(b,x)
.

Let
\Sigma_{ij},F_{i}:B\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. We define operators
L_{1},L_{2},L,\Gamma
for suitable functions
f
by

\displaystyle L_{1}f(b,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ij}(b,x)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f(b,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{i}(b,x)\partial_{i}f(b,x)

\displaystyle L_{2}f(b,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{A\times\mathcal{X}}f(c,y)-f(b,x)d\mu_{(b,x)}(c,y)

\displaystyle Lf(b,x)

\displaystyle=L_{1}f(b,x)+L_{2}f(b,x)

\displaystyle\Gamma f(b,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Sigma_{ij}(b,x)\partial_{i}f(b,x)\partial_{j}f(b,x)

\displaystyle+\int_{A\times\mathcal{X}}(f(c,y)-f(b,x))^{2}d\mu_{(b,x)}(c,y)\,.

Note that if
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, then
Lf,\Gamma f
are well defined and continuous.

Assumption 6
.

Assume
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
(see
Definition

2.7
) and for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
the equality
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
holds.

Lemma 8.17
.

Suppose the Feller quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
satisfies
Assumption

6
and let
A=\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Then for any
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
:

(1)

If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
,
f\geq 0
, and
L_{2}f
is finite and continuous, then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
with
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
(see
Definition

2.9
).

(2)

If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
,
\Gamma f
is finite and continuous, and
Lemma

8.12

(iv)
holds then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
with
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
and
\Gamma f
as above (see
Definition

2.10
).

Proof.

We only prove the second claim, since the first one follows similarly, using
Lemma

8.11
instead of
Lemma

8.12
.

Let
\mathcal{U},\tilde{f}
be as in
Definition

8.16
. Let
K_{n}\subset\mathcal{U}
,
n\geq 1
be compact such that
K_{n}\subset K_{n+1}^{\circ}
, where the interior is with respect to the subspace topology on
\mathcal{U}
inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
, and
\cup_{n}K_{n}=\mathcal{U}
.

Next, we construct a continuous cut-off function which is smooth in
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Our argument is standard, but since we were not able to locate the exact statement in the literature, we provide details.

For each
n\geq 1
, by Urysohn’s lemma there is
a continuous function
g_{n}:\mathcal{U}\to[0,1]
such that
K_{n}\subset\{g_{n}=1\}
and
\{g_{n}\neq 0\}\subset K_{n+1}
. By convolving
g_{n}(b,x)
with a positive mollifier
\phi_{n}(b)
, we may obtain a continuous function
h_{n}:\mathcal{U}\to[0,1]
such that
h_{n}
is twice continuously differentiable in
b
,
K_{n-1}\subset\{h_{n}=1\}
, and
\{h_{n}\neq 0\}\subset K_{n+2}
. Define
f_{n}=h_{n}\tilde{f}
on
K_{n+2}
and
f_{n}=0
on
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X})\setminus K_{n+2}
, and therefore
f_{n}
is a bounded continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
. Restricting the domain of
f_{n}
to
\mathcal{M}
, we have
\{f_{n}\neq 0\}\subset K_{n+2}\cap\mathcal{M}
.
Since
\tilde{f}(b,x),h_{n}(b,x)
are twice continuously differentiable in
b
, then
f_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
. Thus, by
Assumption

6
,
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}f_{n}=Lf_{n}
. Applying the same argument to
f_{n}^{2}=h_{n}^{2}f^{2}
and noting that
h_{n}^{2}
shares the same properties as
h_{n}
shows that
f_{n}^{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
, and thus
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}f_{n}^{2}=Lf_{n}^{2}
.

Since
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{+}
is closed in
\mathcal{M}
, it is closed in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
. Thus, if
A=\mathcal{M}_{+}
we may without loss of generality assume that
\mathcal{U}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{+}
(otherwise intersect
\mathcal{U}
with the open set
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X})\setminus(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{+})
), or equivalently that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
. This ensures that
|f|\geq|f_{n}|\geq 0
on
\mathcal{M}
, since if
(b,x)\in\mathcal{M}
then either
(b,x)\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}_{+}
in which case
f_{n}(b,x)=h_{n}(b,x)f(b,x)
or
(b,x)\notin\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{M}
in which case
f_{n}=0
. Similarly,
f_{n}\geq 0
on
\mathcal{M}
if
f\geq 0
on
\mathcal{M}
. Also note that
(f_{n}^{+},f_{n}^{-})\to(f^{+},f^{-})
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
since
K_{n-1}\subset\{h_{n}=1\}
, so
f_{n}=f_{n}^{+}-f_{n}^{-}\to f^{+}-f^{-}=f
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
as well. Thus, we have verified
Lemma

8.12

(i)
.

Next, we show that
Lemma

8.12

(ii)
holds true, that is,
\mathcal{L}f_{n}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
A
to
Lf
. Observe that
Lf=L_{1}f+L_{2}f
is continuous since
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
implies that
L_{1}f
is continuous, and
\Gamma f
being continuous with
Lemma

8.15
yields that
L_{2}f
is continuous.
Next, since
L_{1}f_{n}(b,x)=L_{1}f(b,x)
for
(b,x)\in K_{n-1}^{\circ}\cap A
, then
L_{1}f_{n}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
A
to
L_{1}f
. To treat
L_{2}f_{n}
, by the continuity of
L_{2}f
and
\Gamma f
we have that
(b,x)\mapsto\mu_{(b,x)}f^{2}
is continuous. Consequently,
by
Lemma

8.15
the maps
(b,x)\mapsto\mu_{(b,x)}f^{+}
and
(b,x)\mapsto\mu_{(b,x)}f_{n}^{-}
are continuous. Since
(b,x)\mapsto\mu_{(b,x)}f_{n}^{+}
is an increasing sequence of nonnegative continuous functions and by Monotone Convergence Theorem it converges pointwise to the continuous function
\mu_{(b,x)}f^{+}
, by Dini’s Theorem the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
A
. Similarly,
\mu_{(b,x)}f_{n}^{-}\to\mu_{(b,x)}f^{-}
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
. This shows that
L_{2}f_{n}\to L_{2}f
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
and thus
Lf_{n}=\mathcal{L}f_{n}\to Lf
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
.

Applying the same argument to
f_{n}^{2}=h_{n}^{2}f^{2}
shows that
\mathcal{L}f_{n}^{2}\to Lf^{2}
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
. Standard computations show that
\Gamma f=Lf^{2}-2fLf
and thus
\Gamma f_{n}=Lf_{n}^{2}-2f_{n}Lf_{n}\to Lf^{2}-2fLf=\Gamma f
uniformly on compact subsets of
A
, verifying
Lemma

8.12

(iii)
. Thus, by
Lemma

8.12
we conclude that
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
, as desired.
∎

9.
Some Important Classes of Examples

In this section we introduce model examples of Markov processes to which our theory can be applied: switching diffusions, SDEs driven by Brownian motion, and discrete-time Markov chains. In each case, under general assumptions, we provide results that verify some or all of
Assumption

1
–
5
.

9.1.
Switching Diffusions

In this section we consider a class of Markov processes given by solutions to switching diffusions, which are a generalization of SDEs driven by Brownian motion. In particular, we investigate
the model from
[
YZ10
, equations (2.2) and (2.3)]
:

(9.1)

\displaystyle dX^{(x,i)}(t)

\displaystyle=F(X^{(x,i)}(t),\alpha^{(x,i)}(t))dt+\sigma(X^{(x,i)}(t),\alpha^{(x,i)}(t))dw(t)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\alpha^{(x,i)}(t)=j)

\displaystyle=q_{ij}(x)t+o(t)\quad\text{ for }i\neq j

where
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{X}=\{1,\dots,m\}
,
(x,i)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
is the initial condition,
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are locally Lipschitz,
w
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
Brownian motion, and
Q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous, uniformly bounded, and such that
q_{ij}(x)\coloneqq Q(x,i,j)
satisfies
q_{ij}(x)\geq 0
if
i\neq j
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}q_{ij}=0
.

In other words,
\alpha
is a pure jump process on the finite state
\mathcal{X}
with
x
-dependent generator
q(x)
. We define the following operators as in
[
YZ10
, (2,4)]
for suitable functions
f
:

(9.2)

\displaystyle L_{1}f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ij}(x,\alpha)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f(x,\alpha)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{i}(x,\alpha)\partial_{i}f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle L_{2}f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\neq\alpha}q_{\alpha\beta}(x)(f(x,\beta)-f(x,\alpha))

\displaystyle Lf(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=L_{1}f(x,\alpha)+L_{2}f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle\Gamma f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Sigma_{ij}(x,\alpha)\partial_{i}f(x,\alpha)\partial_{j}f(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta\neq\alpha}q_{\alpha\beta}(x)(f(x,\beta)-f(x,\alpha))^{2}\,.

where
\Sigma(x,\alpha)=\sigma(x,\alpha)\sigma(x,\alpha)^{T}
(here
T
denotes the transpose).

Lemma 9.1
.

If there is a proper
W:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to[0,\infty)
which is twice continuously differentiable with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
LW\leq\gamma_{0}W
for some constant
\gamma_{0}>0
, then there is a (unique) solution
(X_{t}^{y},\alpha_{t}^{y})
to (
9.1
) for any initial condition
y=(x,i)
and
(X_{t}^{y},\alpha_{t}^{y})
satisfies
Assumption

2
(the Feller property).

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of
solution
(X_{t}^{y},\alpha_{t}^{y})
to (2.2) and (2.3) for any initial condition
y=(x,i)
follows from
[
YZ10
, Proposition 2.20]
. To show
Assumption

2
, define the truncated process
X_{t}^{N,y}
as in
[
YZ10
, Proposition 2.20]
, and note that
[
YZ10
, Proposition 2.20]
implies that

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leq t<\beta_{N}^{x}}|X_{t}^{N,y}-X_{t}^{y}|>0)=0\,,

where
\beta^{x}_{N}
is the first exit time from a ball of radius
N
.
If we denote
W_{N}\coloneqq\inf_{|x|\geq N,i\in\mathcal{X}}W(x,i)
, then by
[
YZ10
, proof of Theorem 2.7]
, for any
T>0
we have

W(y)e^{\gamma_{0}T}=W(x,i)e^{\gamma_{0}T}\geq W_{N}\mathbb{P}(\beta_{N}^{y}\leq T)\,,

and since
W_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
(
W
is proper) we obtain
\mathbb{P}(\beta_{N}^{y}\leq T)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
(for fixed
T
). Consequently, if
g\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X})
, then the functions
u_{N}(x,\alpha)=\mathbb{E}[g(X_{t}^{N,(x,\alpha)},\alpha_{t}^{(x,\alpha)})]
converge to
u(x,\alpha)=\mathbb{E}[g(X_{t}^{(x,\alpha)},\alpha_{t}^{(x,\alpha)})]
uniformly on compact sets for any
t>0
. By
[
YZ10
, Theorem 2.18]
,
u_{N}
is continuous, and so
u
is also continuous and thus
Assumption

2
holds.
∎

Lemma 9.2
.

Supposed
Assumption

2
holds for (
9.1
). Then if
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
is invariant (see
Definition

2.2
) for
(X_{t}^{y},\alpha_{t}^{y})
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
(see
Definition

8.16
), then

(1)

If
f\geq 0
, then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(A)
and
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
(see
Definition

2.9
).

(2)

If
Lemma

8.12

(iv)
holds then
f\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(A)
(see
Definition

2.10
),
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
, and
\Gamma f
is as above.

Proof.

To prove (1) and (2), by
Lemma

8.17
, it suffices to verify
Assumption

6
since
L_{1}f
and the continuous part of
\Gamma
are clearly continuous for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(A)
and

L_{2}f
and the jump part of
\Gamma f
are continuous for continuous
f
since
q_{ij}
are continuous.
To verify
Assumption

6
, we use the generalized Itô formula from
[
YZ10
, (2.8)]
that imply that
M^{f}_{t}
in (
2.1
) is a martingale with
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
. Then
Assumption

6
follows since
the converse of
Corollary

8.3
is true: if
f,g
are bounded continuous functions such that (
2.1
) with
\mathcal{L}f
replaced with
g
is a martingale for all
x
, then
f
is in the domain of the generator and
\mathcal{L}f=g
(see
Definition

2.7
).
∎

9.2.
SDEs

As a special case of
Section

9
, we suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a singleton set so that (
9.1
) is just a SDE driven by Brownian motion and
L_{2}=0
in (
9.2
). Then we have the following sufficient conditions for verifying the bulk of our assumptions.

Lemma 9.3
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple, where
X_{t}^{x}
is a solution to a SDE driven by Brownian motion ((
9.1
) with
\alpha
constant). Let
V\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
. Then
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
are satisfied if there is a proper map
\bar{U}:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty)
in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
and constants
K,c>0
such that
L\bar{U}\leq K-c\bar{U}
and
|\mathcal{L}V|+\Gamma V\lesssim 2K-\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}+\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}
.

Remark 9.4
.

In what follows,

f\lesssim g
means that there is a constant
C>0
depending only on the parameters of the problem such that
f\leq Cg
.

Remark 9.5
.

Lemma

9.3
also applies in the case where
\mathcal{X}
is not a singleton set but
U
only depends on
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
L_{2}U=0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume
c<1<K
.
We show that

Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
are satisfied for
U=\bar{U}^{1/2}
,

W=\bar{U}^{1/4}
,
U^{\prime}=\frac{c}{8K}\bar{U}^{1/2}\phi
and
W^{\prime}=\frac{c}{16K}\bar{U}^{1/4}\phi
, where

(9.3)

\phi\coloneqq 2K-\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}+\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}=K+\Big(K-\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}\Big)+\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\geq K\,.

Since
\bar{U}\geq 1
and
\bar{U}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, then
U,W\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M})
. By

Lemma

9.2
, we obtain
U\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
(
Assumption

5

(i)
)
and
\mathcal{L}U=LU
.
By calculus and (
9.2
),

\mathcal{L}U=L\bar{U}^{1/2}=\frac{1}{2\bar{U}^{1/2}}L\bar{U}-\frac{1}{8\bar{U}^{3/2}}\Gamma{\bar{U}}=\bar{U}^{1/2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-\frac{1}{8}\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)\,.

Since
\bar{U}
is proper and satisfies
L\bar{U}\leq K-c\bar{U}
, then outside of a compact set we have
\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}\leq-\frac{3c}{4}
, and so outside of the compact set we have
\frac{1}{2}\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}\leq\frac{c}{8K}(\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-2K)
, where we used that for all
0<\gamma<\frac{1}{8}
and
x\leq-\frac{3c}{4}\leq-\frac{2c}{3}
it holds that
\frac{1}{2}x\leq\gamma x-\frac{c}{4}
. Then for large enough
K^{\prime}>\frac{K}{4}
, by
\Gamma\bar{U}\geq 0
and
c\leq 1\leq K
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}U

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime}+\bar{U}^{1/2}\Big(\frac{c}{8K}\Big(\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-2K-\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)+\Big(\frac{c}{8K}-\frac{1}{8}\Big)\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime}-\frac{c}{8K}\bar{U}^{1/2}\phi=K^{\prime}-U^{\prime}\,,

and thus
Assumption

5

(ii)
holds.

Next, we focus on
W
:

LW=L(\bar{U})^{\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{1}{4\bar{U}^{\frac{3}{4}}}L\bar{U}-\frac{3}{32\bar{U}^{\frac{7}{4}}}\Gamma{\bar{U}}=(\bar{U})^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\frac{1}{4}\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-\frac{3}{32}\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)

and by reusing the estimate
\frac{1}{2}\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}\leq\frac{c}{8K}(\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-2K)
(which holds outside of a compact set) we obtain for sufficiently large
K^{\prime\prime}>0
that

(9.4)

\displaystyle LW

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime\prime}+(\bar{U})^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\frac{c}{16K}\Big(\frac{L\bar{U}}{\bar{U}}-2K-\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)+\Big(\frac{c}{16K}-\frac{3}{32}\Big)\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime\prime}-\frac{c}{16K}(\bar{U})^{\frac{1}{4}}\phi=K^{\prime\prime}-W^{\prime}\,,

where in the last inequality we used
\Gamma\bar{U}\geq 0
and
c\leq 1\leq K
.
Furthermore, since
\phi\geq\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}
, by calculus

(9.5)

\Gamma W=\Gamma(\bar{U}^{\frac{1}{4}})=\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{16\bar{U}^{\frac{3}{2}}}=\frac{1}{16}\bar{U}^{1/2}\frac{\Gamma\bar{U}}{\bar{U}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{16}\bar{U}^{1/2}\phi=\frac{1}{16}U^{\prime}.

Thus,
W
satisfies
Lemma

8.12
(iv)
, and therefore
Lemma

9.2
implies
W\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}W=LW
. By (
9.4
) and (
9.5
),

W
satisfies
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5
(iii)
, where we note that
W
and
W^{\prime}
are proper on account of
\bar{U}
being proper and (
9.3
).

Since
\bar{U}\geq 1
, then
\Gamma V\lesssim\phi\lesssim U^{\prime}
. Thus,
V
satisfies
Lemma

8.12
(iv)
, and therefore
Lemma

9.2
implies
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
and
Assumption

5
(iv)
holds true.
Finally,
Assumption

4
(ii)
is a consequence of
|LV|\lesssim\phi\lesssim U^{\prime}\lesssim(W^{\prime})^{2}
.
∎

9.3.
Discrete Time

In this section, we investigate discrete time processes.
In
Section

8.3
we defined
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{X}
, but since our process is discrete, we assume
n=0
, that is
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\{0\}
. In the rest of the section we suppress any dependence on the “continuous” variable
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and simply write
\mathcal{M}
instead of
\mathcal{X}
.

Suppose
\{X_{n}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a discrete-time Markov chain, which means that there is a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\mathbb{P})
and a family of
\mathcal{M}-
valued random variables
\{X_{n}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that:

•

X_{0}^{x}=x
a.s.

•

X^{x}_{\cdot}
is adapted to
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, meaning
X_{n}^{x}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n}
measurable for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

For all bounded measurable functions
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
, the map

\mathcal{M}\ni x\mapsto\mathcal{P}f(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[f(X_{1}^{x})]

is measurable and for any
n\geq 0
we assume (homogeneity)
that

\mathcal{P}f(X_{n}^{x})=\mathbb{E}[f(X_{n+1}^{x})|\mathcal{F}_{n}]\,.

We also define the operators

(9.6)

\displaystyle Lf(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{P}f(x)-f(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(X_{1}^{x})-f(x)]

\displaystyle\Gamma f(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Lf^{2}(x)-2f(x)Lf(x)=\mathbb{E}[(f(X_{1}^{x})-f(x))^{2}]

Again we let
\mathcal{M}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
be closed and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
be open and dense. Let
\{N_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be a Poisson process with rate
1
independent of
X_{n}^{x}
and define
Y_{t}^{x}\coloneqq X_{N_{t}}^{x}
. Then it is standard to show that
Y_{t}^{x}
is a continuous-time Markov process on
\mathcal{M}
with cadlag sample paths and semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}=e^{-t}e^{t\mathcal{P}}
.
Indeed, heuristically we have for any set
A
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}^{x}\in A)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{n}^{x}\in A\cap N_{t}=n)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\mathbb{P}(X_{n}^{x}\in A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}\mathcal{P}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=e^{-t}e^{t\mathcal{P}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}\,.

The following lemma helps us translate between conditions on the discrete-time Markov chain
X^{x}_{n}
and the continuous-time Markov process
Y_{t}^{x}
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\{X_{n}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{Y_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0}
be as above and
L,\Gamma
be as in (
9.6
). Then

(i)

A probability measure
\mu
is invariant for
Y_{t}
if and only if
\mu
is invariant for
X_{t}
. Specifically,

\mu\mathcal{P}f=\mu f
for each
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
if and only if
\mu\mathcal{P}_{t}f=\mu f
for each
t\geq 0
and
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{U}
is accessible from
x
in the sense of
Definition

3.4
applied to
Y_{t}
if and only if one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(a)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-n}\mathbb{P}(X_{n}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

(b)

There exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{n}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

(c)

\mathbb{P}(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }X_{n}^{x}\in\mathcal{U})>0
.

(iii)

If for
A=\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
A=\mathcal{M}_{+}
it holds that for all
x\in A,

\mathcal{P}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=1
, then
Assumption

1
holds for
Y_{t}^{x}
.

(iv)

If
\mathcal{P}f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, then
Assumption

2
holds for
Y_{t}^{x}
.

In particular, if
(iii)
and
(iv)
hold then
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple (see
Definition

2.6
), which is assumed for the remaining items.

(v)

If
f\in C_{b}(\mathcal{M})
, then
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{L}f=Lf
(see
Definition

2.7
).

(vi)

Suppose
\Upsilon:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty)
is proper (see
Definition

2.13
) and
V:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous. Suppose also that
\mathbb{E}[|V(X_{1}^{x})-V(x)|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim\sqrt{\Upsilon(x)}
(see
Remark

9.4
) and
\mathcal{P}\Upsilon\leq\rho^{2}\Upsilon+C^{2}
for some constants
\epsilon>0,\rho\in(0,1),C>0
. Then
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
hold for
Y_{t}^{x}
with
U=\sqrt{\Upsilon},U^{\prime}=(1-\rho)U,W=\Upsilon^{1/4},\text{ and }W^{\prime}=(1-\sqrt{\rho})W
.

(vii)

If
Assumption

3
–
5
hold for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
, then
Theorem

3.2
(and its corollaries
Theorem

3.1
,
Theorem

3.5
, and
Theorem

3.9
) hold for
X_{n}^{x}
(with
t
replaced with
n
).

Proof.

The statements (i)–(iv) are standard, so we omit the proofs.

For
(v)
, fix
x\in\mathcal{M}
and compute

\displaystyle\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f(x)-f(x)}{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{e^{-s}e^{s\mathcal{P}}f(x)-f(x)}{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\frac{se^{-s}(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}s^{n}\frac{\mathcal{P}^{n+1}f(x)}{(n+1)!})-(1-e^{-s})f(x)}{s}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}f(x)-f(x)\,,

where the last equality is justified since
\mathcal{P}
is a contraction and

e^{-s}\Big|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}s^{n}\frac{\mathcal{P}^{n+1}f(x)}{(n+1)!}\Big|\leq e^{-s}\Big(\frac{e^{s}-1-s}{s}\Big)\|f\|\to 0\qquad\textrm{as}\quad s\downarrow 0\,.

Similarly to the above, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{s>0}\Big\|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{s}f-f}{s}\Big\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{s>0}\Big\|e^{-s}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}s^{n}\frac{\mathcal{P}^{n+1}f}{(n+1)!}-\frac{(1-e^{-s})}{s}f\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s>0}\Big|\frac{e^{-s}(e^{s}-1)}{s}\Big|\|f\|+\sup_{s>0}\Big|\frac{(1-e^{-s})}{s}\Big|\|f\|

\displaystyle=2\sup_{s>0}\Big|\frac{(1-e^{-s})}{s}\Big|\|f\|<\infty\,.

Thus,
(v)
is verified, which shows that
Assumption

6
is satisfied with
L_{1}=0
and
\mu_{x}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)
.

To prove
(vi)
we first note that if
\mu_{x}
is the law of
X_{1}^{x}
, then by the Feller property,
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
is continuous. Hence, by
\mathbb{E}[|V(X_{1}^{x})-V(x)|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim U
and
Lemma

8.15
, the function
\Gamma V
defined in (
9.6
) is finite and continuous. In addition, by our assumption in
(vi)

\mathbb{E}[|U(X^{x}_{1})-U(x)|^{2}]\leq 2\mathcal{P}U^{2}(x)+2U^{2}(x)\leq 2\rho^{2}\Upsilon(x)+2C^{2}+2U^{2}(x)

and so the continuity of
x\mapsto LU(x)
follows from (
9.6
), the continuity of
U
and
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
, and
Lemma

8.15
.
Analogously, we establish that
\Gamma W
is continuous. Thus,
Lemma

8.17
implies

U\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{+}(\mathcal{M})
and
V,W\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and consequently
Assumption

3
(i)
and
Assumption

5
(i)
are satisfied. Next, by Jensen’s inequality and
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
we have

\mathcal{P}U=\mathcal{P}\sqrt{\Upsilon}\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{P}\Upsilon}\leq\rho U+C\,,

and therefore

(9.7)

LU=\mathcal{P}U-U\leq(\rho-1)U+C=C-U^{\prime}\,

and
Assumption

5
(ii)
follows. Similarly we obtain
LW\leq(\sqrt{\rho}-1)W+\sqrt{C}
, which proves
Assumption

3
(ii)
. Also, since
W=\sqrt{U}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{P}W\geq 0
, using (
9.7
) we have

\Gamma W=LU-2WLW=LU-2W\mathcal{P}W+2U\leq(\rho+1)U+C\lesssim U

and
Assumption

5
(iii)
holds true. Furthermore, by Jensen’s inequality

|LV|\leq(\mathbb{E}[|V(X_{1}^{x})-V(x)|^{2+\epsilon}])^{\frac{1}{2+\epsilon}}\lesssim(W^{\prime}(x))^{\frac{2}{2+\epsilon}}

and since
\epsilon>0
and
W^{\prime}
is proper,
LV
vanishes over
W^{\prime}
, establishing
Assumption

4
(ii)
. Finally, by Jensen’s inequality and
U\geq 1
we have

\Gamma V\leq(\mathbb{E}[|V(X_{1}^{x})-V(x)|^{2+\epsilon}])^{\frac{2}{2+\epsilon}}\lesssim U^{\frac{2}{2+\epsilon}}\lesssim U\,,

which implies
Assumption

5
(iv)
and the proof of (vi) is finished.

For
(vii)
, recall that
Assumption

1
-
2
are met since
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Y_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple. Then
by the strong law of large numbers we have that almost surely
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t}{N_{t}}=1
and thus (almost surely)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{V(X_{n}^{x})}{n}=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(Y_{t}^{x})}{N_{t}}=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(Y_{t}^{x})}{t}\frac{t}{N_{t}}=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{V(Y_{t}^{x})}{t}\,.

∎

10.
Examples

In this section we illustrate how our theory can be applied to some characteristic examples. We recommend that the reader is familiar with the notation and definitions from
Section

2
and the results in
Section

3
. The first example is a model of an epidemic using a switching diffusion, the second one is a SDE obtained by adding noise to the Lorenz system (a prototypical model for turbulence), the third one is a very general class of discrete-time ecological models, and the fourth is a very general class of continuous-time ecological models based on Stochastic Kolmogorov equations. We use the setup and results from
Section

9.1
for the first example, from
Section

9.2
for the second and fourth examples, and from
Section

9.3
for the third example.

10.1.
Stochastic SIS epidemic model on network with Markovian switching

In this example, analyzed in
[
BO23
]
, the dynamics of
(x(t),s(t))
with initial condition
(x_{0},s_{0})
are given by the following switching diffusion:

\displaystyle dx_{i}

\displaystyle=[\beta(s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})-\delta(s)x_{i}]dt+\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})dW_{i}(t)

\displaystyle b_{i}(x,s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{ij}(s)x_{j}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(s(t)=s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=q_{s_{0}s^{\prime}}t+o(t)\quad\text{ for }s_{0}\neq s^{\prime}\,.

Here, we set
\mathcal{M}=[0,1]^{N}\times S=[0,1]^{N}\times\{1,\dots,m\}
for some
N,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{0\}\times S
, and
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
.
For
x\in[0,1]^{N}
we write
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for the components of
x
and use
(x,s)
to denote an element of
\mathcal{M}
(where
s\in S
). Also,
x_{i}
represents the probability that the
i
th node is infected and
s
represents a different regime/environment. The functions
\beta,\delta:S\to(0,\infty)
represent the infection and recovery rates and the parameters
a_{ij}:S\to\{0,1\}
for
1\leq i,j\leq N
characterize the connections in the network so that the matrix
A(s)
with entries
a_{ij}(s)
is the adjacency matrix of the network when the environment is in state
s
. The intensity of the noise is given by continuously differentiable functions
\sigma_{i}:[0,1]\times S\to[0,\infty)
for
1\leq i\leq N
. We assume that
\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)>0
for
x_{i}\in(0,1)
in order to guarantee that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is accessible, and also that
\sigma_{i}(0,s)=0
so that
x_{i}
remains nonnegative for all times. The driving noise is an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
(W_{1}(t),\dots,W_{N}(t))
.

s(t)
is an irreducible Markov process on
S
which is independent of the Brownian motion and
q_{ss^{\prime}}
denotes the transition rate from state
s\in S
to
s^{\prime}\in S
, meaning that
q_{ss^{\prime}}\geq 0
if
s\neq s^{\prime}
and
\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{m}q_{ss^{\prime}}=0
. Since
s(t)
is irreducible, it has a unique invariant probability measure which we denote as
\rho\in[0,1]^{m}
satisfying
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}q_{ss^{\prime}}=0
and
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}=1
.

In
[
BO23
, Theorem 1 and Remark 1]
it is shown that the solution
(x(t),s(t))
exists and is unique, and furthermore
(x(t),s(t))
remains in
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}
) when the initial condition is in
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{0}
) so that
Assumption

1
is satisfied. Since
\mathcal{M}
is compact, it follows from
Lemma

9.1
that the Feller property (
Assumption

2
) holds. Thus,
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{(x(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple (we suppress the superscript for the initial condition).

In the notation of (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) we have

(10.1)

\displaystyle F_{i}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\beta(s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})-\delta(s)x_{i}

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ij}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]^{2}&\text{if }i=j\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Our strategy is to use
Theorem

3.9
and
Lemma

3.13
to conclude that
Theorem

3.5
applies to the problem. For
(x,s)\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we set
V(x,s)=-\frac{1}{2}\log{\|x\|^{2}}
, where
\|x\|\coloneqq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{2}}
is the Euclidean norm, and observe that
V
satisfies
Assumption

4
(i)
. Since
V
is independent of
s
, from
(
9.2
) and (
10.1
) follows

(10.2)

\displaystyle LV(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]^{2}\frac{-\|x\|^{2}+2x_{i}^{2}}{\|x\|^{4}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\beta(s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})-\delta(s)x_{i}]\frac{-x_{i}}{\|x\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]^{2}\frac{-\|x\|^{2}+2x_{i}^{2}}{\|x\|^{4}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\beta(s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]\frac{-x_{i}}{\|x\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\Gamma V(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]^{2}\Big(\frac{-x_{i}}{\|x\|^{2}}\Big)^{2}\,.

Since
b_{i}(x,s)
is a linear function of
x
, it satisfies
|b_{i}(x,s)|\lesssim\|x\|
and thus
\Gamma V
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. It follows from
Lemma

9.2
that
V\in\mathcal{D}^{ext}_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
with
\mathcal{L}V=LV
and
\Gamma V
as above.
Since

\lim_{x\to 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\beta(s)b_{i}(x,s)(1-x_{i})]\frac{-x_{i}}{\|x\|^{2}}

does not exist (it depends on the direction
x
approaches
0
), we need to enlarge the state space. Let
\pi:S^{N-1}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
denote the polar coordinate map, that is,
\pi(v,r)=rv
for
v\in S^{N-1}\coloneqq\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mid\|v\|=1\}
,
r\in[0,\infty)
. Let
\mathcal{N}=\pi^{-1}([0,1]^{N})\times S
,
\mathcal{N}_{0}=\pi^{-1}(\{0\})\times S
,
\mathcal{N}_{+}=\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}
.

It is routine to show that there exists a Markov process
\{(v(t),r(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{N}
such that
(x(t),s(t))=(\pi(v(t),r(t)),s(t))
, but we provide some details anyway. For
x=\pi(v,r)
we have
r=\|x\|
,
v=\frac{x}{\|x\|}
, and then Itô’s Formula (note that
dx_{i}dx_{j}=0
) gives us

(10.3)

\displaystyle dr

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{x_{i}dx_{i}}{r}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{r-x_{i}^{2}/r}{r^{2}}(dx_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle=r\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\frac{dx_{i}}{r}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-v_{i}^{2})\Big(\frac{dx_{i}}{r}\Big)^{2}\Big]

and

(10.4)

\displaystyle dv_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{i}dr^{-1}+r^{-1}dx_{i}+dx_{i}dr^{-1}

\displaystyle=v_{i}\Big(\frac{-dr}{r}+\Big(\frac{dr}{r}\Big)^{2}\Big)+\frac{dx_{i}}{r}-\frac{dx_{i}}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\,.

Since

\frac{dx_{i}}{r}=[\beta(s)b_{i}(v,s)(1-rv_{i})-\delta(s)v_{i}]dt+\sigma_{i}(rv_{i},s)b_{i}(v,s)(1-rv_{i})dW_{i}(t)

we obtain that (
10.3
) and (
10.4
) define a Markov process
\{(v(t),r(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0}
(again suppressing the superscript for the initial condition) on
\mathcal{N}
.
It is standard to verify that
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{(v(t),r(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple and
\tilde{\pi}\coloneqq(v,r,s)\mapsto(rv,s)
is a quadruple map from
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{(v(t),r(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0})
to
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{(x(t),s(t))\}_{t\geq 0})
(see
Definition

3.7
).

Substituting
x=rv
into the formula for
LV(x,s)
(
10.2
), we define the continuous extension
H:\mathcal{N}\to\mathbb{R}
of
\mathcal{L}V\circ\tilde{\pi}
by

\displaystyle H(v,r,s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\delta(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{i}(rv_{i},s)b_{i}(rv,s)(1-rv_{i})]^{2}\frac{-r^{2}+2v_{i}^{2}r^{2}}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\beta(s)b_{i}(rv,s)(1-rv_{i})]\frac{-rv_{i}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{i}(rv_{i},s)b_{i}(v,s)(1-rv_{i})]^{2}(-1+2v_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\beta(s)b_{i}(v,s)(1-rv_{i})]v_{i}\,,

where the equality is by the linearity of
b
in
x
.
Since
\sigma(0,s)=0
and
b(v,x)=A(s)v
, for
(v,0,s)\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle H(v,0,s)

\displaystyle=\delta(s)-\beta(s)\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}(v,s)v_{i}=\delta(s)-\beta(s)v^{T}A(s)v\,.

Let
\lambda_{1}(s)
denote the largest eigenvalue of
A(s)
. For any
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
, by using
\|v\|=1
, we have

\mu H\geq\mu(\delta(s)-\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s))=\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(\delta(s)-\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s))\,,

where recall that
(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{m})
are components of the unique invariant measure for
s(t)
. Thus,
if
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(\delta(s)-\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s))>0
then
there is a constant
\alpha>0
such that
\mu H\geq\alpha
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
, and so (
3.2
) is satisfied.

Recall that
\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)>0
for
x_{i}\in(0,1)
and
\sigma_{i}
is continuously differentiable in
x_{i}
, and consequently by the Stroock-Varadhan support theorem (see
[
SV72
]
),
(0,s)
is accessible from
(x,s)
in the sense of
Remark

3.6
, and thus (
3.1
) is satisfied.

Furthermore, by
Lemma

3.13
we have that
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4
(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
are satisfied for the Feller quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{X_{t}^{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{M},t\geq 0})
.

Thus,
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

3.5
provide the following result.

Theorem 10.1
.

If
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(-\delta(s)+\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s))<0

then for any initial condition almost surely

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log{\|x\|}}{t}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(-\delta(s)+\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s))<0\,,

and in particular
x\to 0
exponentially fast.

Remark 10.2
.

Observe that
[
BO23
, Theorem 2]
shows the exponential convergence under the stronger assumption that

\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}\Big(-\delta(s)+\beta(s)\lambda_{1}(s)+\frac{M(s)^{2}\lambda_{1}(s)^{2}}{32}\Big)<0\,,

where
M(s)>0
is a global bound on the strength of the noise
\frac{\sigma_{i}(x_{i},s)}{x_{i}}
for
x\in(0,1)^{N}
. Our
Theorem

10.1
shows that the extra term
\frac{M(s)^{2}\lambda_{1}(s)^{2}}{32}
is unnecessary. Heuristically, the term
\frac{M(s)^{2}\lambda_{1}(s)^{2}}{32}
should not be needed, since it depends on the behavior of the process everywhere on
\mathcal{M}
, even at points which are far from
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, which should not be affecting the rate of convergence to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, as long as the accessibility of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
holds true.

Remark 10.3
.

If the adjacency matrix of the network
A(s)=A
is independent of
s
and the network is connected, then using the persistence theorem in
[
Ben23
]
and observing that the only invariant measure of
(v(t),s(t))
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
is the one where
s(t)
is distributed according to
\rho
and
v(t)
is constantly equal to the (unique) positive eigenvector of
A
with norm 1, one obtains that the condition
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(-\delta(s)+\beta(s)\lambda_{1})>0
gives persistence.
In the general case, one could also take
V(x,s)=-\log{\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}}
to obtain that the condition
\sum_{s=1}^{m}\rho_{s}(-\delta(s)+\beta(s)d_{min}(s))>0
gives persistence, where
d_{\min}(s)
is the minimum degree of nodes of the network with adjacency matrix
A(s)
, an improvement over
[
BO23
, Theorem 6]
.

10.2.
Lorenz System

In this example taken from
[
CZH21
]
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid x=y=0\}
(the
z
-axis), and
\mathcal{M}_{+}=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{c}
. The Markov process
(X_{t},Y_{t},Z_{t})
is given by the Lorenz system with constant noise in the
Z
component. Specifically, there are constants
\sigma,\rho,\beta,\hat{\alpha_{0}}>0
and a (one-dimensional) Brownian motion
W_{t}
such that
(X_{t},Y_{t},Z_{t})
satisfies the SDE

\displaystyle dX

\displaystyle=\sigma(Y-X)dt

\displaystyle dY

\displaystyle=[X(\rho-Z)-Y]dt

\displaystyle dZ

\displaystyle=[-\beta Z+XY]dt+\hat{\alpha_{0}}dW

(We use
\hat{\alpha_{0}}
instead of
\hat{\alpha}
in order to avoid confusion with the
\alpha
in
Assumption

4
.)

To consolidate the constants,
[
CZH21
, (2.1)]
makes a linear change of variables
(x_{t},y_{t},z_{t})=(c_{1}X_{\chi t},c_{2}(Y_{\chi t}-X_{\chi t}),z_{*}-c_{3}Z_{\chi t})
, where
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},z_{*},\chi>0
are constants so that the system is rewritten as in
[
CZH21
, (2.2)]
:

\displaystyle dx

\displaystyle=ydt

\displaystyle dy

\displaystyle=[x(z-2)-2y]dt

\displaystyle dz

\displaystyle=-[\gamma(z-z_{*})+x(x+\eta y)]dt+\alpha_{0}dW\,,

where
\gamma,\eta>0
are constants and
\alpha_{0}=c_{4}\hat{\alpha_{0}}
(
c_{4}>0
is also a constant).

The operator
L
in (
9.2
) is given by
[
CZH21
, (2.5a)]
:

(10.5)

L=y\partial_{x}+[x(z-2)-2y]\partial_{y}-[\gamma(z-z_{*})+x(x+\eta y)]\partial_{z}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\,.

As in
[
CZH21
, (4.11)]
, we define

U(x,y,z):=\tilde{U}(X,Y,Z):=\exp(\frac{\beta}{2\hat{\alpha_{0}}^{2}}(X^{2}+Y^{2}+(Z-\sigma-\rho)^{2}))

and we remark that the notation
V_{1},\tilde{V_{1}}
was used instead of
U,\tilde{U}
in

[
CZH21
]
. By straightforward calculations (see
[
CZH21
, (4.14)]
) we have
LU\leq K-c(1+x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})U
for some constants
K,c>0
.

Remark 10.4
.

In fact, using
\tilde{U}
with
\frac{\beta}{2\hat{\alpha_{0}}^{2}}
replaced by a constant
A
shows that for fixed
\sigma,\beta
there are constants
A,K,c,p>0
for which
L\tilde{U}\leq K-c(1+x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})\tilde{U}
as long as
\rho<1
and
\alpha_{0}\leq p
.

By standard arguments
\mathcal{M}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{0}
are invariant, and consequently

Lemma

9.1
with
W=\tilde{U}
implies that

(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{(x_{t},y_{t},z_{t})\}_{t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple (see
Definition

2.1
). In particular,
Assumption

1
–
2
are satisfied.

Also,
[
CZH21
, (2.3)]
introduces a “cylindrical” change of variables via the map
\pi:S^{1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by
\pi(\theta,R,z)=(R\sin\theta,R(\cos\theta-\sin\theta),z)
, where
S^{1}
is viewed here as
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. With
\mathcal{N}=S^{1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{N}_{0}=S^{1}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{+}=\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}
we see that
\pi
is a quadruple map (see
Definition

3.7
) between the Feller quadruples
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{(\theta_{t},R_{t},z_{t})\}_{t\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{(x_{t},y_{t},z_{t})\}_{t\geq 0})
(we suppress superscripts for the initial condition), where the dynamics of
(\theta_{t},R_{t},z_{t})
are governed by the SDE

(10.6)

\displaystyle d\theta

\displaystyle=[1-z\sin^{2}\theta]dt

\displaystyle dR

\displaystyle=R[-1+\frac{z}{2}\sin(2\theta)]dt

\displaystyle dz

\displaystyle=-[\gamma(z-z_{*})+R^{2}\sin\theta(\sin\theta+\eta(\cos\theta-\sin\theta))]dt+\alpha_{0}dW

We define
V:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
by
V(x,y,z)=-\frac{1}{2}\log(x^{2}+(x+y)^{2})=-\log{R}
and
note that such
V
satisfies
Assumption

4
(i)
. Since

LV\circ\pi=-\frac{dR}{R}=1-\frac{z}{2}\sin(2\theta)
and
\Gamma V=0
, then
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
follow from
Lemma

9.3
with our fixed
V
and
\bar{U}=U
. Also,
\mathcal{L}V\circ\pi
extends continuously to the function
H(\theta,R,z)=1-\frac{z}{2}\sin(2\theta)
on
\mathcal{N}
.

Remark 10.5
.

In fact, by
Remark

10.4
and the definitions of
W,W^{\prime},K,U,U^{\prime}
in the proof of
Lemma

9.3
, it follows that
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
are satisfied using the same
W,W^{\prime},K,U,U^{\prime}
(independent of
\alpha_{0}
) as long as
\alpha_{0}
is small enough.

Concerning the accessibility, (
3.1
) is satisfied (see
Remark

3.6
),
by
[
CZH21
, Proposition 3.3]
and the Stroock-Varhadan support theorem
[
SV72
]
.

Remark 10.6
.

It is not hard to show that (
3.1
) is satisfied, even without using
[
CZH21
, Proposition 3.3]
. In the notation of
[
CZH21
, Proposition 3.3]
, we only need
|(x(T),y(T))|<\epsilon
and
|z(T)|\leq M
for some
M,T
that may depend on
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0})
but not on
\epsilon>0
. Finding a control
h
so that such a condition holds is not difficult since we can choose a fixed
z
so that the eigenvalues of

\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
z-2&-2\end{bmatrix}
have negative real parts and then have
h
force
z(t)
to eventually be equal to
z
.

Finally, we focus on the assumption (
3.2
) from
Theorem

3.9
and recall that
H(\theta,R,z)=1-\frac{z}{2}\sin(2\theta)
.
Since for
\alpha_{0}>0
the set
P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
consists of a single measure
\mu_{\alpha_{0}}
(see the discussion preceding
[
CZH21
, Theorem 4.1]
),
the assumption
\mu H\geq\alpha>0
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
is equivalent to

(10.7)

-\lambda_{\alpha_{0}}\coloneqq\int_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}}1-\frac{z}{2}\sin(2\theta)d\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(\theta,z)>0\,.

Note that this is precisely the condition considered in
[
CZH21
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Thus, assuming (
10.7
),
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

3.5
imply

\mathbb{P}\Big(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\log(x_{t}^{2}+(x_{t}+y_{t})^{2})}{t}\leq\lambda_{\alpha_{0}}<0\Big)=1

for any initial condition in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
.

If
\alpha_{0}=0
then all invariant measures
\mu_{0}
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
have
z
constantly equal to
z_{*}
and, analyzing the eigenvalues of
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
z-2&-2\\
\end{bmatrix}
,

\lambda_{0}\coloneqq-\inf_{\mu_{0}\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mu_{0}H=\begin{cases}\sqrt{z_{*}-1}-1&\text{if }z_{*}>1\\
-1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Fixing
R_{t}\equiv 0
and
\alpha_{t}\equiv\alpha_{0}
and noting that the coefficients of (
10.6
) are locally Lipschitz in
(\theta,z,\alpha_{0})
, we may use standard arguments similar to those in
Lemma

9.1
to verify the Feller property (
Assumption

2
) for the Markov process
(\theta_{t},0,z_{t},\alpha_{t})
. Then it follows from
Remark

10.5
that
Theorem

3.11
applies to the Markov Processes
(\theta_{t},0,z_{t})
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
for
\alpha_{0}\in\Theta\coloneqq[0,p]
(where
p
is as in
Remark

10.4
), and we conclude that for
\rho<1
(which implies
z_{*}<2
, see
[
CZH21
, page 5]
) we have
\limsup_{\alpha_{0}\downarrow 0}\lambda_{\alpha_{0}}\leq\lambda_{0}<0
,
and therefore (
10.7
) is satisfied for all sufficiently small
\alpha_{0}
.
In particular we recover the following half of
[
CZH21
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 10.7
.

If
\rho<1
then there is a constant
\alpha_{*}>0
(depending on
\sigma,\beta
) such that if
0\leq\hat{\alpha_{0}}<\alpha_{*}
then, regardless of initial condition,
(x_{t},y_{t})\to 0
exponentially fast almost surely.

Remark 10.8
.

Using the exact same average Lyapunov function
V
, the second half of
[
CZH21
, Theorem 1.1]
(persistence) follows from
[
Ben23
]
,
[
CZH21
, Theorem 3.1]
, and
[
CZH21
, Theorem 5.2]
. This shows that the only the construction of
V_{1}

in
[
CZH21
, Section 4]
is necessary for proving their main theorems. In particular, there is no need to construct
V_{0}
.

10.3.
Discrete Time Ecological Models

In this section we consider a general discrete-time ecological model discussed in
[
HNC21
, (2.1)]

(10.8)

\displaystyle X_{i}(t+1)

\displaystyle=X_{i}(t)F_{i}(Z(t),\xi(t)),\quad i=1,\dots,n

\displaystyle Y(t+1)

\displaystyle=G(Z(t),\xi(t))\,.

Here
\mathcal{M}=[0,\infty)^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{0}}
, where each coordinate of
X=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})\in[0,\infty)^{n}
represents the population of some species and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{0}}
represents auxiliary variables such as “eco-environmental feedbacks, forcing, the structure of each species or other factors”
[
HNC21
]
. The stochasticity is represented by iid random variables
\{\xi(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
taking values in a Polish space
\Xi
which models the state of the environment on the time interval
[t,t+1)
. The functions
F:\mathcal{M}\times\Xi\to(0,\infty)^{n}
and
G:\mathcal{M}\times\Xi\to\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{0}}
are measurable in
(z,\xi)\in\mathcal{M}\times\Xi
and continuous in
z
for every fixed
\xi
.

We also assume the existence of a proper function
\Upsilon:\mathcal{M}\to[1,\infty)
and constants
\epsilon>0,\rho\in(0,1),C>0
such that
\mathcal{P}\Upsilon\leq\rho^{2}\Upsilon+C^{2}
and

(10.9)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[|\log F_{i}(z,\xi(0))|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim\Upsilon(z)^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\Upsilon(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

where the last inequality follows since
\Upsilon\geq 1
.

Remark 10.9
.

Compared to
[
HNC21
]
, we do not need to assume
[
HNC21
, Assumption 2.2]
. Also, the existence of our
\Upsilon
is weaker than
[
HNC21
, Assumption 2.1 A3]
since for any fixed
\gamma>0
,
|\log x|^{4}\lesssim(x\vee\frac{1}{x})^{\gamma}
for all
x>0
.

Remark 10.10
.

By using more delicate arguments in
Lemma

9.6

(vi)
, it suffices to assume
\mathbb{E}[|\log F_{i}(z,\xi(0))|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim\Upsilon(z)^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}
, but we choose
\gamma=\frac{1}{4}
for ease and concreteness. Also, for
Theorem

10.12
it is enough to take
\gamma<1
.

For
I\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}\mid x_{i}=0\quad\forall i\notin I\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}^{I}\mid\exists i\in I\text{ such that }x_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M}^{I}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I}

When
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
we suppress the superscript and write

\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
instead of
\mathcal{M}^{I}
and
\mathcal{M}^{I}_{0}
. Note that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is then the set of states where at least one species is extinct. It is standard to verify (continuity of
F_{i}
and the dominated convergence theorem) that
Lemma

9.6

(iii)
and
(iv)
are satisfied for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}})
so that
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple (see
Definition

2.6
).

Remark 10.11
.

We slightly abuse notation by using notation
\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
also for the continuous-time Markov process
Z_{t}\coloneqq Z(N(t))
, where
N:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{N}
is a Poisson process. We keep this notation for the rest of this section, using
\cdot(t)
for discrete-time Markov chains and
\cdot_{t}\coloneqq\cdot(N(t))
for the continuous-time counterpart.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we consider the functions
V_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
V_{i}(x,y)=-\log{x_{i}}
. For any
(x,y)=z\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
we have

(10.10)

V_{i}(Z(1))-V_{i}(z)=-\log{x_{i}F_{i}(z,\xi(0))}+\log{x_{i}}=-\log{F_{i}(z,\xi(0))}

and then by the definition of
\mathcal{L}
in (
9.6
) and our assumption (
10.9
), it holds that

(10.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}V_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}[\log F_{i}(z,\xi(0))]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|V_{i}(Z(1))-V_{i}(z)|^{2+\epsilon}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\Upsilon(z)^{1/4}

Recall that
F_{i}>0
, and therefore

\log(F_{i}(z,\xi(0)))
is well defined.
Also,
\mathcal{L}V_{i}
extends to the continuous function
H_{i}(z)\coloneqq-\mathbb{E}[\log F_{i}(z,\xi(0))]
on
\mathcal{M}
, where the continuity follows from (
10.9
),
Lemma

8.15
, and
Assumption

2
(the Feller property requires continuity of
z\mapsto\mu^{z}_{Z}
, where
\mu^{z}_{Z}
is the law of
Z_{1}^{z}
). Similarly, we define
\tilde{V}_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to[0,\infty)
by
\tilde{V}_{i}\coloneqq V_{i}\vee 0
. Since
a\mapsto a\vee 0
is 1-Lipschitz, by (
10.11
)

(10.12)

\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{V}_{i}(Z(1))-\tilde{V}_{i}(z)|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim\Upsilon(z)^{1/4}

and, as above,

\mathcal{L}\tilde{V}_{i}
extends to a continuous function
\tilde{H}_{i}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Explicitly, since
\mathcal{L}\tilde{V_{i}}(z)
is given by
-\mathbb{E}[\log F_{i}(z,\xi(0))]+\mathbb{E}[(-\log x_{i}F_{i}(z,\xi(0)))\wedge 0]-\mathbb{E}[(-\log x_{i})\wedge 0]
,
\tilde{H}_{i}(z)
can be defined by formally setting
(-\log 0)\wedge 0=0
, so in particular
\tilde{H}_{i}
agrees with
H_{i}
on
\{x_{i}=0\}
.

Let us first focus on the case
I=\{1,\cdots,n\}
.
If
p_{i}>0
are constants and
V\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}V_{i}
, then by (
10.11
) and
Lemma

9.6

(vi)
we have that
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
hold for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
. In addition,
Assumption

4
(i)
follows from the definition of
V
.

Next, we provide sufficient condition for
Assumption

4
(iii)
to hold.
For ergodic
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M})
,
[
HNC21
, (2.3)]
defines the “expected per-capita growth rate of species i”

(10.13)

r_{i}(\mu)\coloneqq-\mu H_{i}=-\mu\tilde{H}_{i}\,,

which is well defined by
Lemma

2.22
. The second equality follows from
[
HNC21
, Proposition 2.1]
and the fact that
\tilde{H}_{i}
agrees with
H_{i}
on
\{x_{i}=0\}
. The following theorem (
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.4]
) is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

9.6

(vii)
and
Theorem

3.5
:

Theorem 10.12
.

Suppose there exist
p_{i}>0
such that for all ergodic
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0})

\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}r_{i}(\mu)<0\,,

so by
Lemma

2.22

V
satisfies
Assumption

4
(iii)
.
If
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is accessible in the sense of (
3.1
) (see
Lemma

9.6

(ii)
), then there exists an
\alpha>0
such that for all initial conditions
z\in\mathcal{M}_{+}

\mathbb{P}\Big(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log\min_{1\leq i\leq n}X_{i}^{z}(t)}{t}\leq-\alpha\Big)=1\,.

Remark 10.13
.

Note that the definition of accessibility in
[
HNC21
, pages 11-12]
implies (
3.1
) as detailed in
Remark

3.6
.

Next we focus on the case
I\subsetneq\{1,\dots,n\}
and prove the assertions of
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.5]
. Note that as remarked in
[
HNC21
]
, by using
[
HN18
]
it is possible to derive the (nontrivial) corollaries
[
HNC21
, Theorem 2.6, Theorem 2.7]
.

Theorem 10.14
.

Suppose
I\subsetneq\{1,\dots,n\}
is such that

(i)

P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I})
is nonempty and all ergodic measures
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I})
satisfy
\max_{i\notin I}r_{i}(\mu)<0
.

(ii)

For all
\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I})
(not necessarily ergodic),
\max_{i\in I}r_{i}(\nu)>0
.

Then there exists
\alpha_{I}>0
such that for any compact set
\mathcal{K}_{I}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}
,

\lim_{z\to\mathcal{K}_{I},z\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log\max_{i\notin I}X_{i}^{z}(t)}{t}\leq-\alpha_{I}\Big)=1\,.

Remark 10.15
.

Recall that
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}
is the set where the species in
I
all have positive population while all other species are extinct. As explained in
[
HNC21
]
, the assumption
(i)
asserts that when all species in
I
are alive and a potential invading species not in
I
has small population, on average the invading species will die out. The assumption
(ii)
is equivalent to saying that, in the absence of other species, the species in
I
will coexist (persist). Thus, heuristically, if the initial populations are close to
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}
, then with high probability, the species not in
I
disappear exponentially fast.

Proof.

As above, it is standard to verify that
Lemma

9.6

(iii)
and
(iv)
are satisfied for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{I},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}})
, so
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{I},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
is a Feller quadruple.

First note that by
Lemma

2.22
the sets

\{(r_{i}(\nu))_{i\in I}\mid\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I})\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|I|}
and
\{(r_{i}(\nu))_{i\notin I}\mid\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I})\}
are compact and convex. Thus, by Hahn-Banach separation theorem and
(ii)
there are
p_{i}>0
such that

(10.14)

\inf_{\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I})}\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}r_{i}(\nu)>0\,,\qquad\sup_{\nu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{I})}\max_{i\notin I}|r_{i}(\nu)|<\infty\,.

Similarly,
\{(r_{i}(\mu))_{i\notin I}\mid\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I})\}
is compact and so
[
HNC21
, Proposition 2.1]
and
(i)
imply

(10.15)

\sup_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I})}\max_{i\notin I}r_{i}(\mu)<0\,,\qquad r_{i}(\mu)=0\quad\textrm{for any}\quad i\in I,\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I})\,.

Since
\mathcal{M}^{I}=\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}\cup\mathcal{M}^{I}_{0}
, then any
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}^{I})

can be decomposed as a convex combination of invariant measures on
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{I}
and
\mathcal{M}^{I}_{0}
respectively. Thus, by (
10.14
) and (
10.15
)
there is
\hat{p}>0
such that

(10.16)

\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}^{I})}\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}r_{i}(\mu)-\hat{p}\max_{i\notin I}r_{i}(\mu)>0\,.

This motivates the choice of average Lyapunov function

(10.17)

V_{p}\coloneqq-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{V}_{i}-\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\log\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}

for some
1>p>0
to be determined later. Since
\tilde{V}_{i}\geq 0
, then
Assumption

4

(i)
holds and

\limsup_{t\to\infty}-\frac{V_{p}(X_{i}^{z}(t))}{t}\geq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\log\sum_{i\notin I}(X_{i}^{z}(t))^{p}\geq\hat{p}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log\max_{i\notin I}X_{i}^{z}(t)}{t}\,,

so the proof will be finished once we show that
Theorem

3.2
applies.

We will use
Theorem

3.9
with a suitable change of coordinates for the variables
x_{i}
where
i\notin I
. Specifically, we introduce the Feller quadruple
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},

\{(v_{p}(t),r_{p}(t),w_{p}(t),y_{p}(t))\}_{t\geq 0})
and the quadruple map
\pi_{p}
as follows.

Without loss of generality, otherwise relabel the variables, we assume that
I
are the last
|I|
coordinates of
[0,\infty)^{n}
. Let
\mathcal{N}=\bigtriangleup^{n-|I|-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)^{|I|}\times\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{0}}
, where

\bigtriangleup^{n-|I|-1}=\Big\{v\in[0,1]^{n-|I|}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n-|I|}v_{i}=1\Big\}

is a simplex. For
v\in\bigtriangleup^{n-|I|-1}
,
r\in[0,\infty)
,
w\in[0,\infty)^{|I|}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\kappa_{0}}
define
\pi_{p}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{M}
by

\pi_{p}(v,r,w,y)=(x,y)\text{ where }x_{I}=w\text{ and }x_{i}=rv_{i}^{1/p}\text{ for }i\notin I\,,

where
x_{I}
is the element of
[0,\infty)^{|I|}
with coordinates equal to the last
|I|
coordinates of
x
.
With
\mathcal{N}_{0}\coloneqq\pi_{p}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}^{I})=\{r=0\}
and
\mathcal{N}_{+}\coloneqq\pi_{p}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{+})
, we obtain a Feller quadruple
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{(v_{p,t},r_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})\}_{t\geq 0})
, where
(v_{p,t},r_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})
is the Markov process corresponding to the Markov chain
(v_{p}(t),r_{p}(t),w_{p}(t),y_{p}(t))
(see
Remark

10.11
) given by setting
(X(t),Y(t))=\pi_{p}(v_{p}(t),r_{p}(t),w_{p}(t),y_{p}(t))
in (
10.8
):

(10.18)

\displaystyle v_{i}(t+1)

\displaystyle=v_{i}(t)\frac{\tilde{F}_{i}(v(t),r(t),w(t),y(t),\xi(t))^{p}}{\sum_{i\not\in I}\tilde{F}_{i}(v(t),r(t),w(t),y(t),\xi(t))^{p}}\quad i\notin I

\displaystyle r(t+1)

\displaystyle=r(t)\Big(\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}(t)\tilde{F}_{i}(v(t),r(t),w(t),y(t),\xi(t))^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle w(t)

\displaystyle=X_{I}(t)

\displaystyle y(t)

\displaystyle=Y(t)

where we omit the subscript
p
and use
\tilde{F}_{i}
to denote
F_{i}\circ\pi_{p}
.
Then clearly
\pi_{p}
is a quadruple map (see
Definition

3.7
) from
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}_{0},\mathcal{N}_{+},\{(v_{p,t},r_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})\}_{t\geq 0})
to
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{I},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
.

Next we compute
\mathcal{L}V_{p}
in the new and old coordinates, where recall
V_{p}
was defined in (
10.17
). Since we have already discussed
\mathcal{L}\tilde{V}_{i}
above, we provide details for the
\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\log\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}
term. Note that for the initial condition
z=(x,y)=\pi_{p}(\vec{a})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
where
\vec{a}=(v,r,w,y)\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
we have

(10.19)

\displaystyle\log\sum_{i\notin I}X_{i}^{z}(1)^{p}-\log\sum_{i\notin I}X_{i}^{z}(0)^{p}

\displaystyle=\log\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}F_{i}(x,y,\xi(0))^{p}-\log\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}F_{i}(x,y,\xi(0))^{p}}{\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}}

\displaystyle=\log\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}^{p}\tilde{F_{i}}(\vec{a},\xi(0))^{p}\,.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}V_{p}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{H}_{i}(x,y)-\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\mathbb{E}[\log\Big(\frac{\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}F_{i}(x,y,\xi(0))^{p}}{\sum_{i\notin I}x_{i}^{p}}\Big)]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}V_{p}\circ\pi_{p}(\vec{a})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{H}_{i}\circ\pi_{p}(\vec{a})-\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\log\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}(\vec{a},\xi(0))^{p}\Big]

Then for any
v\in\bigtriangleup^{n-|I|-1}
and
0<p<1
, since
|\log|
is quasiconvex,

(10.20)

\Big|\log\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}(\vec{a},\xi(0))^{p}\Big|\leq\max_{i\notin I}|\log\tilde{F}_{i}(\vec{a},\xi(0))^{p}|\leq p\sum_{i\notin I}|\log\tilde{F}_{i}(\vec{a},\xi(0))|\,.

In particular, (
10.9
) and
Lemma

8.15
imply that
\mathcal{L}V_{p}\circ\pi_{p}
extends to a continuous function
H_{p}
on
\mathcal{N}
. By combining the definition of
V_{p}
in (
10.17
) with (
10.19
), (
10.20
), (
10.9
), and (
10.12
) we obtain

\mathbb{E}[|V_{p}(Z(1))-V_{p}(z)|^{2+\epsilon}]\lesssim\Upsilon(z)^{1/4}\,,

and therefore by
Lemma

9.6

(vi)
we have that
Assumption

3
,
Assumption

4

(ii)
, and
Assumption

5
hold for
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{I},\mathcal{M}_{+},\{Z_{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
.

It remains to choose
1>p>0
so that the assumption (
3.2
) in
Theorem

3.9
holds, that is, so that there is a constant
\alpha>0
such that
\mu H_{p}\geq\alpha
for all
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
. By (
10.16
) it suffices to show that

(10.21)

\lim_{p\downarrow 0}\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}^{p}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mu H_{p}\geq\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}^{I})}\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}r_{i}(\mu)-\hat{p}\max_{i\notin I}r_{i}(\mu)\,,

where
P_{inv}^{p}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
is the set of all invariant measures supported on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
for (
10.18
) (see
Lemma

9.6

(i)
).

To show (
10.21
), we will define a function
H_{0}
and a Markov Process which are (in some sense) the limits of
H_{p}
and (
10.18
) as
p\downarrow 0
. Then, we verify (
10.21
) with
\lim_{p\downarrow 0}
removed and
H_{p}
replaced by
H_{0}
. Finally, by
Theorem

3.11
we will conclude (
10.21
). Next we provide details.

In the remainder of the argument we only deal with Markov processes on
\mathcal{N}_{0}=\{r=0\}
. Since
\pi_{p}(v,0,w,y)
,
\tilde{F}_{i}(v,0,w,y,\xi(0))
, and
\tilde{G}(v,0,w,y,\xi(0))
are independent of
v
and
p
, we write
\pi(w,y)
,
\tilde{F}_{i}(w,y,\xi(0))
, and
\tilde{G}(w,y,\xi(0))
instead. Similarly, we write
\tilde{\Upsilon}(w,y)
for
\Upsilon\circ\pi_{p}(v,0,w,y)
. Additionally, we drop
r
and use
(v,w,y)
to denote
(v,0,w,y)\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
.

We define the function
H_{0}
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
by

H_{0}(v,w,y)=-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{H}_{i}\circ\pi(w,y)+\hat{p}\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}H_{i}\circ\pi(w,y)

and the Markov chain
\{(v_{0}(t),w_{0}(t),y_{0}(t))\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
by

(10.22)

\displaystyle v_{0}(t+1)

\displaystyle=v_{0}(t)

\displaystyle w_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=X_{I}(t)

\displaystyle y_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=Y(t)\,.

Overall, we defined a collection of Markov processes
\{\{(v_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})\}_{t\geq 0}\}_{p\in[0,1/2]}
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
corresponding to the Markov chains given by (omitting the subscript
p
)

(10.23)

\displaystyle v_{i}(t+1)

\displaystyle=v_{i}(t)\frac{\tilde{F}_{i}(w(t),y(t),\xi(t))^{p}}{\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}(t)\tilde{F}_{i}(w(t),y(t),\xi(t))^{p}}\quad i\notin I

\displaystyle w_{i-n+|I|}(t+1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{F}_{i}(w(t),y(t),\xi(t))w_{i-n+|I|}(t)\quad i\in I

\displaystyle y(t+1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{G}(w(t),y(t),\xi(t))

and a function
H:[0,1/2]\times\mathcal{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

H(p,v,w,y)=\begin{cases}-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{H}_{i}\circ\pi(w,y)-\frac{\hat{p}}{p}\mathbb{E}[\log\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}(w,y,\xi(0))^{p}]&\text{if }p>0\\
-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\tilde{H}_{i}\circ\pi(w,y)+\hat{p}\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}H_{i}\circ\pi(w,y)&\text{if }p=0\end{cases}

To apply
Theorem

3.11
, we note that
Lemma

9.6

(iv)
implies
Assumption

2
for the Markov process
(p_{t},v_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})
on
[0,1/2]\times\mathcal{N}_{0}
, where
p_{t}\equiv p
(simply look at (
10.23
) and note that the right hand side is continuous in
(p,v,w,y)
). Also, since we have already noted that by
Lemma

9.6

(vi)
that
Z_{t}
satisfies
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
with
U=\sqrt{\Upsilon},U^{\prime}=(1-\rho)U,W=\Upsilon^{1/4},W^{\prime}=(1-\sqrt{\rho})W
, and some
K>0
, it follows easily from
\pi(w_{p,t}^{\vec{b}},y_{p,t}^{\vec{b}})=Z_{t}^{z}
for all initial conditions
\vec{b}=(v,w,y)\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
,
z=\pi(w,y)\in M^{I}
that each
(v_{p,t},w_{p,t},y_{p,t})
satisfies
Assumption

3
and
Assumption

5

(i)
-
(iii)
with
U=\sqrt{\tilde{\Upsilon}},U^{\prime}=(1-\rho)\sqrt{\tilde{\Upsilon}},W=\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4},\text{ and }W^{\prime}=(1-\sqrt{\rho})\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4}
, and
K
the same as for
Z_{t}
. In particular,
W,W^{\prime},K,U,U^{\prime}
are independent of
p
.

To satisfy the rest of the assumptions of
Theorem

3.11
, we claim that
H
is a continuous function which vanishes over
(p,v,w,y)\mapsto\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4}(w,y)
. The continuity on
(0,1/2)\times\mathcal{N}_{0}
follows from (
10.9
), (
10.20
), and
Lemma

8.15
. Also,
H_{0}
is clearly continuous. Thus, for continuity of
H
it suffices to show that
H_{p}\to H_{0}
uniformly on compact sets.

As a preliminary calculation, for fixed
(v,w,y)\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
and
p\in(0,1/2)
, we set
\tilde{F}_{i}:=\tilde{F}_{i}(w,y,\xi(0))
and estimate by the mean value theorem that

(10.24)

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p}}{\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p}}-v_{i}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq p\sup_{0<p*<p}\Big|\frac{v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}\log\tilde{F}_{i}}{\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}}\Big|+\Big|\frac{v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}\log\tilde{F}_{i}}{(\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*})^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq p|\log\tilde{F}_{i}|+p\max_{i\not\in I}|\log\tilde{F}_{i}|\leq 2p\sum_{i\not\in I}|\log\tilde{F}_{i}|

Applying the mean value theorem to
p\mapsto\log\Big(\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p}\Big)
and recalling that
\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}=1
and
H_{i}\circ\pi=-\mathbb{E}[\log\tilde{F_{i}}]
, we obtain

\displaystyle|H_{p}(v,w,y)-H_{0}(v,w,y)|

\displaystyle=\hat{p}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{p}\Big[\log\Big(\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p}\Big)-\log\Big(\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\Big)\Big]-\sum_{i\notin I}v_{i}\log\tilde{F_{i}}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0<p*<p}\hat{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\sum_{i\notin I}\Big(\frac{v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}}{\sum_{i\not\in I}v_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}^{p*}}-v_{i}\Big)\log\tilde{F_{i}}\Big|\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\hat{p}p\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{i\not\in I}|\log\tilde{F}_{i}|\Big)^{2}\Big]\lesssim\hat{p}p(\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4}(w,y))^{2/(2+\epsilon)}\,,

where the first
\lesssim
is by (
10.24
) and the second is by (
10.9
). This shows that
H_{p}\to H_{0}
uniformly on compacts, and therefore
H
is continuous. Additionally, it shows that
H
vanishes over
(p,v,w,y)\mapsto\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4}(w,y)
if and only if
H_{0}
vanishes over
\tilde{\Upsilon}^{1/4}
. The latter is a consequence of the fact that
H_{i}
and
\tilde{H_{i}}
vanish over
\Upsilon^{1/4}
(in fact, over
\Upsilon^{1/2}
), which is shown above (see (
10.11
) and (
10.12
)).

Thus, the assumptions of
Theorem

3.11
are satisfied and so we conclude that

(10.25)

\lim_{p\downarrow 0}\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}^{p}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\mu H_{p}\geq\inf_{\nu\in P_{inv}^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\nu H_{0}\,,

where
P_{inv}^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
is the set of all invariant measures for (
10.22
) (see
Lemma

9.6

(i)
). By
Lemma

2.22
, the right hand side of (
10.25
) is unchanged if we restrict
\nu\in P_{inv}^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
to be ergodic, in which case
\nu
is supported on
\{v=v_{0}\}
for some
v_{0}\in\bigtriangleup^{n-|I|-1}
(recall from (
10.22
) that
v_{0}(t+1)=v_{0}(t)
). In particular, there is
\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}^{I})
(equal to the pushforward of
\nu
by
\pi
) such that

\nu H_{0}=-\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\mu\tilde{H}_{i}+\hat{p}\sum_{i\notin I}(v_{0})_{i}\mu H_{i}\geq\inf_{\mu\in P_{inv}(\mathcal{M}^{I})}\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}r_{i}(\mu)-\hat{p}\max_{i\notin I}r_{i}(\mu)\,.

Thus, (
10.25
) implies (
10.21
), which finishes the proof.
∎

10.4.
Stochastic Kolmogorov Systems

In this subsection we mention how our results can be applied to Stochastic Kolmogorov Equations, that is, SDEs of the form

(10.26)

dX_{i}=X_{i}f_{i}(X)dt+X_{i}g_{i}(X)dE_{i}\,,

where
X_{t}\in[0,\infty)^{n}
,
f,g:[0,\infty)^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are locally Lipschitz functions, and
(E_{1}(t),\dots,E_{n}(t))^{T}=A^{T}W(t)
, where
A
is a
d
by
n
matrix and
W(t)
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion. We denote
A^{T}A
by
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
. Similarly to the previous example, we could have also included auxiliary variables
Y_{t}
, but we omit these for brevity. Our technical assumption is that there exists a proper
U:[0,\infty)^{n}\to[1,\infty)
in
\mathcal{C}^{2}([0,\infty)^{n})
and
K,c>0
such that
\mathcal{L}U\leq K-cU
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}(x)|+g_{i}(x)^{2}\lesssim 2K-\frac{\mathcal{L}U}{U}+\frac{\Gamma U}{U^{2}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}U(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}g_{i}(x)g_{j}(x)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}U(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}f_{i}(x)\partial_{i}U(x)

\displaystyle\Gamma U(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Sigma_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}g_{i}(x)g_{j}(x)\partial_{i}U(x)\partial_{j}U(x)\,.

Remark 10.16
.

If
[
HN18
, Assumption 1.1 (3)]
is satisfied then our technical assumption holds with
U=(1+c^{T}x)^{\theta}
for some small enough
1>\theta>0
. Similarly to
[
HNC21
, Assumption 3.1]
, if there exists a proper
U:[0,\infty)^{n}\to[1,\infty)
in
\mathcal{C}^{2}([0,\infty)^{n})
such that
\mathcal{L}U\leq K-cU\Big(1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}(x)|+g_{i}(x)^{2}\Big)
, then our technical assumption is satisfied.
We have no need of
[
HN18
, Assumption 1.1 (1)]
(nondegeneracy),
[
HN18
, Assumption 1.4]
, or
[
HNC21
, Assumption 3.2]
, although nondegeneracy is useful for deducing uniqueness of invariant measures on certain sets and for deducing accessibility.

With
H_{i}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ii}g_{i}(x)^{2}-f_{i}(x)
and
r_{i}(\mu)
defined as in (
10.13
), using
Lemma

9.3
in place of
Lemma

9.6

(vi)
one can show that the analogues of
Theorem

10.12
and
Theorem

10.14
hold for (
10.26
). We omit the proofs since they are exactly the same as those given in
Section

10.3
except that the details are easier (Itô’s formula gives a closed-form expression for
H_{p}
so we can directly compute
H_{p}-H_{0}
as opposed to using the mean value theorem twice to bound
|H_{p}-H_{0}|
) and also instead of defining
\tilde{V_{i}}
as
V_{i}\vee 0
one should choose some smooth function
v:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
with bounded first and second derivatives with
v(t)=t
for
t\geq 1
and instead set
\tilde{V_{i}}\coloneqq v\circ V_{i}
(as in the proof of
[
Ben23
, Theorem 5.1ii]
).

Finally, we remark that the results in
[
HNS22
]
(under the weaker assumptions described above) are corollaries of our theory. For example,
[
HNS22
, Theorem 6.2]
can be proved by considering
V(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=-p_{1}\ln{x_{1}}-p_{2}\ln{x_{2}}-p_{3}\ln{x_{3}}-\frac{p_{0}}{p}v(-\ln({x_{1}^{p}+x_{2}^{p}+x_{3}^{p}}))
for small enough
p>0
and suitable
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}>0
(see
[
HNS22
, Lemma 6.1]
).
